Filtrations on quantum cohomology via Morse–Bott–Floer Spectral Sequences
Alexander F. Ritter, Filip Živanović

Abstract
. Using Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequences, we describe a filtration by ideals on quantum cohomology for symplectic manifolds with a Hamiltonian S 1 S^{1} -action that extends to a pseudoholomorphic ℂ ∗ \mathbb{C}^{*} -action. These spaces include all Conical Symplectic Resolutions, in particular all Quiver Varieties. Our Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequences give explicit descriptions of birth-death phenomena of the barcode of the persistence module associated to the ℂ ∗ \mathbb{C}^{*} -action, defined in our earlier paper. This paper contains the foundational work to rigorously construct a filtration on Floer complexes, announced in that earlier paper. We also include a substantial appendix on Morse–Bott–Floer theory, where a large part of the technical difficulties of the paper are dealt with. We compute a plethora of explicit examples, each highlighting various features, for Springer resolutions, ADE resolutions, and several Slodowy varieties of type A. We also consider certain Higgs moduli spaces, for which we compare our filtration with the well-known P = W P=W filtration.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
The filtration on quantum cohomology of symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds

We introduced in
[
RŽ23
]
a large class of symplectic manifolds called symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds. This class includes many
equivariant resolutions of affine singularities, such as Conical Symplectic Resolutions (CSRs)
1
1

1

A weight-
s
CSR is a projective
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant resolution
\pi:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{M}_{0}
of a normal affine variety
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
, whose
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action contracts
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
to a point, and the holomorphic symplectic structure
(\mathfrak{M},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
satisfies
t\cdot\omega_{\mathbb{C}}=t^{s}\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
.
for example Nakajima quiver varieties and hypertoric varieties,
and crepant resolutions of quotient singularities
\mathbb{C}^{n}/G
for finite subgroups
G\subset SL(n,\mathbb{C})
(the spaces involved in the generalised McKay correspondence).
It also includes Moduli spaces of Higgs bundles, cotangent bundles of flag varieties,
negative complex vector bundles, and Kähler quotients of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
including semiprojective
toric manifolds.

These spaces are often hyperkähler, so they can be studied also in terms of exact symplectic forms
\omega_{J}
,
\omega_{K}
making them Liouville manifolds that often admit special exact
\omega_{J}
-Lagrangian submanifolds
[
Ž22
]
relevant to the A-side of Homological Mirror Symmetry.
Our goal however is to study directly the B-side: we consider the complex structure
I
with its typically non-exact Kähler form
\omega_{I}
. These spaces are rarely convex at infinity, so they have been out of reach of Floer-theoretic study so far; they are very rich in
I
-holomorphic curves, whereas in the hyperkähler case the
\omega_{J}
,
\omega_{K}
are exact and therefore prohibit non-constant closed
J
- or
K
-holomorphic curves.

We will develop the Morse–Bott–Floer
techniques needed to study that class of spaces. Such Morse–Bott methods were used in McLean–Ritter
[
MR23
]
to prove the cohomological McKay correspondence via Floer theory when
\mathbb{C}^{n}/G
was an isolated singularity. The assumption ensured the crepant resolution was convex at infinity. The tools developed in this paper, for example, bypass that condition.

A
symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold
(over a convex base) is a symplectic manifold
(Y,\omega)
, with a pseudoholomorphic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
\varphi
with respect to an
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
I
,
such that on the outside
Y^{\text{out}}
of a compact subset there is a pseudoholomorphic proper map

(1)

\Psi:Y^{\mathrm{out}}\to B=\Sigma\times[R_{0},\infty)

to the positive symplectisation of a closed contact manifold
\Sigma
. Moreover, the
S^{1}
-part of the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
\varphi

is Hamiltonian, and its vector field

X_{S^{1}}
maps to the Reeb field via
\Psi
,

(2)

\Psi_{*}X_{S^{1}}=\mathcal{R}_{B}.

There are no conditions on the dimension of
B
, and we emphasise that
\Psi
is typically not symplectic. Indeed
\omega
on
Y^{\mathrm{out}}
is usually non-exact, and
I
-holomorphic spheres often appear in the fibres of
\Psi
. We refer to the introduction of
[
RŽ23
]
for a discussion of many classes of examples.

By definition, such spaces therefore come with a moment map for the
1
-periodic
S^{1}
-action,

(3)

H:Y\to\mathbb{R},

yielding two commuting vector fields,
X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}=\nabla H
and
X_{S^{1}}=X_{H}=IX_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
.
The action is automatically contracting: the
-\nabla H
flow pushes
Y
arbitrarily close to a compact path-connected singular
2
2

2

In most cases.
core,

\mathrm{Core}(Y):=\{y\in Y:\lim_{t\to\infty}\varphi_{t}(y)\textrm{ exists}\}.

The core contains the fixed locus of the action, and we denote
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
its connected components,

(4)

\mathfrak{F}=\mathrm{Crit}(H)=Y^{S^{1}}=Y^{\mathbb{C}^{*}}=\sqcup_{\alpha}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}.

There is a unique minimal component
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}:=\min H
. For weight-1 CSRs,
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
is an
\omega_{J}
-Lagrangian submanifold
[
Ž22
]
.
3
3

3

Whilst the core is an
\omega_{J}
-Lagrangian subvariety, and it is singular when the CSR is not the cotangent bundle of a flag variety.
All
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
are
I
-holomorphic and
\omega
-symplectic submanifolds, and the linearised
S^{1}
-action on the complex vector space
T_{y}Y
, at
y\in\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
, yields a weight decomposition

(5)

\qquad\qquad\qquad T_{y}Y=\oplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{k},\qquad\textrm{ where }H_{0}=T_{y}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}.

The
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
are also Morse–Bott submanifolds of the Morse–Bott function

H
. Their Morse–Bott indices
\mu_{\alpha}\geq 0
are even and they determine the
cohomology of
Y
as a vector space (working over a field),
4
4

4

Here
A[d]
means we shift a graded group
A
down by
d
, so
(A[d])_{n}=A_{n+d}
, and
\check{H}^{*}
is Čech cohomology.

(6)

H^{*}(Y)\cong\oplus H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu_{\alpha}]\cong\check{H}^{*}(\mathrm{Core}(Y)).

The first isomorphism is originally due to Frankel
[
Fra59
]
for closed Kähler Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-manifolds. It can be proved by using Atiyah–Bott’s filtration of the space
Y
in terms of the stable manifolds of the
-\nabla H
flow
[
AB83
]
. We reviewed this in
[
RŽ23
]
, to motivate a Floer-theoretic generalisation of (
6
), namely we constructed a
\varphi
-dependent filtration by ideals of the quantum cohomology algebra.

Theorem 1.1
.

[
RŽ23
]

There is a filtration by graded ideals of
QH^{*}(Y)
, ordered by
p\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
,

(7)

\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{p}:=\bigcap_{\mathrm{generic}\,\lambda>p}\left(\ker c_{\lambda}^{*}:QH^{*}(Y)\to HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\right),\qquad\mathscr{F}_{\infty}^{\varphi}:=QH^{*}(Y),

where
c_{\lambda}^{*}
is a Floer continuation map, a grading-preserving
QH^{*}(Y)
-module homomorphism, and
H_{\lambda}:Y\to\mathbb{R}
is a Hamiltonian that at infinity equals
\lambda H+\textrm{constant}
.

The filtration
\mathscr{F}_{p}^{\varphi}(QH^{*}(Y))

is an invariant of
Y
up to isomorphism of symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds.
5
5

5

i.e. pseudoholomorphic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant symplectomorphisms, without conditions on the
\Psi
maps in (
1
).

The real parameter
p
is a part of that invariant, and has a geometric interpretation:
x\in\mathscr{F}_{p}^{\varphi}(QH^{*}(Y))
means that
x
can be represented as a Floer chain involving non-constant
S^{1}
-orbits of period
\leq p.

More generally, this idea is used in
[
RŽ23
]
to construct a persistence module
(V)_{p\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}}
, with
V_{0}=QH^{*}(Y)
, giving rise to a barcode associated to the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
\varphi
, satisfying a
1
-periodicity property.

In
Section

2.3
we mention the mild assumptions on
Y
needed for quantum and Floer cohomology to be well-defined, and we comment on the field of coefficients
\mathbb{K}
. The Novikov field
\mathbb{K}
is defined over a base field
\mathbb{B}
and involves Laurent “series” in a formal variable
T
. We define the “specialisation” map “
\mathrm{ini}
” to be the initial term of the series, meaning the coefficient of the smallest real power of
T
:

{\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{B}}}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}(H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{B}))={\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{B}}}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}:=\mathrm{ini}(\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi})\subset H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{B}).

This is a filtration by ideals
with respect to ordinary cup product, satisfying

(8)

\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{B}}{\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{B}}}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}=\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{K}}\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}.

Thus there is a
\mathbb{K}
-linear isomorphism
{\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{B}}}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}\otimes_{\mathbb{B}}\mathbb{K}\to\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}\subset\mathbb{K}\cdot({\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{B}}}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}+H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{K}_{>0})),

where
\mathbb{K}_{>0}\subset\mathbb{K}
denotes the ideal of elements involving only
T^{>0}
-terms.

The main goal of this paper is to develop spectral sequence methods to effectively describe the filtration
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
of
QH^{*}(Y)
, and
{\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{B}}}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
of
H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{B}).

This is a Floer-theoretic analogue of the
Morse–Bott spectral sequence for Morse cohomology which can be used to prove the left isomorphism in (
6
).

To give the reader a sense of the structure of the maps in (
7
), we recall the main tool used in
[
RŽ23
]
to study the
\varphi
-filtration:
suppose
H^{odd}(Y)=0
, or equivalently, each
H^{odd}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=0
, due to (
6
). Then for generic
\lambda>0
we have
HF^{odd}(\lambda H)=0
, moreover
the
\mathbb{K}
-linear continuation map becomes:

(9)

\textstyle c_{\lambda}^{*}:QH^{*}(Y)\cong\bigoplus_{\alpha}H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu_{\alpha}]\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\alpha}H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})]\cong HF^{*}(\lambda H),

where the even integers
\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})
are Floer-theoretic indices generalising the Morse–Bott indices
\mu_{\alpha}
.
For small
\lambda=\delta>0
,
\mu_{\alpha}=\mu_{\delta}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})
, and
c_{\lambda}^{*}:QH^{*}(Y)\to HF^{*}(\lambda H)
is an isomorphism, reproving the left isomorphism in (
6
). For larger slopes
\lambda
, analysing (
9
) we obtained some constraints, such as

(10)

\textstyle{\mathscr{F}_{\mathbb{B}}}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}\subset\bigoplus_{\alpha\neq\min}\ H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha};\mathbb{B})[-\mu_{\alpha}]\;\textrm{for }\;\;\lambda<\lambda_{\min}:=\min\{\tfrac{1}{|k|}:k\neq 0\textrm{ is a weight of }\mathfrak{F}_{\min}\textrm{ in \eqref{Intro weight spaces}}\}.

Beyond that, we really need the Morse–Bott–Floer methods from this paper, as we confirm in examples in
Section

7
.
We begin by providing the big picture, which relates quantum cohomology with Floer theory.
This involves symplectic cohomology
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
, whose chain level generators are loosely the
S^{1}
-orbits, and its positive version
SH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi)
, which ignores the constant
S^{1}
-orbits in
\mathfrak{F}
.
The direct limit as
\lambda\to\infty
in (
7
) defines a canonical algebra homomorphism
c^{*}:QH^{*}(Y)\to SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
, satisfying:

Theorem 1.2
.

[
RŽ23
]
6
6

6

The technical assumption, needed for Floer theory and to make sense of
QH^{*}(Y)
, is that
Y
satisfies a certain weak+ monotonicity property (
Section

2.3
). This includes for example all non-compact Calabi-Yau and non-compact Fano
Y
.

The homomorphism
c^{*}:QH^{*}(Y)\to SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
is surjective. It equals localisation at a
Gromov–Witten invariant

Q_{\varphi}\in QH^{2\mu}(Y),

where
\mu
is the Maslov index of
\varphi
.

SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)\cong QH^{*}(Y)/E_{0}(Q_{\varphi})\cong QH^{*}(Y)_{Q_{\varphi}}

where

E_{0}(Q_{\varphi})=\ker c^{*}\subset QH^{*}(Y)

is the generalised
0
-eigenspace of quantum product by
Q_{\varphi}
.
This yields a Floer-theoretic presentation of
QH^{*}(Y)
as a
\mathbb{K}
-module,

(11)

QH^{*}(Y)\cong SH^{*-1}_{+}(Y,\varphi)\oplus SH^{*}(Y,\varphi).

Moreover, for
N^{+}\in\mathbb{R}
just above
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the continuation maps
c^{*}_{N^{+}}
(whose direct limit is
c^{*}
),

(12)

c^{*}_{N^{+}}:QH^{*}(Y)\to HF^{*}(H_{N^{+}}),

can be identified with quantum product
N
times by
Q_{\varphi}
on
QH^{*}(Y)
. In particular,

SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)=0\Leftrightarrow(\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}=QH^{*}(Y)\textrm{ for some }\lambda<\infty)\Leftrightarrow(Q_{\varphi}\in QH^{2\mu}(Y)\textrm{ is nilpotent}),

which always occurs if
c_{1}(Y)=0
, including all CSRs, crepant resolutions of quotient singularities and Higgs moduli spaces.

The above vanishing result is in fact beneficial in determining the
\varphi
-filtration by Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequences, as it implies that a posteriori all edge differentials must kill ordinary cohomology.

For CSRs,
QH^{*}(Y)\cong H^{*}(Y)
is ordinary cohomology (with suitable coefficients)
so we obtain a
\varphi
-dependent filtration on
H^{*}(Y)
by ideals with respect to cup-product, and
H^{*}(Y)\cong SH^{*-1}_{+}(Y,\varphi)
.

1.2.
Morse–Bott manifolds of
1
-orbits

We recall from
[
RŽ23
]
that via the
1
-periodic
S^{1}
-flow, and letting
G_{\lambda}:=\langle e^{2\pi i\lambda}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{*}
,

(1\textrm{-orbits of }\lambda H)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1:1}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}(\lambda\textrm{-periodic orbits of the }S^{1}\textrm{-flow})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1:1}}{{\longleftrightarrow}}(G_{\lambda}\textrm{-fixed points in }Y).

We consider Hamiltonians
H_{\lambda}=c(H)
that are increasing functions of the moment map
H
of the
S^{1}
-action. Floer cohomology
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
at chain level is generated by the
1
-orbits of
H_{\lambda}
.

At infinity,
H_{\lambda}
has a
generic
slope
c^{\prime}(H)=\lambda>0
, meaning
\lambda
is not an
S^{1}
-period (e.g. irrational
\lambda
), so no
1
-orbits appear at infinity. We ensure that
1
-orbits of
c(H)
near
\mathrm{Core}(Y)
are precisely the constant orbits at points of
\mathfrak{F}
.
The interesting
1
-orbits are the non-constant ones: such
1
-orbits
x
arise with a positive rational slope
T
, which equals some
S^{1}
-period,

(13)

T=c^{\prime}(H(x))=\tfrac{k}{m}\quad(\textrm{for }k,m\textrm{ coprime}).

These
1
-orbits are trapped in the fixed locus
Y_{m}
of the cyclic subgroup
G_{T}\cong\mathbb{Z}/m
of
\mathbb{C}^{*}
. That locus
Y_{m}:=Y^{\mathbb{Z}/m}
is a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-invariant
I
-pseudoholomorphic
\omega
-symplectic submanifold, whose connected components are the
torsion
m
-submanifolds

Y_{m,\beta}
.
Locally near
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
,
Y_{m}
is
\exp|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}}(\oplus_{b\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{mb})
, using (
5
). Thus, there are at most
\#\{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\}
such components
Y_{m,\beta}
. Each
Y_{m,\beta}
contains a distinguished subcollection of the
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
, and has strata that converge to those
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
under the contracting
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action.
There are also only finitely many such integers
m
, as the weights in (
5
) depend only on
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
, not on
y
.

The
Y_{m,\beta}
are Morse–Bott manifolds of
1/m
-periodic orbits of
H
. “Morse–Bott” here refers to a non-degeneracy property analogous to the classical Morse–Bott property of functions, but referred to a certain functional defined on free loops. We are however more interested in the Morse–Bott manifolds of
1
-orbits of
H_{\lambda}
. The motivation is the same as in the case of the geodesic flow on tangent bundles, where one wants to consider longer and longer closed geodesics. In our setting, using
H_{\lambda}=c(H)
ensures that, as the slope
c^{\prime}(H)
grows, we are considering
S^{1}
-orbits of higher and higher periods.

Let us blur the distinction between a
1
-orbit
x(t)
and its initial point
x(0)
. Then
1
-orbits of
H_{\lambda}
of period
T=\tfrac{k}{m}
are slices of the torsion
m
-submanifolds, obtained by intersecting with a level set of
H
,

(14)

B_{T,\beta}:=Y_{m,\beta}\cap\{c^{\prime}(H)=T\}.

These are connected smooth odd-dimensional submanifolds
which we call
Morse–Bott manifolds
(of
1
-orbits) of
H_{\lambda}
.
Their diffeomorphism type only depends on
m
, not
k
.
The compact torsion manifolds
Y_{m,\beta}\subset\mathrm{Core}(Y)
do not contribute to (
14
) as the only
1
-orbits of
H_{\lambda}
near
\mathrm{Core}(Y)
are the constant
1
-orbits in
\mathfrak{F}.
Thus, only the
non-compact

Y_{m,\beta}
arise in (
14
), called
outer torsion manifolds
, and the
c^{\prime}(H)=\tfrac{k}{m}
in (
14
) are called
outer
S^{1}
-periods
. To simplify notation we label the outer
S^{1}
-periods in ascending order by
T_{p}\in\mathbb{Q}
, for
p=-1,-2,-3,\ldots
, and we often abbreviate
B_{p,\beta}:=B_{T_{p},\beta}
. These come with Floer-theoretic indices
\mu(B_{p,\beta})
which we compute explicitly in terms of weight spaces (
5
). We abusively use
p=0
,
T_{0}=0
, to label the constant orbits in
\mathfrak{F}
.

1.3.
The Lyapunov property of the
F
-filtration

Our idea, and motivation for the definition of symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds, is to use the map (
1
)
to project Floer solutions
u:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\to Y
for Hamiltonians of type
H_{\lambda}=c(H)
to a Floer-like solution in
B
(at least, the part on
Y^{\mathrm{out}}
).
By (
2
), the Floer equation
\partial_{s}u+I(\partial_{t}u-c^{\prime}(H)X_{S^{1}})=0
becomes

(15)

\partial_{s}v+I_{B}(\partial_{t}v-k(s,t)\mathcal{R}_{B})=0

once we have projected to
B
, where
k(s,t):=c^{\prime}(H(u(s,t))).
We will construct a functional

F:\mathcal{L}Y\to\mathbb{R}

on the free loop space of
Y
, satisfying the following
Lyapunov property
:
F
is non-increasing along the above Floer solutions
s\mapsto u(s,\cdot)
.
This yields a filtration on the Floer chain complex
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
.
We build
H_{\lambda}
so that this filtration is equivalent (up to reversal) to filtering by
S^{1}
-periods. In practice, this means that the Floer differential will be non-increasing on the period-value, in particular it must increase or preserve the
p
-labelling of the periods
T_{p}
. This phrasing has the advantage of showing that the filtration does not depend on the specific choices in the construction, so we call it
period-filtration
.

We remark that in
[
RŽ23
]
we provided a simplified construction of the
F
-filtration, sufficient to construct for example
SH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi)
, which at chain level quotients out the subcomplex of constant orbits. For the purpose of constructing spectral sequences that are compatible under increasing the slope
\lambda
, the more complicated construction from this paper is necessary.

In either case, however, there is a catch: to obtain smooth moduli spaces of Floer solutions, either a perturbation of
I
or a perturbation of
H_{\lambda}
is required. The first perturbation ruins pseudoholomorphicity,
\Psi_{*}\circ I=I_{B}\circ\Psi_{*}
, so ruins the “
I_{B}
” in (
15
); the second perturbation ruins
\Psi_{*}X_{H_{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}\mathcal{R}_{B}
, so ruins the “
k(s,t)\mathcal{R}_{B}
” in (
15
). Both those properties were crucial for the proof of the Lyapunov property.

Overcoming these transversality issues is carried out in
Section

4
. It involves a delicate Gromov compactness argument; energy bracketing the Novikov ring; and carrying out direct limits over continuation maps as the perturbed
I^{\prime}\to I
. In particular, the energy bracketing procedure was crucial to be able to apply Gromov-compactness in our setting where
\omega
is not exact, as there are no a priori energy estimates. The ideas here were inspired by the work of Ono
[
Ono95
]
on filtered Floer cohomology.

Theorem 1.3
.

For a suitable construction of
H_{\lambda}
, one can filter the Floer chain complex
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
by
S^{1}
-period values, so that the Floer differential is non-increasing on periods. One can construct Hamiltonians
H_{\lambda}
, for a sequence of values
\lambda\to\infty
, and continuation homomorphisms
\psi_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda}:CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\to CF^{*}(H_{\lambda^{\prime}})
for
\lambda\leq\lambda^{\prime}
, so that

(1)

\psi_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda}
is an inclusion of a subcomplex;

(2)

\psi_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda}
preserves the period-filtration.

Moreover, any two such choices of sequences of Hamiltonians can be related by period-filtration preserving continuation maps.

Corollary 1.4
.

Period-bracketed symplectic cohomology

SH^{*}_{(a,b]}(Y,\varphi):=\varinjlim HF^{*}_{(a,b]}(H_{\lambda})

is well-defined: at chain level one only considers
1
-orbits of
H_{\lambda}
of
S^{1}
-period
T\leq b
, and quotients out those of period
T\leq a
.
These groups admit a natural long exact sequence for
a<b<c
in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
.
For
SH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi):=SH^{*}_{(0,\infty]}(Y,\varphi)
, and
QH^{*}(Y)\cong SH^{*}_{(-\infty,\delta]}(Y,\varphi)
for small
\delta>0
, the LES becomes:

\cdots\to SH^{*-1}(Y,\varphi)\to SH^{*-1}_{+}(Y,\varphi)\to QH^{*}(Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c^{*}}}{{\to}}SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)\to\ldots

Remark 1.5
.

Although
1.4
will sound familiar to readers who have considered symplectic cohomology for Liouville manifolds
[
Vit96
]
, where such a result is immediate from the Floer action functional, we cannot overstate how fiendishly unfriendly Floer theory has been in complying with that result in the non-exact setup, as the action functional is not defined.

1.4.
Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequence

Once we have rigorously built the
F
-filtration on Floer chain complexes, by standard homological algebra arguments we obtain a spectral sequence that converges to
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
, and more generally to the period-bracketed
SH^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}(Y,\varphi)
for any period interval
\mathcal{P}\subset(-\infty,+\infty]
.

The heart of the matter now becomes identifying the
E_{1}
-page; discovering its properties such as symmetries and periodicities; and obtaining bounds for the degrees in which it is supported.

Our previously discussed period-labelling
T_{p}\in\mathbb{Q}
by
p=0,-1,-2,\ldots
corresponds to the
p
-index of the
E_{1}
-page
E_{1}^{p,q}
. So
E_{1}^{p,q}=0
for
p>0
, and
E_{1}^{0,*}\cong H^{*}(Y)
as the constant orbits recover the cohomology of the fixed locus with index shifts (this essentially underlies the Morse–Bott proof of (
6
)).

More interestingly, for
p<0
, the
E_{1}^{p,*}
column is computing the Floer cohomology for
S^{1}
-orbits that have the same period
T_{p}
, i.e. they lie in the same slice
c^{\prime}(H)=T_{p}
. In traditional Floer-theoretic settings, this is usually harmless, as everything reduces to Morse cohomology: a zero action functional difference implies Floer solutions have zero energy, so – as we are working in a Morse–Bott setting – the only solutions are those arising from the Morse cohomology of a chosen auxiliary Morse function

(16)

f_{p,\beta}:B_{p,\beta}\to\mathbb{R}.

In our case, unfortunately, it is a hornets’ nest: high-energy Floer solutions could arise, as there are no a priori energy bounds, and in fact the fibres of (
1
) are typically rich in
I
-holomorphic spheres.

Theorem 1.6
.

Local Floer cohomology of the slice
c^{\prime}(H)=T_{p}
is well-defined, and admits a convergent energy-spectral sequence starting with Morse cohomology of (
16
),

(17)

\oplus_{\beta}MH^{*}(B_{p,\beta};f_{p,\beta};\mathcal{L})[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]\Rightarrow\oplus_{\beta}H_{p,\beta}^{*}[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})],

where
\mathcal{L}
is a suitable Floer-theoretic local system supported near the Morse trajectories, and
\mu(B_{p,\beta})
are Floer-theoretic indies that are explicitly computable in terms of weight decomposition (
5
).

When
c_{1}(Y)=0
, the local system
\mathcal{L}
becomes trivial.

Remark 1.7
.

The study of local Floer cohomology, when the Morse–Bott manifolds are circles, goes back to Cieliebak–Floer–Hofer–Wysocki
[
CFHW96
]
.
Kwon – van Koert
[
KvK16
]
extended that construction to Liouville manifolds whose Morse–Bott manifolds satisfy a symplectic triviality condition. Unfortunately, for symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds their symplectic triviality condition rarely holds. This is the main reason for Appendices
B
-
D
, in which we work in full generality.

On first reading, it may be easier to ignore the difference between
H^{*}(B_{p,\beta})[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
and the above limit group
H_{p,\beta}^{*}[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
.
Indeed, although the latter needs to be placed in our
E_{1}
-page, in examples we always abusively place
H^{*}(B_{p,\beta})[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
in the columns instead. We describe circumstances where this is legitimate, but in general one must be cautious in our spectral sequence tables that some residual vertical differentials from the
E_{0}
-page still need to be accounted for on the
E_{1}
-page.
7
7

7

There are two local Floer cohomologies in play: the one occurring in a neighbourhood of
B_{p,\beta}
, and the one occurring in a neighbourhood of the slice
c^{\prime}(H)=T_{p}
. Low-energy Floer solutions will be local to a single
B_{p,\beta}
, but we cannot exclude high-energy Floer solutions, either local to a single
B_{p,\beta}
, or travelling between different
B_{p,\beta}
with equal
T_{p}
-values.

The rationale for this abuse of notation is that, a posteriori, one can often deduce that no such vertical differentials occur, because all ranks need to be accounted for if the spectral sequence
E_{*}^{p,q}
is to converge correctly. For example for
c_{1}(Y)=0
, we have
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)=0
, so all edge-differentials of
E_{1}
and later pages of the spectral sequence must eventually “cancel out” all cohomology.

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
Y
be a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold.
For any period interval
\mathcal{P}\subset(-\infty,+\infty]
, and working over a Novikov field
\mathbb{K}
,
there is a convergent spectral sequence of
\mathbb{K}
-modules,

E_{r}^{pq}(\varphi;\mathcal{P})\Rightarrow SH_{\mathcal{P}}^{*}(Y,\varphi),\textrm{ where }E_{1}^{pq}(\varphi;\mathcal{P})=\begin{cases}H^{q}(Y)&if $p=0${ and }$0\in\mathcal{P}$\\
\bigoplus_{\beta}H_{p,\beta}^{*}[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]&if $p<0,\textrm{ and }T_{p}\in\mathcal{P}$\\
0&otherwise.\end{cases}

Remark 1.9
.

The proof of the Theorem takes up
Section

5
, but also relies on a substantial series of Appendices
A
-
D
in which we construct
Morse–Bott Floer cohomology
.

In the context of ordinary Morse theory, this is analogous to the cascade model for a Morse–Bott function developed by Frauenfelder
[
Fra04
]
. When
Y
is a Liouville manifold and the Morse–Bott manifolds are circles and points, the Morse–Bott–Floer model was constructed by Bourgeois–Oancea
[
BO09b
]
.
Our Appendices will deal with the construction in full generality, and we will also cover folklore properties of Morse–Bott Floer cohomology that do not appear to be written up elsewhere.

Our Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequences also work when
c_{1}(Y)\neq 0
, but it becomes difficult to compute the local Floer cohomologies
H_{p,\beta}^{*}[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
of the Morse–Bott manifolds
B_{p,\beta}
. One must also be careful that when
c_{1}(Y)\neq 0
, Floer cohomology may not be
\mathbb{Z}
-graded. In the Fano case, it is still
\mathbb{Z}
-graded at the cost of suitably grading the formal Novikov variable that defines the Novikov field
\mathbb{K}
. In general, it is just
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded. In
1.25
we discuss such examples.

1.5.
Deducing the
\varphi
-filtration from Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequence tables

Although Floer invariants are notoriously difficult to compute, in
Section

6
we prove effective properties of the spectral sequence.
Section

7
includes a plethora of spectral sequence tables that illustrate various features that we discovered.
This introduction will just illustrate the simplest example, below.
Our convention in drawing the spectral sequence table
is to abusively draw the possible edge-differentials of later pages already on the
E_{1}
-page: some caution is needed as the arrows, all together, do not constitute a differential. Rather, they need to be interpreted as
iterated differentials
, as the
E_{r+1}
page is the cohomology of the
E_{r}
page using the arrows of a specific length, depending on
r
.

Proposition 1.10
.

A class
x
in the
\mathbb{K}
-module

H^{*}(Y)=QH^{*}(Y)
lies in
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
if the columns of the spectral sequence, given by
\oplus_{\beta}H_{p,\beta}^{*}[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
for
T_{p}\leq\lambda
, kill
x\in E_{1}^{0,*}=H^{*}(Y)
via iterated differentials.

Corollary 1.11

(Stability property)
.

\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}=\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
if there are no outer
S^{1}
-periods
T_{p}\in(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}].

We remark that we could not prove the stability property with the available tools in
[
RŽ23
]
, whereas it is now immediate from
1.10
: no new columns appear as we increase the slope from
\lambda^{+}
to
(\lambda^{\prime})^{+}
, so the
\varphi
-filtration has not jumped since no new classes in
H^{*}(Y)
were killed.

Example 1.12

(
A_{2}
-singularity)
.

Consider the minimal resolution

We embed

Remark 1.13
.

In
[
RŽ23
]
we computed the filtrations
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
directly from the continuation maps
c^{*}_{\lambda}
in (
7
). This approach requires knowing information about the fixed components
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
and their indices
\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})
, which are computed from the weights in (
5
).
The spectral sequence method, on the other hand, requires knowing information about the torsion manifolds
Y_{m,\beta}
which give rise to the slices
B_{p,\beta}
, and their indices
\mu(B_{p,\beta})
, which are also computed from the weights in (
5
).
Although we were able to
reprove with the continuation method many results that we had discovered using Morse–Bott–Floer techniques, the spectral sequence method has proven to be a much more practical tool to study the
\varphi
-filtration (e.g. compare the discussion of
1.12
with the analogous
[
RŽ23
, Ex.1.31,1.41]
).

We can rephrase
1.10
, by the final LES in
1.4
, as follows. The columns
E_{1}^{p,*}
for
p<0
constitute the spectral sequence in
1.8
for
\mathcal{P}=(0,\infty]
, i.e. for
SH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi)
.
Edge-differentials that hit the
0
-th column
E_{1}^{0,*}=H^{*}(Y)
arise from the unfiltered Floer differential going from non-constant orbits to constant orbits: these involve Floer solutions called “Floer spiked-discs”
[
PSS96
]
(the spike is the Morse half-trajectory of an auxiliary Morse function
f:\mathfrak{F}\to\mathbb{R}
on the locus
\mathfrak{F}=\sqcup\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
of constant orbits). Counting such solutions recovers
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
from period-bracketed
SH^{*}
:

Theorem 1.14
.

For any generic
\lambda>0
,

(18)

\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}=\ker\,\left(c_{\lambda}^{*}:QH^{*}(Y)\to SH^{*}_{(-\infty,\lambda]}(Y,\varphi)\right)\cong\mathrm{Image}\,\left(SH^{*-1}_{(0,\lambda]}(Y,\varphi)\to QH^{*}(Y)\right),

where the latter map is the connecting map in the long exact sequence from
1.4
, which coincides with applying the unfiltered Floer differential, so counting certain “Floer spiked-discs”.

1.6.
Properties of the
E_{1}
-page of the spectral sequence

First, we relate the indices
\mu(B_{p,\beta})
with the indices
\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})
from
[
RŽ23
]
. Let
|V|:=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V,
and let
\mu
be the Maslov index of the
S^{1}
-action.

Proposition 1.15
.

Let
T_{p}^{\pm}
be generic values just above and below
T_{p}
. Abbreviate
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}:=\min(H|_{Y_{m,\beta}})
and
\mathrm{rk}(Y_{m,\beta}):=|Y_{m,\beta}|-|\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}|
. Then
H^{*}(B_{p,\beta})[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
is supported precisely between the values

\displaystyle\mu(B_{p,\beta})

\displaystyle=\mu_{T_{p}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})-\mathrm{rk}(Y_{m,\beta})=\mu_{T_{p}^{+}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}),

\displaystyle\mu(B_{p,\beta})+\dim_{\mathbb{R}}B_{p,\beta}

\displaystyle=\mu_{T_{p}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})+|Y_{m,\beta}|+|\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}|-1=\mu_{T_{p}^{-}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})+2|\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}|-1.

We now provide some intuition for these gradings. The difference between the approach in
[
RŽ23
]
and here is that (
9
) is computed at generic times
\lambda
, whereas
B_{p,\beta}
arises at critical times
T_{p}
in (
13
).
Assume
H^{*}(Y)
lies in even degrees.
Columns below time
T_{p}
are a spectral sequence that converges to

HF^{*}(T_{p}^{-}H)\cong\oplus H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu_{T_{p}^{-}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})].

Whereas if we include the time-
T_{p}
column, it converges to
HF^{*}(T_{p}^{+}H)\cong\oplus H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu_{T_{p}^{+}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})]
. Thus,
1.15
suggests that top classes of
H^{*}(B_{p,\beta})[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
are killing (via edge differentials, of total degree
1
) the
H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu_{T_{p}^{-}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})]
summand; the bottom classes are creating the
H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu_{T_{p}^{+}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})]
summand. To see this play out in a simple example, compare
1.12
with
[
RŽ23
, Ex.1.31,1.41]
.
The intuition above is in fact a consequence of the following more general observation.

Proposition 1.16
.

Replacing
H_{\lambda}=c(H)
by
H_{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}:=c(H)+\lambda_{0}H
, for any
\lambda_{0}>0
, yields a spectral sequence converging to
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda+\lambda_{0}})
whose
E_{1}
-page has
0
-th column
HF^{*}(\lambda_{0}H)
and
p
-th columns
\bigoplus_{\beta}H_{p,\beta}^{*}[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
having
T_{p}\in\mathcal{P}:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda+\lambda_{0})
.
9
9

9

Another viewpoint: we have a long exact sequence
\cdots\to HF^{*}(\lambda_{0}H)\to HF^{*}((\lambda+\lambda_{0})H)\to HF^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}(H_{\lambda,\lambda_{0}})\to\cdots
, and if we only consider the columns with
T_{p}\in\mathcal{P}:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda+\lambda_{0})
then the spectral sequence converges to
HF^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}(H_{\lambda,\lambda_{0}})
.

So, given
p
, taking
\lambda_{0}:=T_{p}^{-}
,
\lambda+\lambda_{0}:=T_{p}^{+}
, the
E_{1}
-page has two columns,
HF^{*}(T_{p}^{-}H)
and
\bigoplus_{\beta}H_{p,\beta}^{*}[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
, and converges to
HF^{*}(T_{p}^{+}H)
.

Using
1.15
, we deduce two symmetry properties for the spectral sequence:

Corollary 1.17
.

There is a translation symmetry:
T_{p}\to T_{p}+1
shifts rows down by
2\mu
:

\mu(B_{p,\beta})=\mu(B_{p^{\prime},\beta})-2N\mu\qquad\textrm{ if }\;T_{p}=T_{p^{\prime}}+N,\textrm{ any }N\in\mathbb{N}.

When
\text{char}\ \mathbb{K}=0
, for
T_{p}\in(0,1)
there is a reflection symmetry about
(\frac{1}{2},|Y|-\frac{1}{2}-\mu)
(see the “star-point” in
1.12
). Explicitly, for
T_{p^{\prime}}:=1-T_{p}
,

\biggl(H^{*}(B_{p^{\prime},\beta})[-\mu_{T_{p^{\prime}}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})]\biggr)_{d}\cong\biggl(H^{*}(B_{p,\beta})[-\mu_{T_{p}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})]\biggr)_{2|Y|-1-2\mu-d.}

This Corollary implies that the
E_{1}
-page of the spectral sequence is known once we find the columns for
0<T_{p}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
and for
T_{p}=1
.
Combining
1.17
and
1.15
, we obtain bounds
for the support
of the
E_{1}
-page
(
6.8
). This implies
by which page
the filtration
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
is determined:

Corollary 1.18
.

If
\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})\leq\mu_{\alpha}
for all
\lambda
(e.g. any CSR), then
H^{*}(Y)
can only be hit by columns with

T_{p}\leq 1+\tfrac{t}{2\mu}\;\ \textrm{ if }\ T_{p}\notin\mathbb{N},\textrm{ and }T_{p}\leq\tfrac{|Y|}{\mu}\ \textrm{ if }\ T_{p}\in\mathbb{N},\quad\textrm{where }t:=\max\{m\in\mathbb{N}:H^{m}(Y)\neq 0\}\leq 2|Y|-2.

The
T_{p}=1
column (and all integer-time columns, i.e. slices
\Sigma_{N}:=\{c^{\prime}(H)=N\}
) is often understood:

Proposition 1.19
.

Let
\Sigma
denote the slice
\{c^{\prime}(H)=1\}
.
For
\text{char}\ \mathbb{K}=0
, suppose
H^{*}(Y)
is supported in degrees
[0,n=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y]
(e.g. all CSRs and Higgs Moduli spaces). Then

H^{k}(\Sigma)\cong\begin{cases}H^{2n-1-k}(Y)&\ $k\geq n+1,$\\
H^{n-1}(Y)\oplus\ker\psi&\ $k=n,\ n-1,$\\
H^{k}(Y)&\ $k\leq n-2$,\end{cases}

where
\psi:H_{n}(Y)\rightarrow H_{n}^{lf}(Y)
is the natural map into locally finite homology.
If the intersection form
H_{n}(Y)\times H_{n}(Y)\rightarrow\mathbb{K}
vanishes,
\ker\psi\cong H^{n}(Y)
; if it is non-degenerate,
\ker\psi=0.

For weight-
s
CSRs:
H^{k}(\Sigma)=0
in mid-degrees
k=n,n-1
; it lies in even degrees for
k\leq n-2
and odd degrees for
k\geq n+1
; thus (
17
) collapses for
T_{p}=N\in\mathbb{N}
giving
H_{p}^{*}[-\mu(B_{p})]\cong H^{*}(\Sigma)[snN]
.

When
H^{*}(Y
) lies in even degrees (e.g. all CSRs)
the spectral sequence simplifies considerably:

Proposition 1.20
.

Suppose
H^{*}(Y
) lies in even degrees.
From the
E_{1}
-page onward, the spectral sequence splits horizontally into a direct sum of two-row spectral sequences.

Combining this with the fact that the right-hand side of (
9
) is in even degrees yields:

Corollary 1.21
.

Suppose
H^{*}(Y
) lies in even degrees.
For any given odd degree class
x
of the

-page, some edge-differential is eventually injective on
x
, so kills a class in a column strictly to the left of
x
.

Using

. Suppose

1.7.
Illustration of the results in some examples

Example 1.23

(CSRs)
.

For CSRs, we have
c_{1}(Y)=0.
12
12

12

Since they are holomorphic symplectic.

Moreover,
QH^{*}(Y)=H^{*}(Y)
as rings,
13
13

13

Since by
[
Nam08
]
,
(Y,I)
can be deformed to an affine algebraic variety.

which is supported in even degrees in
[0,n]
,
n:=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y
. The Maslov index satisfies
2\mu=sn
, where
s
is the weight of the CSR (in most examples
s=1
or
2
).
By computing the gradings of the Morse–Bott manifolds, for weight-1 CSRs we show:

\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda<1}\subsetneq H^{\geq 2}(Y),\qquad\quad\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{1}=H^{\geq 2}(Y),\qquad\quad\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{2}=H^{*}(Y),

and

\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{1^{-}}\cap H^{n}(Y)
has codimension one in
H^{n}(Y)
: in the spectral sequence it is the top class of
H^{*}(B_{1})[n]
which kills the last
H^{n}(Y)
class
(see

).

Example 1.24

(
T^{*}\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{m}
and Springer resolutions)
.

Let
Y=T^{*}\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{m}
, viewed as a CSR. We discuss several
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-actions on this. Consider first the standard
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on fibres. The Morse–Bott manifolds
B_{N}
of
1
-orbits arising for full rotations by

are isomorphic to the sphere bundle
ST^{*}\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{m}
, with cohomology
shifted down by
2N\mu=2Nm
.
Figure

3
in
Section

7.1
shows the simple case of
T^{*}\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.
A Gysin sequence argument shows that
H^{*}(ST^{*}\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{m})
is supported in even degrees
0,2,\ldots,2m-2
, and in odd degrees
2m+1,\ldots,4m-1
, and it is free of rank 1 in those degrees. In the spectral sequence, the odd classes of
B_{1}
will kill the classes of
H^{*}(Y)\cong H^{*}(\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{m})=\mathbb{K}[x]/\langle x^{m+1}\rangle
in degrees
2,4,\ldots,2m
. The top odd class of
B_{2}
kills
H^{0}(Y)
. So, the filtration is

0\quad\subset\quad\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{1}=H^{*\geq 2}(Y)=\langle x\rangle\quad\subset\quad\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{2}=H^{*}(Y).

The same filtration holds for Springer resolutions, i.e. cotangent bundles of flag varieties (
Section

7.1
).

With twisted actions, i.e. allowing
\mathbb{C}^{*}
to act non-trivially on the zero section, the filtration can become quite refined. For example, making
\mathbb{C}^{*}
act on
\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{m}
by
[z_{0},tz_{1},\ldots,t^{m-1}z_{m-1}]
, we get the filtration by cohomological grading (see
7.6
and
Figure

4
).

Example 1.25

(Negative vector bundles)
.

For a general negative vector bundle

Y=\mathrm{Tot}(E\to\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{m})

of rank
r=\mathrm{rk}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
,
with the natural weight-1
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on fibres, we have
(2m+2r-1)
-dimensional sphere subbundles
B_{N}=SE
whose cohomology gets shifted down by
2N\mu=2Nr
. A Gysin argument shows

H^{*}(B_{N})
has
even classes in degrees
0,2,\ldots,2r-2

and
odd classes in degrees
2m+1,\ldots,2m+2r-1
.
Although
c_{1}(E)\neq 0
, it can happen that
c_{1}(Y)=0

(e.g. the line bundle
\mathcal{O}(-1-m)\to\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{m}
).

Let us suppose
c_{1}(Y)=0
.
In the spectral sequence, the odd classes of
B_{1}
kill the classes of
H^{*}(E)\cong H^{*}(\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{m})
in degrees
2m+2-2r,\ldots,2m
, the odd classes of
B_{2}
kill the next
2r
-block of classes in degrees
2m+2-4r,\ldots,2m-2r
, etc. The unit will be killed by
B_{N}
for
N=1+\lfloor\tfrac{m}{r}\rfloor
. This is consistent with
1.2
: by
[
Rit14
]
,
Q_{\varphi}
is a non-zero multiple of the pull-back of the Euler class of
E
, in degree
2\mu=2r
, so its
N
-th quantum-product power is zero for degree reasons.

When
c_{1}(Y)\neq 0
, Floer theory is not
\mathbb{Z}
-graded, and
[
Rit14
,
Rit16
]
illustrates many situations where
SH^{*}(Y)\neq 0
in the regime where
Y
is monotone. Let us consider the line bundle

Y=\text{Tot}(\mathcal{O}(-k)\to\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{m})

in the monotone regime
1\leq k\leq m
, with the standard
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
\varphi
on the fibres.
We have
c_{1}(Y)=(1+m-k)x\neq 0
, where
x=\pi^{*}\omega_{\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{m}}
. For a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading we place the Novikov parameter
T
in grading
|T|=2(1+m-k)
. We work over the Novikov field

\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{Q}(\!(T)\!)=\{
formal Laurent series in
T\}
. Abbreviate
y:=x^{1+m-k}-(-k)^{k}T
.
By
[
Rit16
]
, in
1.2
we have

QH^{*}(Y)=\mathbb{K}[x]/(x^{k}y),\quad Q_{\varphi}=-kx,\quad E_{0}(Q_{\varphi})=\langle y\rangle\subset QH^{*}(Y),\quad\textrm{ and }\quad SH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{K}[x]/(y).

The
\varphi
-filtration by ideals on
QH^{*}(Y)
is:

0\subset\langle x^{k-1}y\rangle\subset\langle x^{k-2}y\rangle\subset\cdots\subset\langle xy\rangle\subset E_{0}(Q_{\varphi})=\langle y\rangle\subset QH^{*}(Y).

When considering the Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequence, there are three ways to proceed: one can either use a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading with
|T|=0
by only remembering the parity; or one refines this to a
\mathbb{Z}/2(1+m-k)
-grading with
|T|=0

[
Rit14
, Sec.1.8]
; or one uses a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading at the cost of grading the Novikov ring:
|T|=2(1+m-k)
.
The odd classes of the
H^{*}(B_{N})[2N]
must continue to kill even classes as before, by
1.21
, but also new phenomena occur.
For
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{m}}(-1)
, the degree
-1
class
y_{1+m}
generating
H^{*}(B_{1+m})[2(1+m)]
, which in the CY-case
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{n}}(-n-1)
would have hit the unit in
H^{0}(Y)
, must no longer do so because
SH^{*}(Y)\neq 0
.

Consider
Y=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}P^{1}}(-1)
.
Let
x_{m},y_{m}
be a choice of generators of
H^{0}(B_{m})[2m],H^{3}(B_{m})[2m]
in grading

|x_{m}|=-2m
,
|y_{m}|=-2m+3
,
in column
m
of the
E_{1}
page, noting
B_{m}\cong S^{3}
for
m=1,2,\ldots
. In column
0
we have the generators
x_{0},y_{0}\in H^{*}(\mathbb{C}P^{1})
in gradings
0,2
.
Note
\omega+T\cdot 1\in\ker c_{1^{+}}
, as one full rotation corresponds to quantum product by
\mathcal{Q}=-[\omega]
on
QH^{*}(Y)\cong\mathbb{K}[\omega]/(\omega^{2}+T\omega)
.
It follows that on the
E_{1}
-page,
y_{1}
must hit (a non-zero multiple of)
\omega+T\cdot 1
; note
\omega,T\cdot 1
lie in the same grading even though
\omega,1\in H^{*}(Y)
do not.
So, if we consider only up to column 1, then
x_{0},x_{1}
represent
E_{2}
-page generators (they survive to
HF^{*}(H_{1^{+}})\cong QH^{*}(Y)[2]
). Thus
y_{2}
in column 2 must not survive to
E_{2}
, otherwise it would have to kill the unit
x_{0}
. So
y_{2}
must kill
Tx_{1}
on the
E_{1}
-page.
Similarly,
y_{m}
must kill
Tx_{m}
on the
E_{1}
-page. So only
x_{0}
survives, as expected:
SH^{*}(Y)=\mathbb{K}\cdot[x_{0}]
.

Another illustration of a
c_{1}(Y)\neq 0
example is when the vector bundle is “very negative”
[
Rit13
, Sec.1.10-1.11]
. In that case, Floer theory is
\mathbb{Z}/2M
-graded for a very large integer
M
. The ambiguity of the grading caused by being allowed to translate degrees by
2M
has little to no effect on our ability to determine what happens in the spectral sequence below the
N
-th column, where
N=1+\lfloor\tfrac{m}{r}\rfloor
. So the degree
-1
part of
H^{*}(B_{N})[2rN]
must kill
H^{0}(Y)
, which confirms that
SH^{*}(Y)=0

[
Rit14
]
.

Example 1.26
.

(Higgs moduli and
P=W
)
A famous class of examples are
moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}
of Higgs bundles
over a Riemannian surface
\Sigma_{g}
.
Roughly speaking, their elements are
(conjugacy classes) of stable pairs
(V,\Phi),
where
V
is a vector bundle over
\Sigma_{g}
of fixed coprime rank and degree,
and
\Phi\in\text{Hom}(V,V\otimes K_{\Sigma_{g}})

is the so-called Higgs field,
14
14

14

here,
K_{\Sigma}
is the canonical bundle of
\Sigma_{g}
.

which can potentially have some poles.
The space
\mathcal{M}
is a
complete
hyperkähler manifold,
with a natural
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action given by
t\cdot(V,\Phi)=(V,t\Phi).

It also admits the so-called Hitchin fibration, given as the characteristic polynomial of the Higgs field

(21)

\qquad\qquad\Psi:\mathcal{M}\to B\cong\mathbb{C}^{N}\quad\textrm{ where }N:=\tfrac{1}{2}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{M}.

Interestingly, the torsion manifolds of these spaces, involved in our spectral sequences, consist of so-called
cyclic Higgs bundles
, considered by several authors
[
Bar09
,
Bar15
,
DL20
,
RS21
]
.

Higgs moduli come with the “
P=W
” filtration,
[
dCHM12
,
HMMS22
,
MS22
]
,
which is the Perverse filtration of
\Psi,
and it
has a different structure to ours as it filters from the unit towards the higher classes, whereas ours goes the other way around.
However, it still makes sense to compare these two filtrations on each
H^{k}(\mathcal{M}).

In the simplest case of ordinary Higgs bundles of rank 1, where
\mathcal{M}\cong T^{*}T^{2g},
the filtrations degree-wise agree, both having trivial filtrations
0\subset\mathscr{F}_{1}(H^{k}(\mathcal{M}))=H^{k}(T^{2g}).

More interestingly, consider
parabolic
Higgs bundles of
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}=2,
given as crepant resolutions

(22)

\pi:\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow(T^{*}E)/\Gamma,

where
E
is an elliptic curve, and

\Gamma\in\{{0},\ \mathbb{Z}/2,\ \mathbb{Z}/3,\ \mathbb{Z}/4,\ \mathbb{Z}/6\},

is a finite group of automorphisms of
E
. Their cohomology is supported only in degrees 0 (where both filtrations are trivial) and 2.

Proposition 1.27
.

The filtration
\mathscr{F}(H^{2}(\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}))
refines the
P=W
filtration.

In addition, our filtration has a representation-theoretic meaning for these examples.
Namely, the intersection graph
Q_{\Gamma}
of the components of
\mathrm{Core}(\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma})

is known to be an affine Dynkin graph, thus has the imaginary root
\delta=\sum_{i\in{Q_{\Gamma}^{0}}}n_{i}\alpha_{i},

where
\alpha_{i}
are the simple roots. The following holds:

Proposition 1.28
.

Denoting by
\mathscr{F}_{k}
the
k
-th level of the filtration
\mathscr{F}(H^{2}(\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma})),
we have

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathscr{F}_{k})=\#\{i\in Q_{\Gamma}^{0}\mid n_{i}\leq k\}.
∎

1.8.
Morse–Bott spectral sequence for
S^{1}
-equivariant symplectic cohomology

In
[
RŽ23
,
RŽ24
]
we discuss the construction of
S^{1}
-equivariant symplectic cohomology
ESH^{*}(Y,\varphi).

Briefly, using conventions from
[
MR23
]
,
S^{1}
-equivariant symplectic cohomology
ESH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
is a
\mathbb{K}[u]
-module, with a canonical
\mathbb{K}[u]
-module homomorphism

Ec^{*}:EH^{*}(Y)\cong H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}\to ESH^{*}(Y,\varphi).

Here
u
is a degree two formal variable, and at chain level, each
1
-orbit contributes a copy of the
\mathbb{K}[u]
-module
\mathbb{F}:=\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)/u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cong H_{-*}(\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{\infty})
where we identify
u^{-j}=[\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}]
, so that the action of
H^{*}(\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{\infty})=\mathbb{K}[u]
corresponds to the nilpotent cap product.
Above, we used the notation from
[
MR23
]
,

EH^{*}(Y)\cong H_{2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-*}^{lf,S^{1}}(Y),

to denote locally finite
S^{1}
-equivariant homology.
That
EH^{*}(Y)\cong H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}
is because
S^{1}
is only acting by
S^{1}
-reparametrisation on
1
-orbits, and
EH^{*}(Y)
arises from constant
1
-orbits.

When
c_{1}(Y)=0
, we have
ESH^{*}(Y,\varphi)=0
for the same grading reasons that prove
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)=0
. Thus
SH^{*-1}_{+}(Y,\varphi)
is a free
\mathbb{F}
-module isomorphic to
H^{*}(Y)\otimes\mathbb{F}
as a
\mathbb{K}[u]
-module.

Equivariant and non-equivariant symplectic cohomology are related by a Gysin sequence

\cdots\to SH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{in}}}{{\to}}ESH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u\cdot}}{{\to}}ESH_{+}^{*+2}(Y,\varphi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\to}}SH_{+}^{*+1}(Y,\varphi)\to\cdots

where “
\mathrm{in}
” is induced at chain level by the inclusion as the
u^{0}
-part. For example, when
ESH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi)
lives in odd grading, this implies

SH^{*}_{+}(Y)\cong\ker(u:ESH^{*}_{+}(Y)\to ESH^{*}_{+}(Y)).

Theorem 1.29
.

[
RŽ23
]

There is an
\mathbb{R}_{\infty}
-ordered filtration by graded ideals of the
\mathbb{K}[u]
-module
EH^{*}(Y)
,

(23)

E\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{p}:=\bigcap_{\mathrm{generic}\,\lambda>p}\left(\ker Ec_{\lambda}^{*}:H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}\to EHF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\right),\qquad E\mathscr{F}_{\infty}^{\varphi}:=H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F},

where
Ec_{\lambda}^{*}
is an equivariant continuation map, a grading-preserving
\mathbb{K}[u]
-linear map.

In general,
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}\subset E\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
. If
H^{*}(Y)
lies in even degrees (e.g. CSRs), then
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}=QH^{*}(Y)\cap E\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}.

The analogue of
Equation

18
becomes

E\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}=\ker\,\left(Ec_{\lambda}^{*}:H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}\to ESH^{*}_{(-\infty,\lambda]}(Y,\varphi)\right)\cong\mathrm{Image}\,\left(ESH^{*-1}_{(0,\lambda]}(Y,\varphi)\to H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}\right).

Theorem 1.30
.

Let
Y
be a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold.
For any period interval
\mathcal{P}\subset(-\infty,+\infty]
, and working over a Novikov field
\mathbb{K}
,
there is a convergent spectral sequence of
\mathbb{K}
-modules,

E_{r}^{pq}(\varphi;\mathcal{P})\Rightarrow ESH_{\mathcal{P}}^{*}(Y,\varphi),\textrm{ where }E_{1}^{pq}(\varphi;\mathcal{P})=\begin{cases}H^{q}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}&if $p=0${ and }$0\in\mathcal{P}$\\
\bigoplus_{\beta}EH_{p,\beta}^{*}[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]&if $p<0,\textrm{ and }T_{p}\in\mathcal{P}$\\
0&otherwise,\end{cases}

where the
\mathbb{K}
-module
EH_{p,\beta}^{*}
arises as the limit of an energy-spectral sequence that starts with
S^{1}
-equivariant Morse cohomology
15
15

15

in the sense of
[
MR23
, Sec.4]

\oplus_{\beta}EMH^{*}(B_{p,\beta};f_{p,\beta};\mathcal{L})
,
for the slice
c^{\prime}(H)=T_{p}
with a local system
\mathcal{L}
as in
1.6
.
When
c_{1}(Y)=0
,
\mathcal{L}
can be ignored.

If
H^{*}\left(B_{p,\beta}/S^{1}\right)
lies in even degrees, then the energy-spectral sequence immediately collapses, and
16
16

16

The final isomorphism is
[
MR23
, Thm.4.3]
.

(24)

EH_{p,\beta}^{*}[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]=EH^{*}(B_{p,\beta})[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]\cong H^{*}\left(B_{p,\beta}/S^{1}\right)[-1-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]

lies in odd degrees.
Although the spectral sequence generally forgets the
u
-action, within that column the
u
-action is cap product by the negative Euler class associated with the map

B_{p,\beta}\to B_{p,\beta}/S^{1}
.

This
S^{1}
-equivariant version of the Morse–Bott spectral sequence was particularly helpful in
[
MR23
]
for crepant resolutions of isolated quotient singularities, because the columns
EH^{*}(B_{p,\beta})[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]

were in odd grading.
The same phenomenon occurs for many examples of symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds.

Corollary 1.31
.

If
H^{*}\left(B_{p,\beta}/S^{1}\right)
lies in even degrees for all
p,\beta
, then (
24
) lies in odd degrees and determines the spectral sequence for
ESH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi),
which has collapsed on the
E_{1}
-page:

ESH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi)=E_{1}^{**}(\varphi;(0,\infty]).

In the
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)=0
case (e.g. when
c_{1}(Y)=0
) this also forces
H^{*}(Y)
to lie in even grading, so we can read off
H^{*}(Y)\otimes\mathbb{F}
from that
E_{1}
-page, since
H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}\cong ESH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi)[-1]
.

In
[
MR23
]
this Corollary was the key to proving the cohomological McKay correspondence.
Below we will illustrate a large class of examples where that argument applies, but first a simple example:

Example 1.32
.

Continuing
1.12
,
write
p_{i}
abusively to denote
H^{2}(S^{2}_{i})
; let
\mathbb{K}_{m}(\lambda)
be a copy of
\mathbb{K}
in grading
m
arising from the column at time/slope
\lambda
; and identify the
\mathbb{K}
-modules
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{K}[u^{-1}]=\mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{-2}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{-4}\oplus\cdots
.
For both actions:
B_{\textrm{non-integer}}/S^{1}=\mathrm{(point)}
,
B_{\textrm{integer}}/S^{1}\cong\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. E.g.
H^{*}(B_{1})[4]
, in the first table of
1.12
, in the equivariant case gives
H^{*}(B_{1}/S^{1})[-1+4]=H^{*}(\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{1})[3]=\mathbb{K}_{-3}(1)\oplus\mathbb{K}_{-1}(1)
, and after the very last
[-1]
shift in
1.31
contributes
\mathbb{K}_{-2}(1)\oplus\mathbb{K}_{0}(1)
.
Doing the same for all columns,
we deduce the following for the two actions:

(a)

\displaystyle p[u^{-1}]\oplus p_{1}[u^{-1}]\oplus p_{2}[u^{-1}]

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{K}_{2}(\tfrac{1}{3})^{\oplus 2}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{0}(\tfrac{2}{3})^{\oplus 2}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{0}(1)\oplus\mathbb{K}_{-2}(1)\oplus\mathbb{K}_{-2}(\tfrac{4}{3})^{\oplus 2}\oplus\cdots

(b)

\displaystyle H^{*}(S^{2}_{1})[u^{-1}]\oplus p_{2}[u^{-1}]

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{K}_{2}(\tfrac{1}{2})\oplus\mathbb{K}_{2}(1)\oplus\mathbb{K}_{0}(1)\oplus\mathbb{K}_{0}(\tfrac{3}{2})\oplus\mathbb{K}_{0}(2)\oplus\mathbb{K}_{-2}(2)\oplus\cdots

From this, for (a) we deduce
\mathscr{F}_{1/3}^{\varphi}=p_{1}\oplus p_{2}
,
\mathscr{F}_{1}^{\varphi}=H^{*}(M)
; and in (b),

\mathrm{rk}(\mathscr{F}_{1/2}^{\varphi}\cap H^{2}(Y))=1
,
\mathscr{F}_{1}^{\varphi}=H^{2}(M)
,
\mathscr{F}_{2}^{\varphi}=H^{*}(M).

For (b) we improved
\mathscr{F}_{1}^{\varphi}\subset H^{2}(M)
to an equality as
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{1}\neq H^{*}(M)
:
u
acts non-trivially
\mathbb{K}_{0}(1)\to\mathbb{K}_{2}(1)
by

, whereas
u
acts trivially on
1\cdot u^{0}\in H^{0}(S^{2}_{1})
.
Note in (a) we cannot determine
\mathscr{F}_{2/3}^{\varphi}
: we would need more information about the
u
-action (the edge differentials are
u
-equivariant, but the
u
-action on the
E_{1}
-page is unclear).

We make the following simplifying assumption, which holds for all examples in
Section

7
. Assume that the outer torsion submanifolds are complex vector bundles over their cores,

(25)

Y_{m,\beta}=:E_{m}\rightarrow\mathrm{Core}(Y_{m,\beta}),\;\textrm{ and let }r:=\mathrm{rk}(Y_{m,\beta}):=\mathrm{rk}_{\mathbb{C}}(E_{m}).

Let
B_{p,\beta}
denote the slice arising with slope
T_{p}=\tfrac{k}{m}
for coprime
k,m
. We
get a complex projectivisation

B_{p,\beta}/S^{1}\cong(E_{m}\setminus(\textrm{zero section}))/\mathbb{C}^{*}={\mathbb{P}}(E_{m}).

Omitting the even index shift
[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
for readability,

(26)

EH^{*}(B_{p,\beta})\!\cong\!H^{*}(B_{p,\beta}/S^{1})[-1]\!=\!H^{*}({\mathbb{P}}(E_{m}))[-1]\!\cong\!H^{*}(\mathrm{Core}(Y_{m,\beta}))\langle 1,c_{1}(L),\ldots,c_{1}(L)^{r-1}\rangle[-1]

where the first isomorphism is
[

, and the last is the Leray--Hirsch theorem that yields
17
17

17

B_{p,\beta}/S^{1}
is an orbifold with cyclic stabilisers, but over a field of characteristic
0
this is not an issue
[
MR23
, Sec.4.7]
.

the
free
module over the cohomology of the base

of
E_{m}
, generated by powers of the first Chern class
of the tautological line bundle
L:E_{m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}(E_{m})
.
The cohomology of
\mathrm{Core}(Y_{m,\beta})

6
),
under mild assumptions
18
18

18

e.g. this holds by

that ensure the first isomorphism below,

H^{*}(\mathrm{Core}(Y_{m,\beta}))\cong H^{*}(Y_{m,\beta})\cong\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\subset Y_{m,\beta}}H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha};Y_{m,\beta})],

Assume

An explicit example of this, for the Slodowy variety

Summarising:

Corollary 1.33
.

Suppose that
c_{1}(Y)=0
;
H^{*}(Y)
lies in even degrees; the outer torsion manifolds are complex vector bundles over their cores, and those cores have the homotopy type of CW complexes. Then
we can identify the
\mathbb{K}
-module
H^{*}(Y)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}
with:
19
19

19

where the first isomorphism uses (
6
), and we use that at integer times the hypersurface

is diffeomorphic to
\Sigma:=B_{1}
; and we know
\mu(B_{N})=-2N\mu,
where
\mu
is the Maslov index of the
S^{1}
-action.

(27)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}H^{*}(Y)[2i]\cong\bigoplus_{\alpha}\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-\mu_{\alpha}+2i]\cong ESH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi)[-1]\cong\bigoplus_{p,\beta}H^{*}(B_{p,\beta}/S^{1})[-2-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]\cong

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{N=1}^{\infty}EH^{*}(\Sigma)[-1+2N\mu]\oplus\!\!\bigoplus_{m\geq 2,\,\beta}\;\bigoplus_{(k,m)=1}\!\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{\mathrm{rk}(Y_{m,\beta})}\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\subset Y_{m,\beta}}H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})[-2j-\mu(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha};Y_{m,\beta})-\mu(B_{k/m,\beta})]\right)\!.

For CSRs, one can also explicitly compute

EH^{*}(\Sigma)

inductively in terms of Betti numbers of the

by using the Gysin sequence which relates

and
H^{*}(\Sigma).
All the examples of CSRs we consider in

satisfy the conditions of
1.33
.

We remark that
Equation

27
is in general a highly non-trivial identification:

(1)

Firstly, sometimes
20
20

20

E.g. for the minimal resolution of an
A_{4}
-singularity,
M\to\mathbb{C}^{2}/(\mathbb{Z}/5)
, the three fixed points lying on the inner two spheres of the core do not belong to outer torsion submanifolds.
The copies of the middle point get identified with the
EH^{*}(\Sigma)
-summands. The other two inner points get identified with their adjacent
(connected via a sphere)
outer points.
not all
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
appear on the right of (
27
),
since they may not belong to any outer torsion submanifolds
Y_{m,\beta}
.

(2)

Even when all
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
appear on the right, some may appear more often than others,
resulting in identifications, e.g. between the minimum and the maximum fixed component in an
outer torsion manifold
(this occurs for
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{3}
with a twisted action and for the Slodowy variety
\mathcal{S}_{32}
).

(3)

Even when the
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
match perfectly via (
27
), classes in the
EH^{*}(\Sigma)
rephrased as Betti numbers of
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}

can match non-trivially with Betti numbers of
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
on the left in (
27
),
causing
identifications, e.g. between Poincaré-dual classes for some
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
(this occurs for the Slodowy variety
\mathcal{S}_{211}
).

(4)

Moreover, both types of identifications mentioned in (1) and (2) can occur at the same time (this occurs for the Slodowy variety
\mathcal{S}_{33}
).

(5)

Finally, (
27
) describes a free
\mathbb{F}
-module
ESH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi)[-1]
that is isomorphic to
H^{*}(Y)\otimes\mathbb{F}
as a
\mathbb{K}[u]
-module. So the ranks over
\mathbb{K}
of
(
27
) must arrange themselves into copies of
\mathbb{F}
. This arrangement is not always clear because the spectral sequence forgot the
u
-action.
In any case, (
27
) often yields the precise
\varphi
-filtration ranks

\mathrm{rk}_{\mathbb{K}}\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
, and otherwise yields good lower bounds.

Remark 1.34

(Stacking the
\mathbb{F}
copies)
.

Regarding (5) above, a similar situation arose in the naturality comments in
[
MR23
, Sec.1.5]
(there one would like to stack
\mathbb{K}
-copies to form
\mathbb{F}
-summands in the way that the ranks in
[
MR23
, Cor.2.13]
are suggesting, but not enough information about the
u
-action was retained by the spectral sequence). In our setup,
EH^{*}(B_{k/m,\beta})
should stack with
EH^{*}(B_{(k+1)/m,\beta})
to build
\mathbb{F}
, suitably interpreted when
k,m
or
k+1,m
are not coprime.
21
21

21

For example, if
(k,m)=1
but
(k+1)/m=k^{\prime}/m^{\prime}
after reducing to coprime
k^{\prime},m^{\prime}
, then there is some
B_{k^{\prime}/m^{\prime},\gamma}
containing a stratum diffeomorphic to
B_{k/m,\beta}
such that the diffeomorphism is given by a rescaling of the
X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
-flow.

More precisely, in the coprime case, let
C:=B_{k/m,\beta}/S^{1}
,
C^{\prime}:=B_{(k+1)/m,\beta}/S^{1}
, and
\lambda=((k+1)/m)^{+}
.
We follow the notation from
[
MR23
, Sec.4.2]
, and will refer to maxima/minima of auxiliary Morse functions on
C,C^{\prime}
. One might expect that the maximum
x
of
C
, which yields a class
[xu^{0}]
in the
E_{1}
-page, is represented in
EHF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
by
z=xu^{0}+x^{\prime}u^{-j-1},

j=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}C^{\prime}
, for
x^{\prime}
a chain in the local Floer complex for
C^{\prime}
not appearing in
E_{1}
due to a non-trivial vertical differential
\delta_{j+1}(x^{\prime})\neq 0
. As
[z]
is a cycle, so
(\delta_{0}+u\delta_{1}+\cdots)z=0
, one expects
\delta_{j+1}(x^{\prime})u^{0}=-\delta_{0}(x)u^{0}
, with the
u
-action
u\cdot[z]=[x^{\prime}u^{-j}]
yielding the minimum of
C
on the
E_{1}
-page. The question is what might be a canonical Floer trajectory,
\partial_{s}u+I(\partial_{t}u-k(s,t)X_{S^{1}})=0
for
k(s,t)=c^{\prime}(H(u(s,t)))
, counted by
\delta_{0}(x)
that “links”
C
to
C^{\prime}
?

Conjecture.

It is
u(s,t)=\varphi_{\exp(2\pi im/k)}(v(s))
with
v^{\prime}(s)=-\lambda_{s}\nabla H
, so
v
is a Morse continuation solution for a homotopy
\lambda_{s}H
where
\lambda_{s}=k(s,t)-\tfrac{m}{k}
goes from
0
to
1/k
. The Floer equation decouples as
\partial_{s}u=-\lambda_{s}\nabla H
and
\partial_{t}u=\tfrac{m}{k}X_{S^{1}}
, so a family of
S^{1}
-orbits
u(s,\cdot)
in
Y_{m,\beta}
moving by the
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-action.
