2.
Preliminaries

We will summarise some definitions and results from
[
RŽ23
]
to facilitate referencing them.

2.1.
The definition of symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds

In this paper, symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds are always over a convex base, and we now summarise what that means.
We have a connected symplectic manifold
(Y,\omega)
, a choice of
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
I
on
Y
, and a pseudoholomorphic

\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
\varphi
on
(Y,I)
, such that its
S^{1}
-part is Hamiltonian. We denote by
H:Y\to\mathbb{R}
the moment map of the
S^{1}
-action. The induced
S^{1}
-invariant Riemannian metric is
g(\cdot,\cdot):=\omega(\cdot,I\cdot)
.

We call
\mathbb{R}_{+}
the subgroup
\mathbb{R}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
s\mapsto e^{2\pi s}
, and
S^{1}
the subgroup
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
s\mapsto e^{2\pi is}\subset\mathbb{C}^{*}
. The flows of the
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-part
and
S^{1}
-part
of
\varphi
determine vector fields

X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
and
X_{S^{1}}
such that:

(28)

X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}=\nabla H,\qquad X_{S^{1}}=X_{H}=I\nabla H=IX_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}.

We require that there is an
(I,I_{B})
-pseudoholomorphic proper map

\Psi:Y^{\mathrm{out}}=Y\setminus\mathrm{int}(Y^{\mathrm{in}})\to B^{\mathrm{out}}=\Sigma\times[R_{0},\infty),

where
R_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
is any constant;
(\Sigma,\alpha)
is a closed contact manifold;
I_{B}
is a
d(R\alpha)
-compatible almost complex structure on
B^{\mathrm{out}}
of contact type such that

(29)

\Psi_{*}X_{S^{1}}=w\mathcal{R}_{B}

where
\mathcal{R}_{B}
is the Reeb vector field for
\Sigma
, and
w>0
is a constant. By a rescaling trick, one can always assume
w=1
(
2.2
).
Above,
I
and
I_{B}
are
almost
complex structures.
We also assume that
B^{\mathrm{out}}
is geometrically bounded at infinity.

We often abusively write
\Psi:Y\to B
even though the map
\Psi
is only defined at infinity, and we sometimes write
B^{\mathrm{out}}
instead of
B
even though

B^{\mathrm{out}}
is not required to have a filling
B
.
We call
Y

globally defined over a convex base
if
\Psi
actually extends to a pseudoholomorphic proper
S^{1}
-equivariant map

\Psi:(Y,I)\to(B,I_{B})

defined on all of
Y
, whose target is a symplectic manifold

(B,\omega_{B},I_{B})
convex at infinity, with a Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action, whose Reeb flow at infinity agrees with the
S^{1}
-action. Again,
I_{B}
is an
\omega_{B}
-compatible almost complex structure, of contact type at infinity.
The definition in fact implies that
\Psi
is
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant, by integrating
\Psi_{*}X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
and
\Psi_{*}X_{S^{1}}
on
\mathrm{Im}(\Psi)\subset B
.

Remark 2.1
.

If we only had an
I
-pseudoholomorphic
22
22

22

meaning
(\psi_{t})_{*}\circ I=I\circ(\psi_{t})_{*}
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

S^{1}
-action,

\psi_{t}:=\varphi_{e^{2\pi it}}:Y\to Y
, then this locally extends to a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action. The Lie derivative of its vector field
X_{S^{1}}
satisfies
\mathcal{L}_{X_{S^{1}}}(I)=0
, so
X_{S^{1}}
and
X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}:=-IX_{S^{1}}
commute.
23
23

23

[X_{S^{1}},IX_{S^{1}}]=\mathcal{L}_{X_{S^{1}}}(IX_{S^{1}})=I\mathcal{L}_{X_{S^{1}}}(X_{S^{1}})=0
.
So we get a partially defined pseudoholomorphic map
\varphi:\mathbb{C}^{*}\times Y\to Y
,
\varphi_{e^{2\pi(s+it)}}=\mathrm{Flow}_{X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}}^{s}\circ\psi_{t}
. If
X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
is integrable then this
\varphi
becomes a globally defined
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action.

Remark 2.2
.

B
being
convex at infinity
means there is a compact subdomain
B^{\mathrm{in}}\subset B
outside of which we have a
conical end

B^{\mathrm{out}}:=B\setminus\mathrm{int}(B^{\mathrm{in}})\cong\Sigma\times[R_{0},\infty)
such that the symplectic form becomes
\omega_{B}=d(R\alpha)
.
The radial coordinate
R\in[R_{0},\infty)
yields the Reeb vector field
\mathcal{R}_{B}

for the contact hypersurface
(\Sigma,\alpha)
,
\Sigma:=\{R=R_{0}\}
(defined by
d\alpha(\mathcal{R}_{B},\cdot)=0
and
\alpha(\mathcal{R}_{B})=1
). So
\mathcal{R}_{B}=X_{R}
is the Hamiltonian vector field for the function
R
.
After increasing
R_{0}
if necessary, we can always assume that
I_{B}
is
\omega_{B}
-compatible and of
contact type
on
B^{\mathrm{out}}
, meaning
24
24

24

Equivalently
dR=R\alpha\circ I_{B}
, so
I_{B}
preserves the contact distribution
\xi=\ker\alpha\subset T\Sigma
. The
\omega_{B}
-compatibility condition ensures that
d\alpha
is an
I_{B}
-compatible symplectic form on
\xi
.
By
[
Rit16
, Lemma C.9]
, it suffices to assume
a(R)dR=R\alpha\circ I_{B}
for a positive smooth function
a
, equivalently

I_{B}Z_{B}=a(R)\mathcal{R}_{B}
.

I_{B}Z_{B}=\mathcal{R}_{B}
, where
Z_{B}=R\partial_{R}
is the
Liouville vector field
defined by
\omega_{B}(Z_{B},\cdot)=R\alpha
on
B^{\mathrm{out}}
. If
I_{B}
does not depend on
R
,
then clearly
B^{\mathrm{out}}
is geometrically bounded at infinity due to the radial symmetry.
If
I_{B}
depends on
R
(on the
\xi=\ker d\alpha
orthogonal summand of
TB^{\mathrm{out}}=\xi\oplus\mathbb{R}Z_{B}\oplus\mathbb{R}\mathcal{R}_{B}
),
then it is desirable to require
that
B^{\mathrm{out}}
is geometrically bounded at infinity. This assumption is needed to prove that Floer solutions “consume
F
-filtration” if they go far out at infinity on a long region on which
c^{\prime}(H)
is linear (
3.12
). This property is needed in
1.11
, in the construction of the
Q_{\varphi}
class (
1.2
), and we use it to ensure a certain consistency between Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequences so that we can take the direct limit over slopes
\lambda
, specifically in
3.12
.
25
25

25

The geometric boundedness assumption on
B
is not needed for results preceding that Lemma, e.g. one can prove the maximum principle and define
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
without it.

The rescaling trick to reduce to
w=1
in (
29
)
is to rescale

R,\alpha,R_{0}
to
wR,\alpha/w,wR_{0}
(leaving
\omega_{B}=d(R\alpha)
and the Liouville form
\theta=R\alpha
unchanged on
B^{\mathrm{out}}
).
We showed in
[
RŽ23
]
that the map
\Psi:Y^{\mathrm{out}}\to\mathrm{Im}(\Psi)
is
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant, for a partially defined
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on
\mathrm{Im}(\Psi)\subset B^{\mathrm{out}}

arising from integrating
\Psi_{*}X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}=\nabla R=Z_{B}
and
\Psi_{*}X_{S^{1}}=\mathcal{R}_{B}
on
\mathrm{Im}(\Psi)
.

Lemma 2.3
.

The
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
\varphi
on a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold
(Y,\omega,I,\varphi)
is
contracting
, meaning there is a compact subdomain
Y^{\mathrm{in}}\subset Y
such that the
-X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
flow starting from any point
y\in Y
will eventually enter and stay in
Y^{\mathrm{in}}
. In particular,
H
is bounded below, the
\varphi
-fixed locus
\mathfrak{F}=\sqcup_{\alpha}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
in (
4
) is compact, and all points
y\in Y
have well-defined
convergence points

y_{0}:=\lim_{\mathbb{C}^{*}\ni t\to 0}t\cdot y\in\mathfrak{F}
.

We always assume that the moment map
H
on a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold is proper, by tweaking
\omega
:

Lemma 2.4
.

For
\Psi:Y^{\mathrm{out}}\rightarrow B^{\mathrm{out}}=\Sigma\times[R_{0},\infty)
any symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold,
\omega_{\phi}:=\omega+d(\Psi^{*}(\phi(R)\alpha))

is a symplectic form cohomologous to
\omega
for which the
S^{1}
-action is Hamiltonian and has a proper moment map.
Here
\phi:[R_{0},\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is any non-decreasing smooth function vanishing near
R=R_{0}
.

If
\Psi:Y\rightarrow B
is globally defined over a convex base,
\omega+\Psi^{*}\omega_{B}
is a symplectic form for which the
S^{1}
-action is Hamiltonian and has a proper moment map.

Once
H
is proper, it is also exhausting and has connected level sets (using that
Y
is connected).

2.2.
Torsion points. Attraction graph.

Lemma 2.5
.

The fixed locus of the subgroup

\mathbb{Z}/m\hookrightarrow S^{1}\subset\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
k\mapsto e^{2\pi ik/m}
, for
m\geq 2
, defines a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-invariant
I
-pseudoholomorphic
\omega
-symplectic submanifold
Y_{m}\subset Y
, whose points are called

\mathbb{Z}/m
-torsion points
, and whose connected components
Y_{m,\beta}
are called
torsion submanifolds
. Moreover:

(1)

Y_{m}
is the image of all
1
-periodic Hamiltonian orbits of
\tfrac{1}{m}H:Y\to\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

Y_{m}
contains all
Y_{mb}
for integers
b\geq 1
.

(3)

Y_{m}\subset Y
is a closed subset, with a relatively open dense stratum
Y_{m}\setminus\cup_{b\geq 2}Y_{mb}
.

(4)

Each
Y_{m,\beta}
is a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-submanifold of
Y
, and its
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action admits an
m
-th root.

(5)

At
p\in\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
the tangent space
T_{p}Y_{m}
is the
\mathbb{Z}/m
-fixed locus of the linearised action, so

(30)

T_{p}Y_{m}=\oplus_{b\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{mb}\subset T_{p}Y.

(6)

In a sufficiently small neighbourhood of
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
,
Y_{m}
is the image

Y_{\alpha,m}^{\mathrm{loc}}
via
\exp_{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}}
of a small neighbourhood of the zero section in
\oplus_{b}H_{mb}\subset T_{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}}Y
. Globally
Y_{m}=\cup_{\alpha}(\mathbb{C}^{*}\cdot Y_{\alpha,m}^{\mathrm{loc}})
.

(7)

There is a finite number of
Y_{m,\beta},
each of which is a union of various
\mathbb{C}^{*}\cdot Y_{\alpha,m}^{\mathrm{loc}}
.

(8)

Y_{m,\beta}
has a unique minimal component
\min(H|_{Y_{m,\beta}}:Y_{m,\beta}\to\mathbb{R})
, which is the
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\subset Y_{m,\beta}
for which all weights
mb
in (
30
) are non-negative.

(9)

The intersection
Y_{m,\beta}\cap\mathrm{Core}(Y)=\mathrm{Core}(Y_{m,\beta})
is path-connected.

(10)

If
Y_{m,\beta}
contains a single
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
, it is called a
torsion bundle

\mathcal{H}_{m}\rightarrow\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
, and

Y_{m,\beta}
is diffeomorphic to the weight
m
-part

\oplus_{b}H_{mb}=\oplus_{b\geq 0}H_{mb}
of the normal bundle of
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
, and
\mathrm{Core}(Y_{m,\beta})=\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
.

Definition 2.6
.

\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
is
m
-minimal
if it is the minimal component of some
Y_{m,\beta}
,
equivalently
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
has a non-zero weight divisible by
m

and all such weights are positive (
T_{p}Y_{m,\beta}=T_{p}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\oplus\oplus_{b\geq 1}H_{mb}
in (
30
)).

Lemma 2.7
.

Any
y\in Y
yields a pseudoholomorphic disc

\psi_{y}:\mathbb{D}\to Y
,
\psi_{y}(z)=\varphi_{z}(y)
,
\psi_{y}(0)=y_{0}
.
A unitary basis
v_{i}
for
T_{y_{0}}Y
induces a canonical unitary (so symplectic) trivialisation
v_{i}(z)
of
\psi_{y}^{*}TY
with
v_{i}(z)=v_{i}
. The trivialisation is
S^{1}
-equivariant in
y
in the sense that
v_{i}(tz)
is the canonical trivialisation of
\psi_{ty}^{*}TY
induced by
v_{i}
, for any
t\in S^{1}
.

If the
S^{1}
-orbit of
y
has minimal period
1/m
, for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\psi_{y}(z)
is an
m
-fold cover of a pseudoholomorphic disc
\hat{\psi}_{y}:\mathbb{D}\to Y
, and
v_{i}(z)
is induced by a canonical trivialisation of
\hat{\psi}_{y}^{*}TY
, in particular it is
\mathbb{Z}/m
-equivariant:
v_{i}(\zeta z)=v_{i}(z)
whenever
\zeta^{m}=1
.

\mathrm{Core}(Y)
is covered by copies of
\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
arising as the closures of
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-orbits. The non-constant spheres are embedded except possibly at the two points where they meet
\mathfrak{F}
(where several different
\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
may meet).
The
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-orbit closure of any
y\in\mathrm{Core}(Y)
determines a pseudoholomorphic sphere

u_{y}:\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\to Y,\;\;u_{y}([1:t])=\varphi_{t}(y),\;\;u_{y}([1:0])=y_{0},\;\;u_{y}([0:1])=y_{\infty},

where
y_{0}
is the convergence point of
y
, and
y_{\infty}:=\lim_{\mathbb{C}^{*}\ni t\rightarrow\infty}t\cdot y

In particular,

\mathbb{C}\cong\mathrm{im}\,du_{y}|_{[1:0]}\subset T_{y_{0}}Y\;\;\mathrm{and}\;\;\mathbb{C}\cong\mathrm{im}\,du_{y}|_{[0:1]}\subset T_{y_{\infty}}Y

are weight subspaces of opposite weights
k
and
-k
respectively, for some integer
k\geq 0
(with
k=0
precisely if
u_{y}\equiv y\in\mathfrak{F}
is constantly equal to a fixed point).

Definition 2.8
.

The
attraction graph

\Gamma_{\varphi}
is a directed graph whose vertices represent the
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
, and there are
N
edges from

\alpha_{1}
to
\alpha_{2}
if the space of
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-flowlines from
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha_{1}}
to
\mathfrak{F}_{a_{2}}
(as
t\to\infty
, so
H
increases) has
N
connected components.
The
leaves
of
\Gamma_{\varphi}
are vertices with no outgoing edges, i.e. if
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
is a local maximum of
H|_{\mathrm{Core}(Y)}
.
A leaf is
m
-minimal
if the corresponding
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
is
m
-minimal (
2.6
).

The
extended attraction graph

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\varphi}

decorates
{\Gamma_{\varphi}}
additionally with an outward-pointing arrow at a vertex
\alpha
for each torsion bundle
\mathcal{H}_{m}\rightarrow\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
.

Lemma 2.9
.

The attraction graph satisfies the following properties, where
V:=\#\textrm{Vertices}(\Gamma_{\varphi})
,

(1)

\Gamma_{\varphi}
is a connected directed acyclic graph.

(2)

Each
m
-minimal leaf for
m\geq 2
has an
m
-torsion bundle converging to it (thus the action
\varphi
is not free outside of
\mathrm{Core}(Y)
).

(3)

For CSRs with
V\geq 2
,
every leaf
\alpha
is
m_{\alpha}
-minimal for the largest weight
m_{\alpha}\geq 2
of
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
.

(4)

If
c_{1}(Y)=0
,
V\geq 2
and
\mathrm{Core}(Y)\subset Y
is equicodimensional near
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
and near some leaf, then
\varphi
does not act freely outside of
\mathrm{Core}(Y)
.

2.3.
Technical remarks: coefficients and monotonicity assumptions

We will not review in detail the chain-level construction of
HF^{*}(F).
26
26

26

see e.g.
[
Rit10
]
for details.

The Floer chain complex
CF^{*}(F)
is a module over a certain Novikov field
\mathbb{K}.

When
c_{1}(Y)=0
, we will in fact work over the
Novikov field

(31)

\textstyle\mathbb{K}=\{\sum n_{j}T^{a_{j}}:a_{j}\in\mathbb{R},a_{j}\to\infty,n_{j}\in\mathbb{B}\},

where
T
is a formal variable in grading zero, and
\mathbb{B}
is any choice of base field. In the monotone case, the same Novikov field can be used but
T
lies in non-zero grading
[
Rit16
, Sec.2A]
. In other situations, e.g. the weakly-monotone setup, the Novikov field is more complicated
[
Rit16
, Sec.5B]
.

We always work with
admissible Hamiltonians

F:Y\to\mathbb{R}
, i.e. on
Y^{\mathrm{out}}
they are linear in
H
of a generic slope. The (perturbed) chain complex
CF^{*}(F)
is a free
\mathbb{K}
-module
generated by the
1
-periodic orbits of a generic
C^{2}
-small time-dependent perturbation of
F
supported away from the region at infinity where
F
is linear. In the Appendices, we explain the Morse–Bott–Floer model for
CF^{*}(F)
that avoids perturbations: the free generators over
\mathbb{K}
are the critical points of auxiliary Morse functions on the Morse–Bott manifolds of
1
-orbits of
F
.

The Novikov field
\mathbb{K}
from (
31
) is a
\mathbb{B}
-vector space,
flat over
\mathbb{B}
, so
H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{K})\cong H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{B})\otimes_{\mathbb{B}}\mathbb{K}
. Thus
1.1
yields an (
\omega
- and
\varphi
-dependent) filtration on
H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{B})
by
\mathbb{B}
-vector subspaces for any field
\mathbb{B}
. Floer/quantum cohomology is also defined over a Novikov ring
\mathbb{K}
using any underlying ring
\mathbb{B}

[
HS95
]
(by
Section

2.3
), and
1.1
still holds.
If one forgoes the multiplicative structure by ideals, one can more generally replace
\mathbb{B}
by any abelian group, yielding a filtration on
H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{B})
by abelian subgroups.

We always tacitly assume that our symplectic manifold
Y
is
weakly-monotone

so that transversality arguments in Floer theory can be dealt with by the methods of Hofer–Salamon
[
HS95
]
. This means one of the following holds:

(1)

c_{1}(Y)(A)=0
when we evaluate on any spherical class
A\in\pi_{2}(Y)
, or

(2)

\omega(Y)(A)=0
when we evaluate on any spherical class
A\in\pi_{2}(Y)
, or

(3)

for some
k>0
we have
c_{1}(Y)(A)=k\cdot\omega(A)
for all
A\in\pi_{2}(Y)
, or

(4)

the smallest positive value
c_{1}(Y)(A)\geq n-2
where
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}Y=2n
.

Case (2) holds if
\omega
is exact; (3) is the
monotone
case.
In
1.2
we use
weak+ monotonicity

[
Rit14
, Sec.2.2]
which means the same as above except
n-2
in (4) becomes
n-1
.

We do not use the Floer cohomology of the base
B
, so we do not require that
B
is weakly-monotone when
Y
is globally defined over a convex base (of course
B^{\mathrm{out}}
is exact).

2.4.
Floer-theoretic indices

Let
Y
be a
symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold with
c_{1}(Y)=0
. After choosing a trivialisation of the canonical bundle
\Lambda^{\mathrm{top}}_{\mathbb{C}}T^{*}Y
, Hamiltonian Floer cohomology (and thus
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
) can be
\mathbb{Z}
-graded.
The Hamiltonian
S^{1}
-action
\varphi
admits a
Maslov index

\mu
, discussed in
[
RŽ23
]
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
T_{x}Y=\oplus\mathbb{C}_{w_{i}}
be the weight decomposition for the linearised
S^{1}
-action at a fixed point
x\in\mathfrak{F}
, where
\mathbb{C}_{w_{i}}
is a copy of
\mathbb{C}
with the standard
S^{1}
-action of weight
w_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then

\mu=\sum w_{i}>0
.

In
[
RŽ23
, Appendix A]
we discussed conventions about Robbin–Salamon indices
\mu_{RS}
. This is a
\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
-valued index defined for any continuous path
[0,1]\rightarrow Sp(\mathbb{C}^{n},\Omega_{0})
of real symplectic matrices in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with the standard real symplectic structure
\Omega_{0}
. We recall here the main properties that we will need.

Theorem 2.11
.

The Robbin–Salamon index satisfies the following properties:

(1)

\mu_{RS}
is invariant under homotopies with fixed endpoints.

(2)

\mu_{RS}
is additive under concatenation of paths.

(3)

For a direct sum
\psi_{1}\diamond\psi_{2}:\mathbb{C}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{m}
,
\mu_{RS}(\psi_{1}\diamond\psi_{2})=\mu_{RS}(\psi_{1})+\mu_{RS}(\psi_{2}).

(4)

For
\psi,\phi
two continuous paths of symplectic matrices,
\mu_{RS}(\phi\psi\phi^{-1})=\mu_{RS}(\psi).

(5)

\mu_{RS}((e^{2\pi is})_{s\in[0,x]})=\mathbb{W}(x),
where

(32)

\mathbb{W}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{Z},\quad\mathbb{W}(x):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}2\lfloor x\rfloor+1&\text{if}\ x\notin\mathbb{Z}\\
2x&\text{if}\ x\in\mathbb{Z}.\end{array}\right.

(6)

The Robbin–Salamon index of the symplectic shear

\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
b(t)&1\end{bmatrix}
is equal to
\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathrm{sign}(b(1))-\mathrm{sign}(b(0))).

We remark on the following basic properties of the function
\mathbb{W}
:

(33)

\mathbb{W}(0)=0,\;\;\quad\mathbb{W}(-x)=-\mathbb{W}(x),\;\;\quad\mathbb{W}(x)\textrm{ is odd except at }\mathbb{Z},\;\;\quad 2x\geq\mathbb{W}(x)-1\geq 2x-2.

For any 1-orbit
x
of a Hamiltonian
F
in a symplectic manifold
M
of dimension
2n
, let
\phi_{t}
be the Hamiltonian flow and consider its linearisation

(\phi_{t})_{*}:T_{x(0)}M\rightarrow T_{x(t)}M
.
We pick a symplectic trivialisation
\Phi:x^{*}TM\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}\times S^{1}

of the tangent bundle above the orbit
x
to get a path of symplectic matrices
\psi(t)=\Phi_{t}\circ(\phi_{t})_{*}\circ\Phi^{-1}_{0}:\mathbb{C}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}.

Then define
RS(x,F)
and the
grading
27
27

27

That convention ensures that for a
C^{2}
-small Morse Hamiltonian,
|x|
equals the Morse index.

|x|
of
x
by

(34)

RS(x,F):=\mu_{RS}(\psi)\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad|x|:=n-RS(x,F).

For the Hamiltonian
\lambda H,
the
grading
of a connected component
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
of
\mathfrak{F}
is defined as

\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}):=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\,Y-\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\,\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}-RS(x,\lambda H),

which is independent of the choice of
x\in\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
. This
\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})
is the Floer grading of
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
seen as a Morse–Bott manifold of Hamiltonian
1

(
Appendix

).

Proposition 2.12
.

For a generic slope
\lambda
, the only
1-
periodic orbits of the Hamiltonian
\lambda H
are the constant orbits, i.e. the fixed points
x\in\mathfrak{F}
, and
\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow+\infty}RS(x,\lambda H)=+\infty
uniformly in

.

Lemma 2.13
.

\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})

are even integers for positive
\lambda\notin\cup_{i}\mathbb{Z}\cdot\frac{1}{w_{i}}\subset\mathbb{Q},

where
w_{i}
are weights of
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}.

We abbreviate
|V|:=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\,V
.
In the weight decomposition (
5
), let
h_{k}^{\alpha}=|H_{k}|=\#\{i:w_{i}=k\}
.

Corollary 2.14
.

The Morse–Bott index
\mu_{\alpha}
of
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha},
the Maslov index
\mu
, and the
\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})
above, satisfy:

(35)

\begin{split}&\mu_{\alpha}=2\sum_{k<0}h_{k}^{\alpha}\\
&\mu=\sum k\cdot h_{k}^{\alpha}=\sum_{k>0}k(h_{k}^{\alpha}-h_{-k}^{\alpha})\\
&\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=|Y|-|\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}|-\sum_{k}\mathbb{W}(\lambda k)\,h_{k}^{\alpha}=\sum_{k\neq 0}(1-\mathbb{W}(\lambda k))\,h_{k}^{\alpha}=\sum_{w_{i}\neq 0}(1-\mathbb{W}(\lambda w_{i}))\end{split}

(1)

\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})\leq 2|Y|-|\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}|-2\lambda\mu
for any
\lambda>0
.

(2)

\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})\to-\infty
as
\lambda\to\infty
.

(3)

\mu_{\alpha}-\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=\sum_{k>0}(\mathbb{W}(\lambda k)-1)(h_{k}^{\alpha}-h_{-k}^{\alpha})
.

(4)

\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=\mu_{\alpha}
for all
0<\lambda<\lambda_{\alpha}:=\min\{\tfrac{1}{|k|}:h_{k}^{\alpha}\neq 0\textrm{ for }k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\}
.

(5)

\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=\mu_{\alpha}-2N\mu
for all
N<\lambda<N+\lambda_{\alpha}
, where
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

Definition 2.15
.

Let
k_{1}>k_{2}>\cdots>0
denote the absolute values of the non-zero weights
w_{i}
at
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
in descending order without repetitions.
We call
Y

if
\delta_{r}^{\alpha}:=\sum_{j=1}^{r}(h_{k_{j}}^{\alpha}-h_{-k_{j}}^{\alpha})\geq 0
for all
r
,
\alpha
.
E.g. all CSRs are compatibly-weighted, due to (
36
).

Let
\tau_{p}:=\tfrac{k_{0}}{m}
, for
k_{0},m
coprime positive integers, so
\tau_{p}k\in\mathbb{Z}
precisely when
k=mb
for some

.
Call

for some

.
Call

if
some

\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
(so
\lambda_{\alpha}
is the first
\alpha
-critical time).
Note that
\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})

can only change when
\lambda
is an
\alpha
-critical time (that is when
\mathbb{W}(\lambda k)
can jump/drop).
We abusively call
0<\lambda<\min_{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}
all
0-th critical times
, and
0<\lambda<\lambda_{\alpha}
all 0-th critical
\alpha
-times (for those,
\mu_{\alpha}=\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})
).

Note: if both
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{F}_{\gamma}\subset Y_{m,\beta}
then
\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\gamma})
for the critical time
\lambda=\tfrac{k_{0}}{m}
, as those indices equal the (homotopy-invariant) Floer grading of the
1
-orbits of
\lambda H
whose initial points lie in
Y_{m,\beta}
.

For a torsion submanifold
Y_{m,\beta}
, with minimal component
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
(
2.6
),
call

\textstyle\mathrm{rk}(Y_{m,\beta}):=|Y_{m,\beta}|-|\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}|=\sum_{b\geq 1}h_{mb}^{\alpha}

the
rank
of
Y_{m,\beta}
. When
Y_{m,\beta}
is a torsion bundle, this is its complex rank.

Proposition 2.16
.

If
Y
is compatibly-weighted (
2.15
) then

\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})\leq\mu_{\alpha}
for all
\lambda>0.

Notation 2.17
.

We write
\lambda^{+}
to mean a non-critical time just above a given critical time
\lambda=\tfrac{k_{0}}{m}
(
k_{0},m
coprime), with no critical times between
\lambda
and
\lambda^{+}
. Similarly for
\lambda^{-}
below
\lambda
.

Lemma 2.18
.

(1)

At a critical time
\lambda=\tfrac{k_{0}}{m}
,
\gcd(k_{0},m)=1

we have

\sum_{b\geq 1}(h_{mb}^{\alpha}-h_{-mb}^{\alpha})=\mu_{\lambda^{-}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})-\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})-\mu_{\lambda^{+}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=\frac{1}{2}(\mu_{\lambda^{-}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})-\mu_{\lambda^{+}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}));

(2)

if
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
is
m
-minimal (
2.6
), then the above sum equals
\mathrm{rk}(Y_{m,\beta})
, so:

\mu_{\lambda^{+}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})-\mathrm{rk}(Y_{m,\beta})\quad\textrm{ and }\quad\mu_{\lambda^{-}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})+\mathrm{rk}(Y_{m,\beta}).

(3)

In particular, denoting
H_{+}=\oplus_{k>0}H_{k}
and
H_{-}=\oplus_{k<0}H_{k}
the subspaces of (
5
),

\mu_{N^{-}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=\mu_{\alpha}-2N\mu+2(|H_{+}|-|H_{-}|).

In particular, for weight-1 CSRs,
\mu_{1^{-}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=0
.

2.5.
Review of Conical Symplectic Resolutions

A
weight-
s
CSR
is a projective resolution
28
28

28

Meaning:
\mathfrak{M}
is a smooth variety, and
\pi
is an isomorphism over the smooth locus of
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
.

\pi:\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{0}
of a normal
29
29

29

The normality assumption on
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is equivalent to the condition that
\pi:\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is the affinisation
map,
\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathrm{Aff}(\mathfrak{M}):=Spec(H^{0}(\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{M}})),\ p\mapsto\{f\mid f(p)=0\},
see
[
Ž22
, Lem.3.16]
.

affine variety
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
, where
(\mathfrak{M},\omega_{\mathbb{C}})
is a holomorphic symplectic manifold and
\pi
is equivariant with respect to
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-actions on
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
(both denoted by
\varphi
). We require:

(1)

The complex symplectic form
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
has a
weight

s\in\mathbb{N}
, so

\varphi_{t}^{*}\omega_{\mathbb{C}}=t^{s}\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
for all
t\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
.

(2)

The action
\varphi
contracts
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
to a single fixed point
x_{0}
, so
\forall x\in\mathfrak{M}_{0},\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}t\cdot x=x_{0}.

Algebraically,
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{0}]=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{0}]^{n}
and
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{0}]^{0}=\mathbb{C},
where
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{0}]^{n}
denotes the
n
-weight space.
30
30

30

explicitly

\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{0}]^{n}:=\{f\in\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{0}]\mid(t\cdot f)(x)=f(t\cdot x)=t^{n}f(x)\}.

Actions satisfying (1)-(2) are called
weight-
s
conical actions.

We summarise below the properties of CSRs and refer to
[
RŽ23
]
for detailed references.

Theorem 2.19
.

Let
\pi:\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{0}
be a weight-s CSR.

(1)

(\mathfrak{M},\varphi)
is a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold with
an
S^{1}
-invariant
I
-compatible Kähler structure
(g,I,\omega_{I}),

whose
S^{1}
-action is Hamiltonian, and its moment map
H
is proper.

(2)

(\mathfrak{M},\varphi)
is globally defined over a convex base via a map
\Psi:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{C}^{N}
(
Section

2.1
).

(3)

c_{1}(T\mathfrak{M},I)=0,
where
I
is its complex structure.

(4)

The Maslov index of the
S^{1}
-action is

\mu=\frac{1}{2}s\cdot\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}.

(5)

Its core
\mathfrak{L}:=\{p\in\mathfrak{M}\mid\lim_{\mathbb{C}^{*}\ni t\rightarrow\infty}t\cdot p\text{ exists}\}
is the central fibre,
\mathfrak{L}=\pi^{-1}(0).

(6)

\mathfrak{L}
is an
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
-isotropic and when
s=1,

an
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
-Lagrangian subvariety (usually singular).

(7)

Singular cohomology

H^{*}(\mathfrak{M})
over characteristic zero fields
lies in even degrees
\leq\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}.

(8)

The intersection form
H_{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}}(\mathfrak{M})\times H_{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}}(\mathfrak{M})\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}
is definite.

(9)

QH^{*}(\mathfrak{M})\cong H^{*}(\mathfrak{M},\mathbb{K})
as rings.

(10)

The
\varphi
-filtration in
1.1
, ordered by
p\in\mathbb{R}
, becomes

\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{p}:=\bigcap_{\textrm{generic }\lambda>p}\ker(c_{\lambda}^{*}:H^{*}(\mathfrak{M},\mathbb{K})\rightarrow HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})).

It is a filtration on singular cohomology
H^{*}(\mathfrak{M},\mathbb{K})

by ideals with respect to the cup product.

(11)

Given the
weight decomposition

T_{p}\mathfrak{M}=\oplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}H_{k}
of a fixed point
p\in\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha},

the following pairing is non-degenerate:

(36)

\qquad\qquad\omega_{\mathbb{C}}:H_{k}\oplus H_{s-k}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}.\qquad(\omega_{\mathbb{C}}\textbf{-duality})

(12)

When
s=1
, we have
2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}+\mu_{\alpha}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathrm{rk}(H^{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}}(\mathfrak{M}))=\#\{\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\}\neq 0
.

Remark 2.20
.

The proper
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant holomorphic map
\Psi
can be constructed as follows:

\Psi=\Theta\circ j\circ\pi=(\pi^{*}(f_{1})^{w/w_{1}},\ldots,\pi^{*}(f_{N})^{w/w_{N}}):\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{C}^{N},

where
f_{1},\dots,f_{N}
is any choice of non-constant homogeneous polynomial generators of
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{0}]
, with weights
w_{i}>0
.
Thus
\mathbb{C}^{*}
acts diagonally on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
with weight
w:=\text{lcm}(w_{1},\dots,w_{N})
, but we can use the rescaling trick in
2.2
to get rid of
w
in (
29
). The map
\Theta\circ j:\mathfrak{M}_{0}\to\mathbb{C}^{N}
is
a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant holomorphic map, and a local embedding except at
0\in\mathfrak{M}_{0}
.
Above,
j
is the embedding
j:\mathfrak{M}_{0}\to\mathbb{C}^{N}
,
p\mapsto(f_{1}(p),\dots,f_{N}(p)),
with
j(0)=0
, and
\Theta:\mathbb{C}^{N}\to\mathbb{C}^{N}
,
\Theta(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})=(z_{1}^{w/w_{1}},\dots,z_{N}^{w/w_{N}}).

Let
\hat{\pi}:=3.1415\ldots
. The
S^{1}
-action
e^{2\hat{\pi}it}
on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
has Hamiltonian
\hat{\pi}(|z_{1}|^{2}+\cdots+|z_{N}|^{2})
, and we define

(37)

\Phi:=\hat{\pi}\,\Psi^{*}(|z_{1}|^{2}+\cdots+|z_{N}|^{2})=\hat{\pi}\sum\pi^{*}(|f_{i}|^{2w/w_{i}}):\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R},

so
\mathfrak{L}=\Phi^{-1}(0)
. The function
\Phi
is typically not related to the moment map
H:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}
.

CSRs are rarely ever convex at infinity, because any choice of
I
-compatible (real) symplectic form
\omega
on
\mathfrak{M}
is non-exact at infinity unless
0\in\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is an isolated singularity; indeed a CSR is convex at infinity only if it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{2n}
for some
n
, or it is a minimal resolution of an ADE singularity.

3.
Filtration on the Floer chain complex

In this Section,
(Y,\omega,I,\varphi)
is a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold over a convex base
B
. Recall that we can always tweak
\omega
by
2.4
to make
H
proper in (
3
), which we assume from now on.

3.1.
Overview

Our goal is to construct positive symplectic cohomology
SH^{*}_{+}(Y)
and a Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequence converging to
H^{*}(Y),
from which one can read-off the filtration of
1.1
.
We will construct a specific Hamiltonian

H_{\lambda}
with generic slope
\lambda
which filters
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
by the value of
H
.
Suppose for simplicity in this overview that
\Psi:Y\to B
is globally defined (see Definition
2.1
).
We will use the
2
-form

(38)

\eta:=d(\phi(R)\alpha)=\phi(R)\,d\alpha+\phi^{\prime}(R)\,dR\wedge\alpha

on the base
B
, for a non-decreasing cut-off function
\phi
, with
\phi=0
on
B^{\mathrm{in}}
. This form is non-negative on Floer solutions in
B
, and this in turn leads to a functional that filters the Floer complexes for
B
(a trick introduced in
[
MR23
, Sec.6]
for symplectic manifolds convex at infinity). We cannot apply this directly to
Y
as it is not convex at infinity, so we try to exploit the projection
\Psi
to
B
where it does apply. Unfortunately, the projection of a Floer solution in
Y
may not be a Floer solution in
B
. Even for a Hamiltonian
F=c(H):Y\to\mathbb{R}
which is just a function of the moment map
H
, the Hamiltonian vector field
X_{F}=c^{\prime}(H)\,X_{S^{1}}
projects via
\Psi
to a vector field
c^{\prime}(H)\,\mathcal{R}_{B}
on
B
which still depends
31
31

31

H
is in general unrelated to the pulled back Hamiltonian
\Psi^{*}H_{B}=H_{B}\circ\Psi
since

\Psi^{*}\omega_{B}
and
\omega
typically differ on
Y
.
on the coordinates of
Y
via
H
. For a Floer solution
u=u(s,t)
in
Y
, letting

(39)

k(s,t)=c^{\prime}(H\circ u),

this means that
c^{\prime}(H)\,\mathcal{R}_{B}=X_{k(s,t)\cdot H_{B}}
is a domain-dependent Hamiltonian vector field.
So the second key idea is to create regions in
Y
where
c^{\prime}(H)
is constant, thus
k(s,t)
is domain-independent there, and to pick
\phi
suitably on these regions so that
\eta
is non-negative on projected Floer solutions. Then if
u
crosses these regions it will cause a strict decrease in the filtration functional.

If
\Psi:Y\to B
is not globally defined, we only have
\Psi:Y^{\mathrm{out}}\to\Sigma\times[R_{0},\infty)
at our disposal (see Definition
2.1
) so the projection
v=\Psi\circ u
is not defined at
(s,t)
if
u(s,t)\notin Y^{\mathrm{out}}
.
However, in all our computations in
B
the terms involving
v(s,t)
will contain a factor
\phi
or
\phi^{\prime}
, and so these terms can be extended to be zero when
u(s,t)\in Y^{\mathrm{in}}
. More intrinsically on
Y
, our computations can be rephrased using the form
\Psi^{*}\eta
, and this form is well-defined on all of
Y
by extending it to be zero on
Y^{\mathrm{in}}
, since we assume
\phi=0
near
R=R_{0}
(compare
2.4
).

To avoid over-complicating this Section further, we will postpone the issue of transversality for Floer solutions to
Section

4
.
Although classical methods involving perturbations of
I
(respectively
c
) achieve transversality, such perturbations affect the holomorphicity of
\Psi
(respectively the equation
\Psi_{*}X_{F}=k(x,t)\,\mathcal{R}_{B}
), which are used throughout this Section.

3.2.
Construction of a specific Hamiltonian
H_{\lambda}

Let
(Y,\omega,I,\varphi)
be a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold over a convex base.
We have an
S^{1}
-equivariant proper holomorphic map
\Psi:Y^{\mathrm{out}}\to\Sigma\times[R_{0},\infty)
, but we abusively write

\Psi:Y\rightarrow B

and it is understood that all constructions involving
\Psi
are only defined on
Y^{\mathrm{out}}
. For example, the pull-back of the Reeb flow on the base
B
,

\Phi:=\Psi^{*}H_{B}=H_{B}\circ\Psi:Y\to\mathbb{R},

is abusively written:
\Phi
is only defined on
Y^{\mathrm{out}}
, and
\Phi=R\circ\Psi
there. The abusive notation is to preserve the intuition coming from the setup where
\Psi
is globally defined, such as CSRs (in which case
B=\mathbb{C}^{N}
and
\Phi
is as in
2.20
).

Although
\Psi_{*}X_{S^{1}}=\mathcal{R}_{B}
we emphasise that
\Phi
and
H
are usually unrelated as
\Psi
is not symplectic, and their level sets are in general unrelated as
\|X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\|
is typically not constant on a level set of
H
.

Figure 1.
Graph of
H_{\lambda}
for
r=2

The Hamiltonian
H_{\lambda}
will be constructed as in Figure
1
in terms of a function
c
of
H
,

H_{\lambda}:=c\circ H.

This ensures that
X_{H_{\lambda}}=c^{\prime}(H)\cdot X_{H}
, so
1
-periodic Hamiltonian orbits of
H_{\lambda}
corresponds precisely to orbits of period
T=c^{\prime}(H)
of the flow of
X_{S^{1}}=X_{H}
.
The construction of
c:[\min H,+\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
involves a choice of values
\{R_{0}^{\prime},R_{0}^{\prime\prime},\dots,R_{r}^{\prime},R_{r}^{\prime\prime}\}
for
\Phi
and
\{H_{0}^{\prime},H_{0}^{\prime\prime},\dots,H_{r}^{\prime},H_{r}^{\prime\prime}\}
for
H
, which we describe later, as well as a choice of generic slopes
\lambda_{i}
lying in between the periods
T_{-i}<\lambda
of the
S^{1}
-action, so

0=T_{0}<\lambda_{0}<T_{-1}<\lambda_{1}<T_{-2}<\dots<T_{-r}<\lambda_{r}:=\lambda.

The period
T_{0}=0
refers to the constant orbits, i.e. the fixed loci
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}.

As illustrated in
Figure

1
, over the intervals
[H_{i}^{\prime},H_{i}^{\prime\prime}]
the slope
c^{\prime}(H)
can increase and in particular within this interval we ensure
H
attains precisely one value
H_{-i}\in(H_{i}^{\prime},H_{i}^{\prime\prime})
(for
i\geq 1
) at which the slope is a period,

(40)

c^{\prime}(H_{-i})=T_{-i},

so the non-constant
1
-periodic orbits of
H_{\lambda}
arise precisely in the regions
H=H_{-i}
.

We construct
c:[\min H,+\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
so that

(1)

c^{\prime}\geq 0
.

(2)

c^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
.

(3)

c^{\prime\prime}(H)>0
whenever
c^{\prime}(H)=T_{-i}
, for all
i=1,\dots,r
.

(4)

c(H)=\lambda_{i}H,
when
H\in[H_{i}^{\prime\prime},H_{i+1}^{\prime}],
for all
i=0,\dots,r-1
.

(5)

c(H)=\lambda_{r}H
for
H\in[H_{r}^{\prime\prime},+\infty).

We also assume that
c^{\prime}
is sufficiently small on
Y^{\mathrm{in}}
so that there are no non-constant
1
-periodic orbits of
H_{\lambda}
in
Y^{\mathrm{in}}
since the potential period values
c^{\prime}
are smaller than
T_{-1}
, thus in
Y^{\mathrm{in}}
the only
1
-orbits are constants at points of the
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
submanifolds. More precisely, by properness of
H
we can assume that

Y^{\mathrm{in}}:=\{H\leq m\}

is a sublevel set, where we choose
m
large enough so that

Y^{\mathrm{in}}\supset H^{-1}(H(\mathrm{Core}(Y))\supset\mathrm{Core}(Y).
We construct
c^{\prime}
to be small on
[\mathrm{min}(H),m]
, and ensure that
c^{\prime}\neq 0
except possibly at

\mathrm{Crit}(H)
, so that
\mathrm{Crit}(H)=\mathrm{Crit}(H_{\lambda})
, and so that
H_{\lambda}
has the same (constant)
1
-orbits as
H
in that region.
In particular, (1) and (2) above are really only needed for
Y^{\mathrm{out}}=\{H\geq m\}
.

When
Y=\mathfrak{M}
is a CSR,
\Psi
is globally defined over
B=\mathbb{C}^{N}
, and
H_{B}=\pi w\|z\|^{2}
is defined everywhere (the
S^{1}
-action has weight
w
, see
2.20
), and one can pick
\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{in}}=\{H\leq m\}
to be any sublevel set containing the core
\mathfrak{L}=\Psi^{-1}(0)
.
We recall that
by the rescaling trick in Remark
2.2
we can get rid of the factor of
w
that would appear in (
2
) for CSRs.

Lemma 3.1
.

The level sets
\Phi^{-1}(q)=\Psi^{-1}(\{H_{B}=q\})\subset Y

for
q\geq R_{0}
are closed submanifolds of
Y.

Proof.

d\Phi=dH_{B}\circ\Psi_{*}
is non-zero on
\nabla H=X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
as
\Psi_{*}X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}=\nabla H_{B}
(by
Equation

28
).
∎

As in Figure
2
we pick a function
h(R)
of
R
which is linear of slope
\lambda_{i}>0
over the interval

L_{i}:=[R_{i}^{\prime\prime},R_{i+1}^{\prime}],

where
L
stands for “linear”, and
N_{i}:=[R_{i}^{\prime},R_{i}^{\prime\prime}]
are the intervals where
h
can be non-linear.

Figure 2.
Graph of
h(R)
for
r=2

Explicitly,
h:[R_{0},\infty)\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
is required to satisfy

(1)

h^{\prime}\geq 0
.

(2)

h^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
, and
h^{\prime\prime}(R)>0
if there is a
1
-periodic orbit of
X_{h}
in
\Sigma\times\{R\}

(equivalently, if there is a Reeb orbit in
\Sigma
of period
h^{\prime}(R)
).

(3)

h^{\prime}(R)=\lambda_{i}

on
L_{i}
for
i=0,\ldots,r-1
.

(4)

h^{\prime}(R)=\lambda_{r}

for
R\geq R^{\prime\prime}_{r}
.

We also pick
h
to be
C^{2}
-small and Morse near
R=R_{0}
. The intuition here is that if
\Psi
were globally defined, then we would extend
h
to a Hamiltonian

h:B\to\mathbb{R}

which is
C^{2}
-small and Morse on
B^{\mathrm{in}}
, and which on the conical end
B^{\mathrm{out}}\cong\Sigma\times[R_{0},\infty)
becomes the function
h(R)
that depends only on the radial coordinate
R\in[R_{0},\infty)
.

We will choose
R_{i}^{\prime},H_{i}^{\prime},H_{i}^{\prime\prime},R_{i}^{\prime\prime}
so that the sublevel sets of the functions
H
and
\Phi
are “nested”:

\{H\leq H_{i}^{\prime\prime}\}\subset\{\Phi\leq R_{i}^{\prime\prime}\}\subset\{\Phi\leq R_{i+1}^{\prime}\}\subset\{H\leq H_{i+1}^{\prime}\}\subset\{H\leq H_{i+1}^{\prime\prime}\},

starting with
R_{0}^{\prime}=R_{0}
and
H_{0}^{\prime}=m,
choosing them so that
\{\Phi\leq R_{0}\}\subset\{H\leq m\}.
The nesting condition ensures that
H_{\lambda}=c(H)=\lambda_{i}H+\mathrm{constant}

in a region of
Y
that covers (via
\Psi
) the region in
B
where
R\in L_{i}
, so where
h(R)=\lambda_{i}R+\mathrm{constant}
.
That this nesting can be achieved follows from
\Phi
and
H
being proper. The precise choices are not so important, but we need to carry out this choice.

We fix constants
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and
\varepsilon_{i}>0
(we choose these in Lemma
3.12
), where
\varepsilon_{i}
is the width of
L_{i}
,

\varepsilon_{i}=\textrm{width}(L_{i})=R_{i+1}^{\prime}-R_{i}^{\prime\prime}.

Define
H_{0}^{\prime\prime}:=H_{0}^{\prime}+\varepsilon_{0}
and
R_{0}^{\prime\prime}:=\max\Phi(H^{-1}(H_{0}^{\prime\prime}))+\varepsilon_{0},
and for
i=1,\dots,r
:

\displaystyle R_{i}^{\prime}:=

\displaystyle R_{i-1}^{\prime\prime}+\varepsilon_{i-1}

\displaystyle H_{i}^{\prime}:=

\displaystyle\max H(\Phi^{-1}(R_{i}^{\prime}))+\textrm{(any non-negative constant)}

\displaystyle H_{i}^{\prime\prime}:=

\displaystyle H_{i}^{\prime}+\textrm{(any positive constant)}

\displaystyle R_{i}^{\prime\prime}:=

\displaystyle\max\Phi(H^{-1}(H_{i}^{\prime\prime}))+\textrm{(any non-negative constant)}.

Also a choice of value
H_{-i}
with
H_{i}^{\prime}<H_{-i}<H_{i}^{\prime\prime}
is involved, where (
40
) holds. The choices of the bracketed constants above are irrelevant, so we avoid further notation. One can choose the bracketed constants to be arbitrarily small, and thus make
N_{i}=[R_{i}^{\prime},R_{i}^{\prime\prime}]
arbitrarily small, while still being able to make
\varepsilon_{i}=\textrm{width}(L_{i})
arbitrarily large. Such a choice will begin to matter in Lemma
3.12
.

3.3.
The cut-off functions
\phi

We now choose a smooth cut-off function
\phi:[0,+\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that is zero for small
R
, grows on the “linear intervals”
L_{i}=[R_{i}^{\prime\prime},R_{i+1}^{\prime}]
, and is constant on the “non-linear intervals”
N_{i}=[R_{i}^{\prime},R_{i}^{\prime\prime}]
, in particular
\phi^{\prime}=0
outside of the
L_{i}
. In summary:

(1)

\phi=0
on
[0,R_{0}^{\prime\prime}]
.

(2)

\phi^{\prime}\geq 0
everywhere.

(3)

\phi^{\prime}=0
on
N_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,r-1
(so
\phi
is constant there).

For the purposes of constructing a filtration on
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
the choice of
\phi
on
R\geq R_{r}^{\prime}
is irrelevant, as Floer solutions do not enter the region above
R>R_{r}^{\prime}
by the maximum principle.
We emphasise that the choice of
\phi
depends on the construction of
H_{\lambda}
, or more precisely on the choices of
R_{i}^{\prime},R_{i}^{\prime\prime}
.
We let
\phi_{i}\geq 0
denote the amount by which
\phi
increases when crossing the interval
L_{i}
, so

\phi=\phi_{0}+\phi_{1}+\cdots+\phi_{i-1}\quad\textrm{on}\;N_{i}.

We will always assume
\phi_{0}>0
. Later, starting from Lemma
3.12
, it will be important to choose
\phi_{i}\geq 0
to be sufficiently large, and we will also make
\varepsilon_{i}=\textrm{width}(L_{i})
large and
\phi^{\prime}\geq 1
on a large subinterval of
L_{i}
.
But for now, for everything we carry out before Lemma
3.12
, one could just choose
\phi
to be constant on
R\geq R_{1}^{\prime}
(so
\phi_{i}=0
for
i\geq 1
). In particular, this simple choice of
\phi
works in
Section

3.7
.

3.4.
Filtration functional on
B

The cut-off function
\phi
defines the exact 2-form
\eta
from (
38
) on
B
(where
B
is abusive notation meaning
B^{\mathrm{out}}=\Sigma\times[R_{0},\infty)
when
\Psi
is not globally defined),
and an associated
1
-form
\Omega_{\eta}
on the free loop space
\mathcal{L}B=C^{\infty}(S^{1},B)
given by

(41)

\Omega_{\eta}:T_{x}\mathcal{L}B=C^{\infty}(S^{1},x^{*}TB)\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\ \ \xi\mapsto-{\int}\eta(\xi,\partial_{t}x-X_{h})\,dt.

Further, let
f:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
be the smooth function defined by

\textstyle f(R):=\int_{0}^{R}\phi^{\prime}(\tau)\,h^{\prime}(\tau)\,d\tau.

Define the
filtration functional

F:\mathcal{L}B\to\mathbb{R}
on the free loop space by

F(x):=-\int_{S^{1}}x^{*}(\phi\alpha)+\int_{S^{1}}f(R(x(t)))\,dt.

Lemma 3.2
.

[
MR23
, Thm.6.2(1)]

F
is a primitive of
\Omega_{\eta}.
That is,
dF(x)(\xi)=\Omega_{\eta}(x)(\xi).
∎

When
\Psi
is not globally defined, and we start with a loop
y\in\mathcal{L}Y
, then
x=\Psi\circ y
can be ill-defined (namely when some
y(t)\in Y^{\mathrm{in}}
), since
\Psi:\mathcal{L}Y\to\mathcal{L}B
is only defined when
y
is entirely contained in
Y^{\mathrm{out}}
. Nevertheless the partially defined pull-backs
F^{Y}=\Psi^{*}F
and
\Omega_{\eta}^{Y}=\Psi^{*}\Omega_{\eta}
can be extended naturally to
\Omega_{\eta}:T_{y}\mathcal{L}Y\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
F:\mathcal{L}Y\to\mathbb{R}
by

\Omega_{\eta}^{Y}(\xi)=-\int_{S^{1}}\Psi^{*}\eta(\xi,\partial_{t}y-X_{h\circ\Phi})\,dt,\quad F^{Y}(y):=-\int_{S^{1}}y^{*}(\Psi^{*}(\phi\alpha))+\int_{S^{1}}f(\Phi(y(t)))\,dt,

where
\Psi^{*}\eta
and
\Psi^{*}(\phi\alpha)
both extend by zero to
Y
(as
\phi=\phi^{\prime}=0
near
R=R_{0}
), and
\Phi=\Psi\circ R
on
Y^{\mathrm{out}}
extends to
Y
by making
\Phi
decay
32
32

32

We can extend
\Psi
above a collar neighbourhood
\Sigma\times(R_{0}-\epsilon,R_{0}]
of
\partial B^{\mathrm{out}}=\Sigma\times\{R_{0}\}
since
X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
is strictly outward pointing on
\Psi^{-1}(\partial B^{\mathrm{out}})
. Let
n:(R_{0}-\epsilon,R_{0}]\to[0,1]
be a non-decreasing cut-off function with
n=0
near
R_{0}-\epsilon
, and
n=1
near
R_{0}
. Then set
\Phi:=n(R\circ\Psi)
over the collar, and
\Phi=0
on the rest of
Y^{\mathrm{in}}.

to zero on
Y^{\mathrm{in}}
over a collar neighbourhood of
\partial B^{\mathrm{out}}
(the above data is independent of the choice of extension of
h(R)
to
R\leq R_{0}
as
\eta=0
there). The calculation
[
MR23
, Thm.6.2(1)]
of
dF-\Omega_{\eta}
in
3.2
relied on showing that the relevant integrands cancelled out
at each time

t
(not just after integrating over
t
). Those integrands all vanish when
R\circ x(t)
approaches the boundary
R=R_{0}
of
B^{\mathrm{out}}
since
\phi,\phi^{\prime},\eta
vanish for
R
close to
R_{0}
. The same holds for the integrands involved in
dF^{Y}-\Omega_{\eta}^{Y}
when
y(t)\in Y^{\mathrm{in}}
since we extended the data so that
\Psi^{*}\eta,\Psi^{*}(\phi\alpha),f(\Phi(y))
vanish for
y(t)\in Y^{\mathrm{in}}
.
Thus
F^{Y}
is a primitive of
\Omega_{\eta}^{Y}
on all of
\mathcal{L}Y
. A similar line of argument holds for all our calculations in the next sections: we will abusively write
y(t)=\Psi\circ x(t)
or
v(s,t)=\Psi\circ u(s,t)
even though
\Psi
is not globally defined, because at those values of
s,t
the terms we consider will involve factors of
\phi
or
\phi^{\prime}
, so those terms vanish for
R
close to
R_{0}
and they are extended by zero for
R\leq R_{0}
.

3.5.
The filtration inequality for
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})

For a 1-orbit
x
of
H_{\lambda},
we define by abuse of notation:

F(x):=F(\Psi(x)).

Theorem 3.3
.

The Floer chain complex
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
has a filtration given by the value of
F.
That is, given two
1
-periodic orbits
x_{-},x_{+}
of
H_{\lambda}
and a Floer cylinder for
(H_{\lambda},I)
from
x_{-}
to
x_{+},

(42)

F(x_{-})\geq F(x_{+}).

Proof.

The Floer cylinder
u:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow Y
for
H_{\lambda}
satisfies

\partial_{s}u+I(\partial_{t}u-X_{H_{\lambda}})=0.

Using
\Psi_{*}X_{S^{1}}=\mathcal{R}_{B}
, we get

(43)

\Psi_{*}(X_{H_{\lambda}})=\Psi_{*}(c^{\prime}(H)X_{H})=c^{\prime}(H)\Psi_{*}(X_{H})=c^{\prime}(H)\mathcal{R}_{B},

noting that
c^{\prime}(H)
depends on the original coordinates in
Y
.
Projecting
u
via
\Psi
defines a map

(44)

v:=\Psi\circ u:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow B,\ \ \ \partial_{s}v+I_{B}(\partial_{t}v-k(s,t)\mathcal{R}_{B})=0

that converges to
y_{-}=\Psi(x_{-})
,
y_{+}=\Psi(x_{+})
at
s=-\infty
,
+\infty,
respectively, where

k(s,t):=c^{\prime}(H(u(s,t)))

is a domain-dependent function.
Now define the
s
-parametrised 1-form

(45)

\Omega_{\eta}^{v}(s):T_{x}\mathcal{L}B=C^{\infty}(S^{1},x^{*}TB)\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\ \ \xi\mapsto-{\textstyle\int}\eta(\xi,\partial_{t}x-k(s,t)\mathcal{R}_{B})\,dt.

Using
(
38
),
d\alpha(\cdot,\mathcal{R}_{B})=0
,
dR(\mathcal{R}_{B})=0
and
\alpha(\mathcal{R}_{B})=1
:

(46)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\eta}^{v}(s)(x)(\xi)=

\displaystyle-\int\eta(\xi,\partial_{t}x)\ dt+\int k(s,t)\,\eta(\xi,\mathcal{R}_{B})\ dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\!\int\!\eta(\xi,\partial_{t}x)dt+\!\int\!k(s,t)\phi(R)d\alpha(\xi,\mathcal{R}_{B})dt+\!\int\!k(s,t)\,\phi^{\prime}(R)(dR\wedge\alpha)(\xi,\mathcal{R}_{B})dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int\eta(\xi,\partial_{t}x)\ dt+\int\phi^{\prime}(R)dR(\xi)k(s,t)\ dt.

Similarly, using that
X_{h}=h^{\prime}(R)\mathcal{R}_{B}
in the region where
\phi^{\prime}\neq 0
,

(47)

\Omega_{\eta}(x)(\xi)=-\int\eta(\xi,\partial_{t}x)\ dt+\int\phi^{\prime}(R)dR(\xi)h^{\prime}(R(x(t)))\ dt.

By the construction of
H_{\lambda}
and
h(R),
for
R(v(s,t))\in L_{i}
we have

(48)

k(s,t)=c^{\prime}(H(u(s,t)))=\lambda_{i}=h^{\prime}(R(v(s,t))),

whereas outside that region we have
\phi^{\prime}(R)=0.

Therefore for all
s,t
we have

(49)

\phi^{\prime}(R(v(s,t)))\ k(s,t)=\phi^{\prime}(R(v(s,t)))\ h^{\prime}(R(v(s,t))).

Combining (
46
) and (
47
) yields

\Omega_{\eta}(v(s,t))(\partial_{s}v)=\Omega_{\eta}^{v}(s)(v(s,t))(\partial_{s}v).

Combining this with Lemma
3.2
, we deduce that

(50)

\displaystyle F(x_{-})-F(x_{+})=F(y_{-})-F(y_{+})=

\displaystyle-\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}dF(v(s,t))(\partial_{s}v)ds

\displaystyle=-\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\Omega_{\eta}(v(s,t))(\partial_{s}v)ds=

\displaystyle-\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\Omega^{v}_{\eta}(s)(v(s,t))(\partial_{s}v)ds.

Hence, it is sufficient to prove that

\Omega^{v}_{\eta}(s)(v(s,t))(\partial_{s}v)\leq 0

holds for
v
satisfying equation (
44
). This reduces us to the same computation as in the convex setting
[
MR23
, Lem.6.1]
: we use the Floer equation
\partial_{t}v-k(s,t)\mathcal{R}_{B}=I_{B}\partial_{s}v
, and abbreviate
\rho=R\circ v
:

(51)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\eta(\partial_{s}v,\partial_{t}v-k(s,t)\mathcal{R}_{B})&=&\eta(\partial_{s}v,I_{B}\partial_{s}v)\\
&=&\phi(\rho)\cdot d\alpha(\partial_{s}v,I_{B}\partial_{s}v)+\phi^{\prime}(\rho)\cdot(dR\wedge\alpha)(\partial_{s}v,I_{B}\partial_{s}v)\\
&=&\textrm{positive}\cdot\textrm{positive}+\textrm{positive}\cdot(dR\wedge\alpha)(\partial_{s}v,I_{B}\partial_{s}v),\end{array}

where “positive” here means “non-negative”.
To estimate the last term, we may assume that
R\geq R_{0}^{\prime\prime}
since
\phi^{\prime}=0
otherwise. Thus,
we decompose
\partial_{s}v
according to an orthogonal decomposition of
TB
:

(52)

\partial_{s}v=C\oplus y\mathcal{R}_{B}\oplus zZ\in\xi\oplus\mathbb{R}\mathcal{R}_{B}\oplus\mathbb{R}Z,

where
Z=-I_{B}\mathcal{R}_{B}=R\partial_{R}
is the Liouville vector field and
\xi=\ker\alpha|_{R=1}
. Notice that
\ker\alpha=\xi\oplus\mathbb{R}Z.
Thus:
dR(\partial_{s}v)=Rz
and
\alpha(I_{B}\partial_{s}v)=\alpha(I_{B}zZ)=\alpha(z\mathcal{R}_{B})=z
. So,

(53)

(dR\wedge\alpha)(\partial_{s}v,I_{B}\partial_{s}v)=dR(\partial_{s}v)\alpha(I_{B}\partial_{s}v)-\alpha(\partial_{s}v)dR(I_{B}\partial_{s}v)=Rz^{2}+Ry^{2}\geq 0.

Hence
\Omega^{v}_{\eta}(s)(v(s,t))(\partial_{s}v)\leq 0,
thus
F(\Psi(x_{-}))-F(\Psi(x_{+}))\geq 0.

∎

3.6.
The
F
-filtration values on
1
-orbits

Corollary 3.4
.

The
F
-filtration values satisfy the following properties:

(1)

F=0
at the constant orbits, so at each point of
\mathfrak{F}=\sqcup_{\alpha}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
;

(2)

F(y)=F(\Psi(x))<0
for every non-constant
1
-periodic orbit
x
of
H_{\lambda}
;

(3)

F(y)
only depends on the Reeb period
T_{-i}=c^{\prime}(H(x))
of the projected orbit
y
, see (
54
);

(4)

for non-constant orbits,
F(y)
decreases as
H(y)
increases;

(5)

on non-constant orbits, the
F
-filtration is equivalent to filtering by
-H
, or equivalently: filtering by negative
S^{1}
-period values
-c^{\prime}(H)=-T_{-i}
.

Proof.

Let us calculate the value of the functional
F(y)
explicitly for the projection
y:=\Psi(x(t))
of a 1-periodic orbit
x
of
H_{\lambda}.
If
x
is a fixed point,
y
lies in the region where
\phi=0
so
F(y)=0.
Otherwise,
c^{\prime}(H(x))=T_{-i}
for some
0<i\leq r.
Thus
y
is a 1-periodic orbit in
B
of the Hamiltonian
T_{-i}R,
so
R\in N_{i}
lies between
L_{i-1}
and
L_{i}
, thus
\phi(y)=\phi_{0}+\cdots+\phi_{i-1}
. Using that
\phi^{\prime}=0
except possibly on the intervals
L_{k}
where
h^{\prime}(R)=\lambda_{k}
, we deduce:

(54)

\displaystyle\begin{split}F(y(t))=&-\int_{S^{1}}y^{*}(\phi\alpha)+\int_{S^{1}}f(R(y(t)))dt\\
=&-\phi(y)T_{-i}+\int_{L_{0}}\phi^{\prime}(R)h^{\prime}(R)dR+\cdots+\int_{L_{i-1}}\phi^{\prime}(R)h^{\prime}(R)dR\\
=&-(\phi_{0}+\cdots+\phi_{i-1})T_{-i}+\phi_{0}\lambda_{0}+\cdots+\phi_{i-1}\lambda_{i-1},\end{split}

which is negative as
T_{-i}
is strictly larger than
\lambda_{0},\ldots,\lambda_{i-1}
, and
\phi_{0}>0
. The drop in filtration value for
1-
orbits
y_{-i},y_{-(i+1)}
arising for successive slopes
T_{-i}<T_{-(i+1)}
is:

(55)

F(y_{-i})-F(y_{-(i+1)})=(\phi_{0}+\cdots+\phi_{i-1})(T_{-(i+1)}-T_{-i})+\phi_{i}(T_{-(i+1)}-\lambda_{i})>0.

Thus
F(x(t))<0
strictly decreases when
c^{\prime}(H(x))=T_{-i}
, hence
H(x),
increases.
∎

3.7.
Dependence of the filtration on
\phi
and
H_{\lambda}

Our convention is that
x_{-}
appears in the output of the chain differential
\partial(x_{+})
if a Floer trajectory
u
flows from
x_{-}
to
x_{+}
. As
F(x_{-})\geq F(x_{+})
,
this means
\partial
“increases the
F
-filtration”, so it decreases
H
, and decreases the period
c^{\prime}(H_{-i})=T_{-i}
. Thus, restricting
1-
orbits by the condition
F\geq A
defines a subcomplex,
CF^{*}_{[A,\infty)]}(H_{\lambda})
.
So the filtration separates the Floer chain complex into a finite collection of distinct nested subcomplexes

CF^{*}_{[0,\infty)}(H_{\lambda})\subset CF^{*}_{[-a_{1},\infty)}(H_{\lambda})\subset CF^{*}_{[-a_{2},\infty)}(H_{\lambda})\subset\cdots\subset CF^{*}(H_{\lambda}),

where
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots\in(0,\infty)
are increasing.
The number of these subcomplexes increases with
\lambda
. The
[0,\infty)
-piece corresponds to the subcomplex generated by constant orbits at
\mathfrak{F}
; for those
F=0
.
By
3.4
, these lists of distinct subcomplexes (after forgetting the labelling by
F
-values
a_{i}
) do not depend on the choice of
\phi
, since for non-constant
1
-orbits we can instead label the subcomplexes by
(-H)
-values, or by negative
S^{1}
-period values
-c^{\prime}(H)=-T_{-i}
.

Definition 3.5
.

When we speak of the
filtration
on the Floer chain complex we refer to the above filtration by distinct subcomplexes, without labelling the steps of the filtration by
F
-values. So when we say that a chain map is
filtration preserving
it means that a
1
-orbit with associated period value
p
is sent to a
\mathbb{K}
-linear combination of
1
-orbits whose associated period values are each at most
p
(thus avoiding the issue that
F
depends on
h,\phi
which partly depend on
H_{\lambda}
).

When we wish to prove that certain maps counting Floer solutions
u
respect the filtration, it will be important to obtain an inequality of the type
\partial_{s}(F\circ v)\leq 0
where
v=\Psi\circ u
, as we did for Floer cylinders in
3.3
. For Floer continuation solutions this is problematic since when homotoping by
H_{s}
from
H_{\lambda^{\prime}}
to
H_{\lambda}
, we need to decide how to pick
\phi,h
when defining an
s
-dependent filtration
F_{s}
. Making
\phi

s-
dependent causes problematic terms in
\partial_{s}F_{s}
. In
[
MR23
, Sec.6.5]
it is shown that in a convex symplectic manifold
B
, Floer continuation solutions
v
for a homotopy
h_{s}
of the Hamiltonians still satisfy
\partial_{s}(F_{s}\circ v)\leq 0
when using an
s
-dependent
f_{s}(R)=\int_{0}^{R}\phi^{\prime}(\tau)h_{s}^{\prime}(\tau)\,d\tau
in the definition of the filtration
F=F_{s}
, provided that

\partial_{s}h_{s}^{\prime}\leq 0\textrm{ where }\phi^{\prime}\neq 0.

Lemma 3.6
.

Floer continuation maps
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\to CF^{*}(H_{\lambda^{\prime}})
for
\lambda\leq\lambda^{\prime}
respect the filtration if the homotopy
H_{s}=c_{s}\circ H
of the Hamiltonians is
s
-independent in the region
\phi^{\prime}\neq 0
.

More generally, this holds if the linearity intervals
L_{i}

which are used in the construction of
\phi

are such that
H_{\lambda}
,
H_{\lambda^{\prime}}
and
H_{s}
are linear in
H
over
L_{i}
and
the slope
c_{s}^{\prime}=\lambda_{i,s}
on
L_{i}
satisfies
\partial_{s}\lambda_{i,s}\leq 0
.

Proof.

Under the assumption, (
49
) holds also for projections of continuation solutions, so the filtration is non-increasing along
v
(and the output
x_{-}
of the continuation operator at the chain level has filtration value at least that of
x_{+}
). For the more general assumption, abbreviating
\rho=R(v(s,t))
, (
49
) becomes
\phi^{\prime}(\rho)k(s,t)=\phi^{\prime}(\rho)\lambda_{i,s}=\phi^{\prime}(\rho)h_{s}^{\prime}(\rho)
where
h_{s}
is built to satisfy
h_{s}^{\prime}(R)=\lambda_{i,s}
for
R\in L_{i}
,
so that
\partial_{s}h_{s}^{\prime}\leq 0
for
R\geq R_{0}^{\prime}=R_{0}
ensuring that the argument in
[
MR23
, Sec.6.5]
applies.
∎

Definition 3.7
.

A homotopy
H_{s}
of Hamiltonians is called
monotone
if
\partial_{s}H_{s}\leq 0
. This ensures that the weight
t^{E_{0}(u)}
with which Floer continuation solutions
u
are counted involves a non-negative quantity
E_{0}(u)\geq 0
related to the energy of
u
by
E(u)=E_{0}(u)+\int_{\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}}\partial_{s}H_{s}\,ds\wedge dt
(e.g. see
[
Rit13
, Sec.3.3]
), so
E_{0}(u)\geq E(u)\geq 0
. Call
H_{s}
an
admissible homotopy
if
H_{s}=\lambda_{s}H+\textrm{constant}(s)
at infinity (the slopes
\lambda_{s}
need not be generic).

Corollary 3.8
.

Given any admissible
H_{\lambda}
,
H_{\lambda^{\prime}}
with
\lambda^{\prime}\geq\lambda
, it is always possible to construct the continuation map
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\to CF^{*}(H_{\lambda^{\prime}})
in a way that preserves the filtration, in the sense of
3.5
. This can be achieved by composing a filtration-preserving admissible monotone continuation map
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\to CF^{*}(H_{\lambda^{\prime}}+C)
for some large enough constant
C>0
, with the natural filtration-preserving identification
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda^{\prime}}+C)=CF^{*}(H_{\lambda^{\prime}})
.

Proof.

The statement
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda^{\prime}}+C)=CF^{*}(H_{\lambda^{\prime}})
holds because adding a constant to a Hamiltonian does not change the Hamiltonian vector field, so the Floer theory and the filtration values do not change (we do not need to change
h
; as
F
only depends on
h^{\prime}
, changing
h
to
h+C
does not affect
F
). By picking
C=\max(H_{\lambda}-H_{\lambda^{\prime}})
(finite since
\lambda^{\prime}\geq\lambda
) we may assume
H_{\lambda^{\prime}}\geq H_{\lambda}
everywhere.

For this proof, we will use
\phi
with
\phi^{\prime}=0
except over
L_{0}
(so
\phi_{0}>0
but
\phi_{i}=0
for
i>0
).
If
H_{\lambda},H_{\lambda^{\prime}}
are linear with the same slope
\lambda_{0}
over the linearity interval
L_{0}
, then any admissible monotone homotopy
H_{s}
from
H_{\lambda^{\prime}}
to
H_{\lambda}
which preserves the slope
\lambda_{0}
over
L_{0}
satisfies the claim by
3.6
.

To reduce to the above case, we perform some admissible monotone homotopies of Hamiltonians which are supported in a compact region, so their Floer continuation maps induce quasi-isomorphisms (and we ensure they preserve the filtration in the sense of
3.5
).
Call
L_{0}
,
L_{0}^{\prime}
the respective linearity intervals of the two Hamiltonians, for slopes
\lambda_{0}
,
\lambda_{0}^{\prime}
.
If
\lambda_{0}>\lambda_{0}^{\prime}
, we can first use a monotone admissible homotopy of
H_{\lambda}
supported near
R\leq R_{1}^{\prime}
which is slope-decreasing on
L_{0}
, so that
3.6
applies.
We can also do a monotone admissible homotopy of
H_{\lambda}
supported near
R\leq R_{1}^{\prime}
to increase the size of
L_{0}
to the left (i.e. decreasing
R_{0}^{\prime\prime}
).

For
H_{\lambda^{\prime}}
we use a different
\phi
to define the filtration
F
, namely one with
\phi^{\prime}=0
except over
L_{0}^{\prime}
. By the same argument as above, if
\lambda_{0}^{\prime}>\lambda_{0}
we can homotope
\lambda_{0}^{\prime}
down to
\lambda_{0}
, and we can extend
L_{0}^{\prime}
to the left. That these two filtration-preserving continuation maps were built using a different
F
does not matter in view of
3.4
and
3.5
.

We may now assume that
H_{\lambda^{\prime}},H_{\lambda}
have the same slope over
L_{0}\cap L_{0}^{\prime}
and that this overlap contains a non-trivial interval. By redefining
L_{0}
to be that interval we have reduced to the initial case.
∎

Definition 3.9
.

Abbreviate
CF_{0}^{*}(H_{\lambda}):=CF^{*}_{[0,\infty)}(H_{\lambda})\subset CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
the subcomplex generated by the fixed locus
\mathfrak{F}=\sqcup_{\alpha}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
(constant orbits have filtration
F=0
).
The
positive Floer cohomology

HF_{+}^{*}(H_{\lambda})=H_{*}(CF_{+}(H_{\lambda}))
is the homology of the
quotient complex
CF_{+}(H_{\lambda}):=CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})/CF_{0}^{*}(H_{\lambda})
.

Now pick a cut-off function
\phi
which is constant for
R\geq R_{1}^{\prime}
, and consider those
H_{\lambda}
of
Section

3.2
which are linear in
H
of slope
\lambda_{0}
on
[H_{0}^{\prime\prime},H_{1}^{\prime}]
(which covers via
R\circ\Psi
the interval
L_{0}=[R_{0}^{\prime\prime},R_{1}^{\prime}]
where
\phi^{\prime}\neq 0
). Then
3.6
yields filtration-preserving slope-increasing continuation maps, so the direct limit of the
HF^{*}_{+}(H_{\lambda})
is well-defined and is called
positive symplectic cohomology
,

SH_{+}(Y,\varphi,\omega):=\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow\infty}HF_{+}^{*}(H_{\lambda}).

Up to a filtration-preserving isomorphism,
SH_{+}(Y,\varphi,\omega)
does not depend on the choices of
\lambda_{0},H_{0}^{\prime\prime},H_{1}^{\prime}
by
3.8
and
3.5
.
We deduce the following (see
[
RŽ23
]
for details).

Proposition 3.10
.

For any symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold over a convex base,
there is a long exact sequence

\cdots\to QH^{*}(Y,I)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}SH^{*}(Y,\varphi,\omega)\to SH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi,\omega)\to QH^{*+1}(Y,I)\to\cdots

3.8.
Description of Floer solutions for which the filtration value remains constant

Lemma 3.11
.

If at least one among
x_{-},x_{+}
is non-constant, and
F(x_{-})=F(x_{+})
, then

•

H(x_{-})=H(x_{+})
, and both
x_{\pm}
are non-constant;

•

x_{\pm}
correspond to orbits of the
S^{1}
-flow of equal period
T:=c^{\prime}(H(x_{-}))=c^{\prime}(H(x_{+}))
;

•

v=\Psi(u)
lies entirely in a region where
\phi^{\prime}=0
;

•

y_{\pm}=\Psi(x_{\pm})
correspond to Reeb orbits of period
T
(but may not have the same
R
-value);

•

\mathrm{Im}(v)\subset\mathbb{C}^{*}\cdot p
lies in the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-orbit of some point
p\in B
;

•

\mathrm{Im}(v)
lies in some
m
-torsion submanifold of
B
(
2.5
), for
k,m
coprime,
T=\tfrac{k}{m}\in\mathbb{Q}
;

•

\mathrm{Im}(du)\subset\mathbb{C}\cdot X_{S^{1}}\oplus\ker\Psi_{*}
where
\mathbb{C}\cdot X_{S^{1}}=\mathbb{R}\cdot X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\oplus\mathbb{R}\cdot X_{S^{1}}
.

Proof.

As one among
x_{-},x_{+}
is non-constant,
v
does not lie entirely in the region where
\phi=0
. As (
42
) is not strict, we must have
C=0
in (
52
) otherwise the first positive term
in (
51
) is strictly positive. It follows that
\mathrm{Im}(v)\cap B^{\mathrm{out}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{*}\cdot p
lies in the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-orbit of
p=y_{-}(0)\in B
.
Also,
v
cannot enter the region
\phi^{\prime}\neq 0
, otherwise the last term in (
51
) becomes strictly
33
33

33

If (
53
)
\,\equiv 0
then
\partial_{s}v\equiv 0
, so
y_{\pm}(t)=v(s,t)
is
s
-independent, so
u
lies in
\Psi^{-1}(y_{\pm})=\Psi^{-1}(\Psi(x_{\pm}))
and there
\phi^{\prime}=0
.

positive by (
53
), so
v
stays in a connected subset where
\phi
is constant.
By
3.4
, the condition
F(x_{-})=F(x_{+})
implies
c^{\prime}(H(x_{-}))=c^{\prime}(H(x_{+}))
is the same period
T:=T_{-i}
say, and thus
H(x_{-})=H(x_{+})
by the construction of
c
. Thus
y_{\pm}
are
1
-periodic
T\cdot\mathcal{R}_{B}
-orbits.
As
v\subset\mathbb{C}^{*}\cdot p
, it must lie in the same connected component of torsion points as
p
.
∎

3.9.
Floer solutions “consume filtration” when crossing linearity regions

So far we have not needed to specify the choices of constants
\phi_{i}
and
\varepsilon_{i}
in the construction of
\phi
and
L_{i}
. We now show that one can choose the
\phi_{i}
inductively so that Floer solutions must consume an a priori lower bound of filtration-energy when crossing the “linearity” region above
L_{i}
(so when
R\circ\Psi\in L_{i}
). The reason we wish to have this result, is that if we modify
H_{\lambda}
at infinity by increasing the slope to obtain
H_{\lambda^{\prime}}
, we would like
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
to be a subcomplex of
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda^{\prime}})
. For convex symplectic manifolds this essentially follows from the maximum principle for radial Hamiltonians, but in our setup the maximum principle only holds over the linearity regions, so we instead resort to a filtration argument. This argument will also be needed to ensure that certain spectral sequences are compatible with increasing the slope of the Hamiltonian, so that one can take a direct limit of the spectral sequences.

We refine the choices of
\varepsilon_{i}
,
N_{i}
, so that now
\phi
belongs to the class of cut-off functions satisfying:

(4)

\phi\geq R
for
R\geq R_{1}^{\prime}
.

(5)

\phi^{\prime}\geq 1
on a subinterval of
L_{i}
of width at least
\varepsilon_{i}/2
.

This can be achieved by choosing
N_{0},\ldots,N_{i}
to be small,
\varepsilon_{i}=\textrm{width}(L_{i})
to be large, and by making
\phi^{\prime}
very large on a large subinterval of
L_{i}
, in particular making
\phi_{i}
large enough so that the constant value of
\phi
on
N_{i+1}
is larger than
R_{i+1}^{\prime\prime}=\max_{N_{i+1}}R
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Given
\delta_{i}>0
, and any choice of
\phi
defined on
R\leq R_{i}^{\prime\prime}
within the above class, there is a way to extend
\phi
within that class over
L_{i}=[R_{i}^{\prime\prime},R_{i+1}^{\prime}]
so that any Floer trajectory
u:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\to Y
from
x_{-}
to
x_{+}
for
H_{\lambda}
which crosses the region above
L_{i}
satisfies
F(x_{-})-F(x_{+})>\delta_{i}
.

More precisely, if there are values
s_{0}<s_{1}\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
for which the loops
u(s_{0},\cdot)
and
u(s_{1},\cdot)
project via
R\circ\Psi
to different components of
\mathbb{R}\setminus L_{i}
,
then

(56)

F(x_{-})-F(x_{+})\geq F(u(s_{0},\cdot))-F(u(s_{1},\cdot))>\delta_{i}.

Proof.

As (
51
) is pointwise non-negative, it suffices that we bound the integral

\int\eta(\partial_{s}v,\partial_{t}v-\lambda_{i}\mathcal{R}_{B})

from below by
\delta_{i}
over the subset of all
(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
for which
R(v(s,t))
lies in the subinterval of
L_{i}
where we ensured that
\phi^{\prime}\geq 1
(and
h^{\prime}=\lambda_{i}
). For such
(s,t)
, using

\phi\geq R
on
R\geq R_{1}^{\prime}
,

\eta(\partial_{s}v,\partial_{t}v-\lambda_{i}\mathcal{R}_{B})\geq R\cdot d\alpha(\partial_{s}v,I_{B}\partial_{s}v)+(dR\wedge\alpha)(\partial_{s}v,I_{B}\partial_{s}v)=d(R\alpha)(\partial_{s}v,\partial_{t}v-\lambda_{i}\mathcal{R}_{B}).

The latter expression is precisely the integrand of the energy
\int\|\partial_{s}v\|^{2}\,ds\,dt
for a Floer solution
v
in
B
for the Hamiltonian
\lambda_{i}R
. So the problem reduces to showing that a Floer solution in
B
for a radial Hamiltonian consumes a lot of energy if it crosses a long radial stretch in which the slope of the Hamiltonian is constant, as we can choose the width
\varepsilon_{i}=\textrm{width}(L_{i})
to be arbitrarily large. This is a standard “monotonicity lemma argument”: one uses Gromov’s trick of turning a Floer solution in a symplectic manifold
M
into a pseudoholomorphic section of a bundle with fibre
M
(e.g. see
[
Rit14
, Sec.5.3]
). One then applies the monotonicity lemma to obtain lower bounds on the energy (see
[
Rit14
, Lem.33]
). This is the step that uses the assumption in
Section

2.1
that
(B,I_{B})
is geometrically bounded at infinity, and the observation that in the auxiliary almost complex structure in Gromov’s trick
[
Rit14
, Def.26]
the off-diagonal terms involve
\lambda_{i}\mathcal{R}_{B}
and
\lambda_{i}Z_{B}
which are radially invariant.
∎

Remark 3.13
.

Lemma
3.12
also holds for Floer continuation solutions for homotopies
H_{s}
as in Lemma
3.6
. In the proof, on the interval
L_{i}
we have
h_{s}^{\prime}=\lambda_{i,s}
, and the condition
\partial_{s}\lambda_{i,s}\leq 0
ensures that the monotonicity argument still applies
(see the discussion of monotone homotopies in
[
Rit14
, Sec.5.3]
).

3.10.
Cofinal family of Hamiltonians
H_{\lambda_{i}}
, and a filtration on
SH^{*}

To compute
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
it suffices to take a direct limit over a cofinal family of Hamiltonians
H_{\lambda_{i}}
, meaning
\lambda_{i}\to\infty
as
i\to\infty
. Such
H_{\lambda_{i}}
can be constructed so that the Floer continuation maps are particularly well-behaved.

Corollary 3.14
.

There is a cofinal sequence of Hamiltonians
H_{\lambda_{i}}
and a cut-off function
\phi
, such that
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{i}})\subset CF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{i+1}})
is a subcomplex. There are filtration-preserving Floer continuation maps
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{i}})\to CF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{j}})
for
i<j
which equal the inclusion maps of subcomplexes.
The union

SC^{*}(Y,\varphi):=\cup\,CF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{i}})

of these nested complexes is a filtered complex, and
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
is (isomorphic to) its cohomology.
Analogously
SC^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi):=\cup\,CF^{*}_{+}(H_{\lambda_{i}})
is a filtered complex with cohomology
SH^{*}_{+}(Y,\varphi)
.

Proof.

We build
H_{\lambda_{i}}
inductively as in
Section

3.2
so that
H_{\lambda_{i}}=\lambda_{i}H+\mathrm{constant}
for
H\geq H_{i}^{\prime\prime}
, and
H_{\lambda_{i+1}}
is obtained from
H_{\lambda_{i}}
by increasing its (generic) slope at infinity to
\lambda_{i+1}>\lambda_{i}
.
Inductively, we ensure that
H_{\lambda_{i+1}}=H_{\lambda_{i}}
for
H\leq H_{i}^{\prime\prime}
. The construction of
H_{\lambda_{i+1}}
involves a choice of new parameters
H_{i+1}^{\prime},H_{i+1}^{\prime\prime},R_{i+1}^{\prime},R_{i+1}^{\prime\prime}
in
Section

3.2
, in particular a choice of width
\varepsilon_{i}=\mathrm{width}(L_{i})
.
We build the Floer continuation map by considering a homotopy
H_{s}
from
H_{\lambda_{i+1}}
to
H_{\lambda_{i}}
that decreases the slope of
H_{\lambda_{i+1}}
back down to
\lambda_{i}
on the region
H\geq H_{i}^{\prime\prime}
, in particular over
L_{i}
we ensure that
H_{s}=\lambda_{s}H+\mathrm{constant}(s)
with
\partial_{s}\lambda_{s}\leq 0
.
The continuation map then respects the filtration, by Lemma
3.6
.
If the continuation map were not the inclusion, there would exist a Floer continuation solution
u
from a
1
-orbit
x_{-}\in CF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{i+1}})
with
S^{1}
-period value
T_{-(i+1)}
, to a
1
-orbit

x_{+}\in CF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{i}})
; we need to prevent that.
Such a Floer solution
u
crosses the linearity region
L_{i}
in the construction of
H_{\lambda_{i+1}}
. By
3.13
, picking
\delta_{i}
to be larger than the possible values
F(x_{-})-F(x_{+})
for
1
-orbits
x_{-}
of
H_{\lambda_{i+1}}
, with
S^{1}
-period value
T_{-(i+1)}
, and
1
-orbits
x_{+}
for
H_{\lambda_{i}}
, a Floer continuation solution that crosses the region over
L_{i}
would contradict inequality (
56
).
So, such Floer continuation solutions are trapped in the region
H\leq H_{i}^{\prime\prime}
where
H_{s}
is
s
-independent. But such Floer continuation solutions are constant as they cannot be isolated, due to the
\mathbb{R}
-reparametrisation in
s
. Thus
u
does not cross
L_{i}
after all. ∎

Remark 3.15
.

The previous proof cannot simply be achieved by a maximum principle, since the Floer continuation solution may achieve a maximum
(R\circ\Psi)
-value in the region where
c^{\prime}
is not locally constant, in which case the maximum principle for the
\Psi
-projection to
B
is not known.

4.
Transversality for Floer solutions

4.1.
Overview of the technical difficulty

As mentioned in
Section

2.3
, transversality for Floer solutions can be achieved by the standard methods of
[
FHS95
,
SZ92
]
for
Y
, by
C^{\infty}
-small perturbations
I+\varepsilon
of
I
(where
\varepsilon
is an operator of a small norm, such that
I+\varepsilon
is an
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure). However, such a perturbation will in general ruin the holomorphicity of the map
\Psi
, and the projection
v=\Psi\circ u
of a Floer solution
u\subset Y
would only satisfy an equation in
B
of type

\partial_{t}v-k(s,t)X_{S^{1},B}=I_{B}\partial_{s}v+w(s,t)

where
w(s,t)=\Psi_{*}\circ\varepsilon_{u(s,t)}\circ\partial_{s}u
.
This may ruin the estimate in
Equation

51
due to the term
d\alpha(\partial_{s}v,w(s,t))
. Even in a region where
\phi^{\prime}\neq 0
, so where
\eta
is symplectic, the term
\eta(\partial_{s}v,I_{B}\partial_{s}v)\geq 0
may be too small to off-set a negative term
d\alpha(\partial_{s}v,w(s,t))
. Specifically, a large
\|\partial_{s}u\|
has small
\|\partial_{s}v\|
when
\partial_{s}u
lies close to
\ker\Psi_{*}
, so
\|w\|
cannot a priori be bounded by
\|\partial_{s}v\|
by making
\|\varepsilon\|
small, unless
\varepsilon
preserves
\ker\Psi_{*}
. Once we demand that
\varepsilon
preserves
\ker\Psi_{*}
we run into two problems. Firstly,
\ker\Psi_{*}
is not a locally trivial fibration, e.g.
\dim\ker\Psi_{*}|_{p}
is only known to be upper semi-continuous in
p\in Y
, so it is not always possible to extend a vector
v\in\ker\Psi_{*}|_{p}
to a local section of
\ker\Psi_{*}
. Secondly, the standard construction of the perturbation of
I
in
[
SZ92
, pp.1345-1346]
and
[
FHS95
, p.268]
seems to run into problems when forcing
\varepsilon
to preserve
\ker\Psi_{*}
, for instance if the adjoint
L^{2}
-section along
u
in that argument were entirely contained in
\ker\Psi_{*}\subset TY
.

We will resolve these issues in an indirect way, by using a Gromov compactness argument combined with the use of a construction similar to the filtered Floer homology introduced by Ono
[
Ono95
, Sec.3]
.
Consider what happens to a sequence of Floer solutions
u=u_{\varepsilon}
of
(H_{\lambda},I+\varepsilon)
for a sequence of perturbations
\varepsilon\to 0
converging to zero. Let us assume for simplicity that the Hamiltonian
1
-orbits
x_{\pm}
at the ends of
u_{\varepsilon}
are fixed.
If we knew that the
u_{\varepsilon}
converged, in the sense of Gromov compactness, to a broken Floer solution for
(H_{\lambda},I)
, then we would deduce that the filtration satisfies the correct inequality
F(x_{-})\geq F(x_{+})
by
3.3
.
Gromov compactness applies to Floer solutions with bounded energy
so, as explained in
Section

4.2
,
one achieves the correct filtration inequality for all Floer solutions
u
of
(H_{\lambda},I+\varepsilon)
of energy
E(u)\leq E_{0}

for any given perturbation
\varepsilon
that is sufficiently small. However, how small
\varepsilon
needs to be will a priori depend on the choice of energy bound
E_{0}
, so it does not rule out the possibility of a sequence of Floer solutions
u_{E_{0}}
of energy
E(u_{E_{0}})>E_{0}
that defy the filtration inequality, and which do not have a subsequence with a meaningful Gromov limit.
We construct a filtered version of the Floer chain complex, which artificially creates the necessary energy bounds so that Floer cohomology is obtained by a limiting procedure from Floer cohomology groups that only count Floer trajectories below a certain energy bound.

4.2.
Gromov compactness when the energy is bounded

From now on, we continue with the assumptions on
Y
from the start of
Section

3
.
By the maximum principle from
[
RŽ23
]
, Floer solutions for
(H_{\lambda},I)
remain in a compact region
K\subset Y
, so regularity of Floer solutions can be achieved by perturbing
I
in
K
. We want to do this perturbation compatibly with the filtration:

Lemma 4.1
.

Given an admissible Hamiltonian
H_{\lambda}
, a
constant
E_{0}>0
, and a compact region
K\subset Y
, any sufficiently
C^{\infty}
-small perturbation
I^{\prime}
supported on
K
of the
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
I
satisfies the filtration inequality (
42
),
F(x_{-})\geq F(x_{+})
, for all Floer trajectories
u
of
(H_{\lambda},I^{\prime})
of energy
E(u)\leq E_{0}
.

If
K
contains all
1
-periodic orbits of
H_{\lambda}
in its interior, then any generic perturbation
I^{\prime}
will also satisfy transversality for all Floer solutions.

Moreover, there is a
\delta_{E}>0
such that for any
s
-dependent perturbation
I_{s}^{\prime}
(compactly supported in
s
) homotoping between two given perturbations
I^{\prime}_{-},I^{\prime}_{+}
, with
\|I_{s}-I\|<\delta_{E}
, all Floer continuation solutions for
(H_{\lambda},I_{s})
of energy at most
E
will satisfy the filtration inequality.

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction that this fails.
Then there is a sequence of
\omega
-compatible almost complex structures
I_{n}
agreeing with
I
outside of the compact subset
K
, and a sequence
u_{n}
of Floer trajectories for
(H_{\lambda},I_{n})
, with energy
E(u_{n})\leq E_{0}
, with
F(x_{n,-})<F(x_{n,+})
(here
x_{n,\pm}
are the
1
-orbits of
H_{\lambda}
to which
u_{n}
exponentially converges by virtue of having finite energy).
As there are finitely many filtration values for the
1
-orbits of
H_{\lambda}
, we deduce that
F(x_{n,-})-F(x_{n,+})\leq e_{0}
for some
e_{0}<0
.
As the initial points
x_{n,\pm}(0)
of these orbits lie in a compact subset of
Y
, by passing to a subsequence we may assume that
x_{n,\pm}\to x_{\pm}
converge to
1
-periodic orbits
x_{\pm}
of
H_{\lambda}
.
By Gromov compactness, the Floer trajectories
u_{n}
converge in
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}
to a broken Floer trajectory
u
of
(H_{\lambda},I)
with ends
x_{\pm}
, possibly with finitely many
I
-holomorphic spheres attached, and with
E(u)\leq E_{0}
. In particular,
F(x_{n,-})-F(x_{n,+})\leq e_{0}<0
converges to the inequality
F(x_{-})-F(x_{+})\leq e_{0}<0
which contradicts
Equation

42
,
F(x_{-})\geq F(x_{+})
, for the unperturbed Floer solution
u
of
(H_{\lambda},I)
.

The second claim is a consequence of the standard transversality argument
[
SZ92
,
FHS95
]
, independently of the issue of filtrations.

The final claim is proved analogously: now
u_{n}
are continuation solutions for
(H_{\lambda},I_{s,n})
with
I_{s,n}\to I
as
n\to\infty
, but the Gromov limit
u
is still a broken bubbled trajectory
u
of
(H_{\lambda},I)
with
I
-bubbles.
∎

4.3.
Energy-bracketed Floer cohomology

The energy bound
E_{0}
is essential in
4.1
. A priori, the size of the perturbation
I
needed to achieve the filtration estimate may decay to zero as we let
E_{0}
grow. There is the danger of arbitrarily high-energy conspiring Floer solutions which “disappear” in the limit, meaning: there may exist Floer solutions
u_{n}
of
(H_{\lambda},I_{n})
, with
E(u_{n})\to\infty
and
F(x_{n,-})-F(x_{n,+})<0
, which fail to have a subsequence admitting a Gromov limit.
We circumvent this issue by imposing an artificial bound on the energy by the following algebraic trick. The construction we carry out is essentially that of ‘‘filtered Floer cohomology’’ introduced by Ono
34
34

34

Ono’s paper uses capped
1
-periodic orbits, and works with the Floer action functional on a cover of the free loop space. We could have done the same (for a non-contractible
1
-orbit
x:S^{1}\to Y
, the analogue of a capping is then a choice of homotopy between
x
and a chosen representative loop in the free homotopy class
[x]\in[S^{1},Y]
). We opted for bypassing these matters since we are using a simpler less refined version of the Novikov ring compared to
[
Ono95
]
.
in
[
Ono95
, Sec.3]
.

Initially, let us ignore the almost complex structure.
To simplify the discussion, we assume that we use the Novikov field
\mathbb{K}
from
Equation

31
(e.g. when
c_{1}(Y)=0
).
Define a modified Novikov ring

\mathbb{K}_{E_{0}}=\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}/T^{E_{0}}\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}

where
\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}=\{\sum n_{j}T^{a_{j}}:a_{j}\geq 0\in\mathbb{R},a_{j}\to\infty,n_{j}\in\mathbb{B}\}
. The Floer complex

C^{*}_{E_{0}}:=CF^{*}(H_{\lambda},\mathbb{K}_{E_{0}})

is the
\mathbb{K}_{E_{0}}
-module generated by certain
1
-orbits of
H_{\lambda}
(if one uses a cascade model, the auxiliary Morse functions chosen on the Morse–Bott manifolds of
1
-orbits select a subset of
1
-orbits, see
Appendix

A
). The Floer differential acts as a matrix with
\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}
entries, since the Novikov weights
T^{E(u)}
in the counts involve non-negative energies
E(u)\geq 0
. The matrix is therefore well-defined
also over
\mathbb{K}_{E_{0}}
(and still squares to zero), which is tantamount
35
35

35

Indeed even in the proof that the differential squares to zero we ignore higher energy solutions, because the proof involves looking at broken solutions arising as Gromov limits when compatifying
1
-dimensional moduli spaces of Floer solutions of a specified energy value, and the (non-negative) energy of pieces of the broken solution adds up to that value.

to ignoring Floer trajectories of energy greater than
E_{0}
. The natural quotient map
\mathbb{K}_{E_{0}}\leftarrow\mathbb{K}_{E_{1}}
for
E_{0}\leq E_{1}
induces a natural chain map

(57)

C_{E_{0}}^{*}\leftarrow C_{E_{1}}^{*}.

The inverse limit
C^{*}:=\varprojlim C^{*}_{E}
over these maps as
E\to\infty
can be identified with
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda},\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0})
.
Observe that
\mathbb{K}
is the localisation of
\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}
at
T
