, and the localisation
C^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}}\mathbb{K}
of
C^{*}
at
T
can be identified with
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda},\mathbb{K})
. The exact functor of localisation induces an isomorphism on cohomology,

HF^{*}(H_{\lambda},\mathbb{K})\cong H^{*}(C^{*},\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}}\mathbb{K}.

We need to refine the above argument, by taking into account the almost complex structure.
For now, we assume that a choice has been made of some admissible Hamiltonians
H_{\lambda}
labelled by a sequence of the generic slopes
\lambda=\lambda_{i}>0
which diverge to infinity (later we will compare two such choices). We assume that
H_{\lambda},\phi
are constructed as in
3.14
, so that for
\lambda_{i}<\lambda_{i+1}
the Hamiltonians
H_{\lambda_{i}},H_{\lambda_{i+1}}
agree up to the linearity interval
L_{i}
.

In particular, this means that an admissible monotone homotopy
H_{s}
from
H_{\lambda_{i+1}}
to
H_{\lambda_{i}}
exists (see
3.7
), satisfying the more general condition in
3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{I}_{\lambda}
denote the space (with the
C^{\infty}
-topology) of
\omega
-compatible almost complex structures on
Y
equal to
I
outside of a chosen compact subset
K_{\lambda}\subset Y
containing all
1
-periodic orbits of
H_{\lambda}
in its interior, and equal to
I
over all
36
36

36

It suffices that this holds on specified subintervals of the
L_{i}
, and that in arguments where we need
L_{i}
to be large (in monotonicity lemma arguments on
B
) we also ensure that such subintervals of
L_{i}
are large. We recall that to ensure transversality for Floer solutions, the genericity of
I^{\prime}
just needs to be ensured in some region that the solution crosses through. For example, one might wish to perturb
I
near the boundary of the linearity interval, so that transversality is ensured for any Floer solution that tries to enter the linearity region.
of the linearity intervals
L_{i}
.

Let
\mathcal{I}_{E,\lambda}\subset\mathcal{I}_{\lambda}
denote a path-connected neighbourhood of
I
consisting of
I^{\prime}
for which all Floer solutions of
(H_{\lambda},I^{\prime})
of energy at most
E
satisfy the filtration inequality, and all Floer continuation solutions of
(H_{\lambda},I_{s})
for any homotopy
I_{s}\subset\mathcal{I}_{E,\lambda}
of energy at most
E
satisfy the filtration inequality (that such a neighbourhood exists is implied by
4.1
). When we refer to “generic”
I^{\prime}
or
I_{s}
we are referring to the Baire second category subspaces consisting respectively of those
I^{\prime}
for which transversality holds for Floer trajectories of
(H_{\lambda},I^{\prime})
and those
I_{s}
for which transversality holds for continuation maps of
(H_{\lambda},I_{s})
(here
I_{s}
need not be generic for each
s
, but we assume that
I_{s}=I_{-}
for
s\ll 0
and
I_{s}=I_{+}
for
s\gg 0
for generic
I_{\pm}
). Denote by
\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}\subset\mathcal{I}_{\lambda}
all (or any collection of) neighbourhoods of
I
labelled by partially ordered parameters
\epsilon
(e.g.
\epsilon
can be taken to equal the neighbourhood itself, partially ordered by inclusion). We abusively write “
\epsilon\to 0
” to mean that we are taking the directed limit over inclusions of the
\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}
as the
\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}
converge to
I
in the
C^{\infty}
-topology.

Corollary 4.2
.

For generic
I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{E,\lambda}
, the Floer complex

CF^{*}_{E,\lambda}(I^{\prime}):=CF^{*}(H_{\lambda},I^{\prime},\mathbb{K}_{E})

is well-defined and the differential respects the filtration. The quotient
\mathbb{K}_{E}\leftarrow\mathbb{K}_{E^{\prime}}
for
E\leq E^{\prime}
induces an “energy-restriction map” for any
I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{E^{\prime},\lambda}
,

\psi_{E,E^{\prime}}:CF^{*}_{E,\lambda}(I^{\prime})\leftarrow CF^{*}_{E^{\prime},\lambda}(I^{\prime}).

Any generic homotopy
I_{s}\subset\mathcal{I}_{E,\lambda}
from
I_{-}
to
I_{+}
induces a filtration preserving continuation map

\varphi_{I_{s}}:CF^{*}_{E,\lambda}(I_{+})\to CF^{*}_{E,\lambda}(I_{-}),

which is a quasi-isomorphism.

For any generic
I_{s}\subset\mathcal{I}_{E^{\prime},\lambda}
and
E\leq E^{\prime}
these maps fit into a commutative diagram,

\textstyle{CF^{*}_{E,\lambda}(I_{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{E,E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{I_{s}}}
\textstyle{CF^{*}_{E,\lambda}(I_{-})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{E,E^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{CF^{*}_{E^{\prime},\lambda}(I_{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{I_{s}}}
\textstyle{CF^{*}_{E^{\prime},\lambda}(I_{-})}

Given an admissible monotone homotopy
H_{s}
from
H_{\lambda_{-}}
to
H_{\lambda_{+}}
, there is a neighbourhood
\mathcal{I}_{E,\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\subset\mathcal{I}_{E,\lambda^{\prime}}
of
I
for which the continuation map

\varphi_{H_{s}}:CF^{*}_{E,\lambda_{+}}(I^{\prime})\to CF^{*}_{E,\lambda_{-}}(I^{\prime}).

is filtration-preserving. By making the linearity intervals sufficiently large in the construction of the
H_{\lambda}
, the
\varphi_{H_{s}}
will be inclusions of subcomplexes. The
\varphi_{H_{s}}
maps commute with
\psi_{E,E^{\prime}}
, and up to quasi-isomorphism they commute with
\varphi_{I_{s}}
.

In particular, for any given slopes and energies, any sufficiently small
\epsilon
will ensure that the above statements hold for all
I^{\prime}
, respectively
I_{s}
, inside
\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

These claims follow essentially immediately from
4.1
, so we just make some comments.

Regarding the energies, once we impose an energy bound
E_{0}
, then the proof that the Floer continuation map is a chain map over
\mathbb{K}_{E_{0}}

only requires looking at
1
-dimensional moduli spaces of Floer continuation solutions of total energy up to
E_{0}
, and when such solutions break they can only give rise to a Floer trajectory of energy at most
E_{0}
since energy is additive.

The commutativity of the diagram follows from the fact that both horizontal maps are induced by the natural quotient map
\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{K}_{E}=\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}/T^{E}\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}
applied to the continuation map
\varphi_{I_{s}}:CF^{*}(H_{\lambda},I_{+},\mathbb{K})\to CF^{*}(H_{\lambda},I_{-},\mathbb{K})
(this map is well-defined but may not preserve the filtration). The same reasoning implies that
\varphi_{H_{s}}
commutes with
\psi_{E,E^{\prime}}
. That
\varphi_{H_{s}}
and
\varphi_{I_{s}}
commute up to quasi-isomorphism is a standard fact about Floer continuation maps.

Regarding
\varphi_{H_{s}}
: by
4.1
, for
I^{\prime}
close to
I
, all continuation solutions respect the filtration. We then run the same proof as in
3.14
(in particular see
3.13
), using that
I_{s}=I
on the linearity intervals, so the monotonicity lemma for
B
holds there.

The final statement is the observation that given
E,\lambda
we have
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}\subset\mathcal{I}_{E,\lambda}
for all “small
\epsilon
”.
∎

4.4.
Period-bracketed Floer cohomology

Definition 4.3
.

Let
CF^{*}_{E,\lambda,[0,b]}(I^{\prime})\subset CF^{*}_{E,\lambda}(I^{\prime})
be the subcomplex generated by all
1
-orbits whose associated
S^{1}
-periods lie in
[0,b]
. By
4.2
this is well-defined (see also
Section

3.7
, and notice here we filter by period, rather than by
F
, so imposing “period
\,\leq b
” yields a subcomplex).
Its cohomology

HF^{*}_{E,\lambda,[0,b]}(I^{\prime})
does not depend up to isomorphism on the choice of
I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{E,\lambda}
. Similarly, one can define the quotient complex

CF^{*}_{E,\lambda,(a,b]}(I^{\prime}):=CF^{*}_{E,\lambda,[0,b]}(I^{\prime})/CF^{*}_{E,\lambda,[0,a]}(I^{\prime}),

and
HF^{*}_{E,\lambda,(a,b]}(I^{\prime})
will be independent of such
I^{\prime}
.
We call
(a,b]
the
period filtration bracket
. If in the quotient we used
[0,a)
instead of
[0,a]
, we would get period bracket
[a,b]
, etc.

Any filtration preserving map (see
3.5
)
induces a map with period brackets imposed, e.g. the commutative diagram in
4.2
holds with any period bracket.

Remark 4.4
.

It is more intrinsic that the labels
[a,b]
refer to
S^{1}
-period values
T_{-i}
(and period zero for the constant orbits), rather than using
F
-filtration value brackets. This is because the
F
-values change if we choose a different construction of the
H_{\lambda}
(see Section
3.7
and
3.4
).

Corollary 4.5
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be any period bracket, e.g.
[a,b]
or
(a,b]
for
a,b\in[0,\infty]
.
Up to filtration preserving Floer continuation isomorphisms, the
\mathbb{K}_{E}
-modules

HF^{*}_{E,\lambda,\mathcal{P}}:=\varinjlim_{I^{\prime}\to I}HF^{*}_{E,\lambda,\mathcal{P}}(I^{\prime}):=\varinjlim_{\epsilon\to 0}\{HF^{*}_{E,\lambda,\mathcal{P}}(I^{\prime}):I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}\}

are independent of the choices of
\mathcal{I}_{E,\lambda}
or the homotopies
I_{s}
. These fit into a commutative diagram,

\textstyle{HF^{*}_{E,\lambda,\mathcal{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{E,E^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{H_{s}}}
\textstyle{HF^{*}_{E,\lambda^{\prime},\mathcal{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{E,E^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{HF^{*}_{E^{\prime},\lambda,\mathcal{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{H_{s}}}
\textstyle{HF^{*}_{E^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\mathcal{P}}}

thus the following
\mathbb{K}
-module is well-defined

HF^{*}_{\lambda,\mathcal{P}}:=\mathbb{K}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}\geq 0}\varprojlim_{E\to\infty}HF^{*}_{E,\lambda,\mathcal{P}}.

Up to a filtration-preserving isomorphism induced by Floer continuation maps, this
\mathbb{K}
-module does not depend on the choices of
H_{\lambda}
.

Proof.

This mostly follows by
4.2
, up to some additional comments.

Continuation maps only depend on the choice of homotopies up to chain homotopy, so the maps
\varphi_{I_{s}}
and
\varphi_{H_{s}}
on cohomology are independent of the choice of generic homotopies
I_{s}
and
H_{s}
. By virtue of the colimit definition,
HF^{*}_{E,\lambda,\mathcal{P}}
is independent of the choices of
\mathcal{I}_{E,\lambda}
.

Recall that continuation maps compose correctly at the cohomology level, indeed one can work with homotopies of the pairs
(H_{s},I_{s})
; and composites of such maps up to chain homotopy agree with the continuation map induced by concatenating the homotopies of those pairs. Thus the maps
\varphi_{H_{s}}
and
\varphi_{I_{s}}
(and, as discussed above, also
\psi_{E,E^{\prime}}
) all fit into filtration-preserving commutative diagrams on cohomology whenever they are defined.

Consider now two different choices of constructions of admissible Hamiltonians
H_{\lambda_{1}}^{(1)}
,
H_{\lambda_{2}}^{(2)}
(where
\lambda_{1}
ranges over some cofinal collection of values, and so does
\lambda_{2}
). To build an isomorphism between the two colimits that these two choices induce, it suffices to construct filtration-preserving continuation maps
37
37

37

The inverse map on direct limits will be constructed similarly via maps
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{1}}^{(1)},I^{\prime},\mathbb{K}_{E})\to HF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}^{(2)},I^{\prime},\mathbb{K}_{E})
when
\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}^{\prime}
. The fact that the composites of these two maps is the identity map follows because the composites
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{2}}^{(2)},I^{\prime},\mathbb{K}_{E})\to HF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{1}}^{(1)},I^{\prime},\mathbb{K}_{E})\to HF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}^{(2)},I^{\prime},\mathbb{K}_{E})
are continuation maps
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{2}}^{(2)},I^{\prime},\mathbb{K}_{E})\to HF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}^{(1)},I^{\prime},\mathbb{K}_{E})
which are part of the colimit definition, so they actually define the identity map on the colimit.

HF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{2}}^{(2)},I^{\prime},\mathbb{K}_{E})\to HF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{1}}^{(1)},I^{\prime},\mathbb{K}_{E})
when
\lambda_{2}\leq\lambda_{1}
and
I^{\prime}
is sufficiently close to
I
. As
\lambda_{2}\leq\lambda_{1}
, we can always build such a filtration-preserving admissible monotone continuation map by
3.8
, and being continuation maps they will automatically be compatible with the
\varphi_{I_{s}}
-continuation maps on cohomology, and admissibility ensures they count Floer continuation solutions with non-negative
T
powers thus they are also compatible with the
\psi_{E,E^{\prime}}
-maps.
∎

4.5.
Period-bracketed symplectic cohomology

Morally, we have filtered the Floer cohomology of
(Y,\varphi)
by “action brackets” induced by the action functional on the projected space
B
via
\Psi
. This is not entirely accurate, as we use a cut-off function
\phi
to ignore the contributions of the action functional in certain regions. The natural inclusion/truncation of action brackets induces a long exact sequence:

Corollary 4.6
.

For
a\leq b\leq c
in
[0,\infty]
, there is a long exact sequence of
\mathbb{K}
-modules

\cdots\to HF^{*}_{\lambda,[a,b]}\to HF^{*}_{\lambda,[a,c]}\to HF^{*}_{\lambda,(b,c]}\to HF^{*+1}_{\lambda,[a,b]}\to\cdots

and its
\lambda
-colimit induces a long exact sequence on period-bracketed symplectic cohomology groups,

\cdots\to SH^{*}_{[a,b]}(Y,\varphi)\to SH^{*}_{[a,c]}(Y,\varphi)\to SH^{*}_{(b,c]}(Y,\varphi)\to SH^{*+1}_{[a,b]}(Y,\varphi)\to\cdots

In the case
a=0
,
b=0
,
c=+\infty
, we get the usual long exact sequence relating the
\mathbb{K}
-modules
QH^{*}(Y)
,
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
, and
SH_{+}^{*}(Y,\varphi):=SH^{*}_{(0,\infty]}(Y,\varphi).
∎

Definition 4.7
.

The Corollary ensures that the following
\mathbb{K}
-module is well-defined,

SH^{*}_{[0,b]}(Y,\varphi):=\varinjlim_{\lambda\to\infty}HF^{*}_{\lambda,[0,b]}=\varinjlim_{\lambda\to\infty}\left(\mathbb{K}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}\geq 0}\varprojlim_{E\to\infty}\varinjlim_{I^{\prime}\to I}HF^{*}_{E,\lambda,[0,b]}(I^{\prime})\right).

Corollary 4.8
.

SH^{*}_{[0,b]}(Y,\varphi)
is a graded-commutative unital
\mathbb{K}
-algebra admitting a natural unital
\mathbb{K}
-algebra homomorphism

QH^{*}(Y)\to SH^{*}_{[0,b]}(Y,\varphi)
.

Proof.

That the filtration is compatible with (a careful construction of) the pair-of-pants product was shown in
[
RŽ23
]
.
For
\lambda^{\prime\prime}\geq 2\,\mathrm{max}(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})
, the pair-of-pants product partly respects the period filtration: for
a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
,

HF^{*}_{\lambda,(a,b]}\otimes HF^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime},(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]}\to HF^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime\prime},(\mathrm{max}(a+b^{\prime},a^{\prime}+b),\;b+b^{\prime}]}

(by convention
a+b^{\prime}=-\infty
if
a=-\infty
, and similarly for
a^{\prime}+b
). For
a=a^{\prime}=-\infty
, we obtain the claimed product structure on
SH^{*}_{[0,b]}(Y,\varphi)
.
The natural map from
QH^{*}(Y)
arises from the PSS-isomorphism
QH^{*}(Y)\cong HF^{*}_{\lambda,[0,0]}\cong HF^{*}_{\textrm{small slope}}
(the latter’s chain level generators are the
1
-periodic orbits of
H_{\lambda}
with filtration value
F=0
), followed by the natural map into the direct limit.
∎

Letting
b=\lambda
be an admissible slope for which we chose
H_{\lambda}
in the construction, we deduce that

SH^{*}_{[0,\lambda]}(Y,\varphi)\cong HF^{*}_{\lambda}.

More generally, if
b=\mu
equals an
S^{1}
-period value
T_{-i}=c^{\prime}(H)=\mu\leq\lambda
, then

HF^{*}_{\lambda,[0,b]}\cong HF^{*}_{\mu^{+}}\cong SH^{*}_{[0,\mu^{+}]}(Y,\varphi)

where
\mu^{+}
is any admissible slope
38
38

38

or explicitly use
H_{\mu^{+}}
constructed from
H_{\lambda}
by extending with slope
\mu^{+}
in the region at infinity where
H_{\lambda}
has slope
\,\geq\mu^{+}
. The filtration bracket
[0,b]
means we ignore the new
1
-orbits that
H_{\lambda}
has which
H_{\mu^{+}}
does not, and that
CF^{*}_{\mu^{+}}(I^{\prime})\subset CF^{*}_{\lambda}(I^{\prime})
is a subcomplex for
I^{\prime}
sufficiently close to
I
.
in the interval
(T_{-i},T_{-(i+1)},)
.

Under these identifications, a continuation map
HF^{*}_{\lambda}\to HF^{*}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
for
\lambda\leq\lambda^{\prime}
corresponds to the natural map that enlarges the right-bracket,

SH^{*}_{[0,\lambda]}(Y,\varphi)\to SH^{*}_{[0,\lambda^{\prime}]}(Y,\varphi).

4.6.
The
\varphi
-filtration revisited

We can now rewrite
1.1
as follows.

Corollary 4.9
.

The
\varphi
-filtration ideals of
QH^{*}(Y)
are

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}\;\;:=\bigcap_{\mathrm{generic}\,\mu>\lambda}\ker(QH^{*}(Y)\to SH^{*}_{[0,\mu]}(Y,\varphi)).

Remark 4.10
.

If one wished to carry out a more refined construction at the chain level, one could try to apply the telescope model methods by Varolgunes
[
Var21
]
.

We comment on the order of the limits/colimits.
The colimit over
I^{\prime}
must be done first, otherwise the filtration estimates may fail. In general, the order of the
\lambda
-colimit and the
E
-limit in the definition of
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
is important.
Having the
\lambda
-colimit on the outside means that any given class can be realised for some large enough slope
\lambda
, and a representative is described by following a “zig-zag” diagram

\textstyle{CF^{*}_{E,\lambda}(I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{I_{s}}}
\textstyle{CF^{*}_{E,\lambda}(I^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{I_{s}}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{I_{s}}}
\textstyle{CF^{*}_{E,\lambda}(I^{(N)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{E,E^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{CF^{*}_{E^{\prime},\lambda}(I^{(N)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{I_{s}}}
\textstyle{\cdots}

where one can keep lifting the cycle to higher-energy brackets, such as
E^{\prime}\geq E
above, at the cost of applying enough quasi-isomorphisms to the cycle to reach an almost complex structure close enough to
I
, such as
I^{(N)}
above. If however the
E
-limit were on the outside, then it may happen that a “zig-zag” representative requires repeated increases of
\lambda
, as it may otherwise not be possible to find a lifted
cycle
. Indeed, a cycle
c
for
(H_{\lambda},\mathbb{K}_{E})
will lift to a cycle
c+T^{E}c^{\prime}
for
(H_{\lambda},\mathbb{K}_{E^{\prime}})
precisely if the new boundary terms of order
T^{e}
, with
e\in[E,E^{\prime}]
, appearing in
\partial c
can be cancelled by
T^{E}\partial c^{\prime}
for some
\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}
-combination of
1
-orbits of
H_{\lambda}
. Increasing
\lambda
to
\lambda^{\prime}
means that more
1
-orbits are at our disposal to build
c^{\prime}
.
A simple situation, where lifting cycles is not a problem, occurs when no new
1
-orbits appear in degree
\deg(c)
for large enough
\lambda
.

Corollary 4.11
.

For all symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifolds with
c_{1}(Y)=0
(e.g. all CSRs), for any period bracket
\mathcal{P}
we may interchange the following (co)limits,

SH^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}(Y,\varphi)\cong\mathbb{K}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}\geq 0}\varinjlim_{\lambda\to\infty}\varprojlim_{E\to\infty}\varinjlim_{I^{\prime}\to I}HF^{*}_{E,\lambda,\mathcal{P}}(I^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{K}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}\geq 0}\varprojlim_{E\to\infty}\varinjlim_{\lambda\to\infty}\varinjlim_{I^{\prime}\to I}HF^{*}_{E,\lambda,\mathcal{P}}(I^{\prime}).

Given a degree
k
, for
\lambda
sufficiently large, and any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
, we can identify

SH^{k}_{\mathcal{P}}(Y,\varphi)\cong HF^{k}_{\lambda,\mathcal{P}}:=\mathbb{K}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}\geq 0}\varprojlim_{E\to\infty}\varinjlim_{I^{\prime}\to I}HF^{*}_{E,\lambda,\mathcal{P}}(I^{\prime}).

In particular, picking
\lambda
large enough so that the above holds in all degrees
k\in[-1,2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y)
, then for
I^{\prime}
sufficiently close to
I
we have a
\mathbb{K}
-isomorphism

\partial:HF^{k-1}_{\lambda,(0,\infty]}(I^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}QH^{k}(Y;\mathbb{K})

induced by the Floer differential,
39
39

39

The image of
\partial(c)
on a class
[c]\in HF^{k-1}_{\lambda,(0,\infty]}(I^{\prime})
lies in filtration zero, since
[\partial c]=0\in HF^{k-1}_{\lambda,(0,\infty]}(I^{\prime})
.
for
k\in[0,2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y)
(i.e. where
QH^{k}(Y;\mathbb{K})
may be supported).

The
\varphi
-filtration ideals defined by
1.1
on
QH^{*}(Y;\mathbb{K})
are then precisely the images

\partial:HF^{*-1}_{\lambda,(0,b)}(I^{\prime})\longrightarrow QH^{*}(Y;\mathbb{K})

as
b>0
varies (this image can change only for outer
S^{1}
-period values
b
, which are non-admissible slopes
\lambda
, where the
\varphi
-filtration of
1.1
can jump).

Proof.

The fact that the localisation
\mathbb{K}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}}
commutes with the
\lambda
-colimit
just uses the categorical fact that two colimits always commute (localisation at
T
of a
\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}
-module
M
can be viewed as a colimit over inclusions of the modules
\mathbb{K}_{\geq a}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}}M
as
a\to-\infty
).
By
2.12
,
in any given degree, there are only finitely many generators at the Floer chain level in that degree (
1
-orbits of
H_{\lambda}
corresponding to the critical points of the auxiliary Morse functions on Morse–Bott manifolds of
1-
orbits of
H_{\lambda}
, for
\lambda
sufficiently large, see
Appendix

A
– see also
4.12
).
So the condition mentioned at the end of
4.10
ensures that the
\lambda
-colimit and
E
-limit commute.

As the chain complex in a given degree
k
is finitely generated, the
\lambda
-colimit is taken over linear maps
\varphi_{j}
of finite dimensional
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces
V_{j}
of uniformly bounded dimension. In such a general situation,
V_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{1}}}{{\to}}V_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{2}}}{{\to}}V_{3}\to\cdots
, by considering the dimension of the direct limit, there must be a finite
n
such that all classes are represented in
V_{n}
, and the direct limit is naturally identifiable with
V_{n}/\ker\varphi_{n}^{m}
where we pick any large enough
m
since the kernel of
\varphi_{n}^{m}:=\varphi_{n+m}\circ\varphi_{n+m-1}\circ\cdots\circ\varphi_{n}
stabilises for dimension reasons.
In our case, the
\varphi_{i}
are the cohomology maps of inclusions of subcomplexes, and
V_{n}
is the Floer cohomology for
H_{\lambda}
for large enough
\lambda
. We may assume that increasing
\lambda
further does not produce new
1
-orbits in degrees
k
and
k-1
, thus
\varphi_{n}^{m}
is the identity map on cohomology.

The final claim follows from the long exact sequence in
4.6
, since a class in
QH^{*}(Y)
vanishes via the natural map
\widetilde{c}_{b}:QH^{*}(Y)\cong HF^{*}_{\lambda,[0,0]}\to HF^{*}_{\lambda,[0,b]}
if and only if it lies in the image of the connecting map
HF^{*-1}_{\lambda,(0,b]}\to HF^{*}_{\lambda,[0,0]}
of the long exact sequence (and by chasing the definition of the connecting map of a cohomology long exact sequence induced by a short exact sequence one sees that the connecting map is the Floer differential before quotienting out any high-filtration terms). By the final claim in
4.5
, if
b=T_{-i}=c^{\prime}(H)=\mu
, then
\widetilde{c}_{b}
can be identified with the map
c_{\mu^{+}}
used in
1.1
.
∎

Remark 4.12

(Perturbations of the Hamiltonian)
.

In the above discussion, we used the Morse–Bott (i.e. cascade) model for Floer cohomology (
Appendix

A
), as we did not perturb
H_{\lambda}
. One could carry out a perturbation
H_{\lambda}^{\prime}
of
H_{\lambda}
near the Morse--Bott manifolds to obtain non-degenerate orbits, keeping in mind that this ruins the filtration
40
40

40

(
43
) fails if we perturb
H_{\lambda},
one gets a non-vanishing term
d\alpha(\xi,W)
in equation (
46
), where
W:=\Psi_{*}(X_{\widetilde{H}_{\lambda}}-X_{H_{\lambda}})
is the projection of the difference in Hamiltonian vector fields in
Y
that occurred by perturbation. The change in the difference of the filtration values (
50
) is caused by integration
\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\alpha(\partial_{s}v,W)ds
of this term, which we cannot estimate.

analogously to what happened for perturbations of
I
. By using the perturbation procedure described in
Appendix

C
, one can ensure that the non-constant
1
-orbits are a subset of the original
1
-orbits and therefore project via
\Psi
to
S^{1}
-orbits in
B
, so their filtration values are unchanged. The same Gromov compactness arguments used for perturbations
I^{\prime}
of
I
apply when we use perturbations
H_{\lambda}^{\prime}
of
H_{\lambda}
, and similarly for continuation maps we use perturbations
I_{s}^{\prime},H_{s}^{\prime}
of
I,H_{s}
. Thus the same arguments as above hold after replacing the innermost
\varinjlim
over
I^{\prime}\to I
by a
\varinjlim
over pairs
(I^{\prime},H^{\prime})\to(I,H)
.

4.7.
Adapting energy-bracketing to the
S^{1}
-equivariant setup

In
Section

1.8

we work with
S^{1}
-equivariant Floer cohomology in the sense of
[
MR23
]
, where a
1
-orbit contributes a copy of the
\mathbb{K}[u]
-module
\mathbb{F}
, rather than
\mathbb{K}
, where

\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)/u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cong H_{-*}(\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{\infty})
.
In
Section

4.3
we explained energy-bracketing using
\mathbb{K}_{E_{0}}=\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}/T^{E_{0}}\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}
, so that each
1
-orbit contributed to
C^{*}_{E_{0}}:=CF^{*}(H_{\lambda},\mathbb{K}_{E_{0}})
a copy of
\mathbb{K}_{E_{0}}
. In the
S^{1}
-equivariant setup, we instead get a contribution of
\mathbb{F}_{E_{0}}
for each
1
-orbit, where

\mathbb{F}_{E_{0}}:=\mathbb{K}_{E_{0}}(\!(u)\!)/u\mathbb{K}_{E_{0}}[\![u]\!].

The non-equivariant methods then adapt very easily to the
S^{1}
-equivariant case, thus will be omitted.

5.
Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequences

5.1.
Morse–Bott manifolds of Hamiltonian 1-orbits of
H_{\lambda}

We continue with the initial assumption from
Section

3
. Call
1-orbits
the 1-periodic orbits of the Hamiltonian
H_{\lambda}
from
Section

3
. As
X_{H_{\lambda}}=c^{\prime}(H)X_{H},
the 1-orbits are either constant
1
-orbits at points in
\mathfrak{F}=Crit(H_{\lambda})=Crit(H),
or, for some
p=-1,\dots,-r,
are non-constant
1
-orbits in a
slice

(58)

\Sigma_{p}:=\{y\in Y\mid H(x)=H_{p}\}\subset Y,

where the values
H_{p}
are defined by
c^{\prime}(H_{p})=T_{p}.

Denote by
\mathcal{O}_{p}:=\mathcal{O}_{p,H_{\lambda}}
the moduli space of parametrised
1
-orbits of
H_{\lambda}
in
\Sigma_{p},
and by
B_{p}
the set of their initial points,

B_{p}:=\{x(0)\mid x\in\mathcal{O}_{p}\}\subset\Sigma_{p}.

It will be convenient to allow the notation in the case
p=0
, for the constant
1
-orbits
B_{0}=\mathcal{O}_{0}=\mathfrak{F}
, so
T_{0}=0
(also
T_{p}=c^{\prime}(T_{p})
will be an abuse of notation when
p=0
, as
H_{0}
is not well-defined since
H
may take different values on the connected components
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
of
\mathfrak{F}
, and we do not require
c^{\prime}=0
there).
By
3.4
, the filtration value
F
on
1
-orbits only depends on
c^{\prime}(H_{p})=T_{p}
, so let

F_{p}:=F(\mathcal{O}_{p})\textrm{ for }p\leq 0,\textrm{ and }F_{p}=p\textrm{ for }p\geq 0.

Recall
X_{S^{1}}=X_{H}
is the vector field of the
1
-periodic
S^{1}
-flow
\varphi_{e^{2\pi it}}
, so
t\in\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\cong S^{1}
.

Recall
Y_{m}
denotes the set of
\mathbb{Z}/m
-torsion points in
Y
(
2.5
), so these points are fixed points of the
S^{1}
-flow for time
1/m
.

Lemma 5.1
.

\displaystyle B_{p}=\Sigma_{p}\cap Y_{m}
where
T_{p}=k/m
for coprime
k,m\in\mathbb{N}.

Proof.

B_{p}
is the union of the period
T_{p}
orbits of the
S^{1}
-flow on
\Sigma_{p}
, as

X_{H_{\lambda}}=c^{\prime}(H_{p})X_{H}=T_{p}X_{S^{1}}
on
\Sigma_{p}.

As all proper closed subgroups of
S^{1}
are discrete,

T_{p}
has to be a rational number,
T_{p}=k/m
for some coprime
k,m\in\mathbb{N}.

The latter condition,
(k,m)=1,
implies
\alpha k+\beta m=1
for some
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}
. Thus, points in
B_{p}
are also fixed by the
S^{1}
-flow for time

\alpha k/m=(1/m)-\beta
, and thus also for time
1/m
, so they lie in
Y_{m}.
Thus,
B_{p}\subset\Sigma_{p}\cap Y_{m}
.
Conversely, a point in
\Sigma_{p}\cap Y_{m}
is fixed by the
S^{1}
-flow for time
1/m
, and thus also for time
T_{p}
, so it lies in
B_{p}.

∎

Corollary 5.2
.

The connected components of
B_{p}=\Sigma_{p}\cap Y_{m}
are precisely the smooth submanifolds

(59)

B_{p,\beta}=\Sigma_{p}\cap Y_{m,\beta},

where the
\beta
-labelling runs over the non-compact connected components
Y_{m,\beta}
of
Y_{m}
.

Proof.

By
5.1
,
B_{p}
is the fixed locus of the
\varphi
-action by
\mathbb{Z}/m\leq\mathbb{C}^{*}
on
\Sigma_{p},
so it is a smooth submanifold of
\Sigma_{p}
.
41
41

41

being a fixed locus of a compact Lie group action on a smooth manifold,
[
DK00
, p.108]
.

By
2.5
,
Y_{m,\beta}\subset Y
is a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-submanifold so
2.4
applied to
H|_{Y_{m,\beta}}:Y_{m,\beta}\to\mathbb{R}
shows that

H|_{Y_{m,\beta}}^{-1}(H_{p})=\Sigma_{p}\cap Y_{m,\beta}
is connected.
Observe that only non-compact components
Y_{m,\beta}
intersect
\Sigma_{p}
. For
\beta\neq\beta^{\prime}
, the
Y_{m,\beta},Y_{m,\beta^{\prime}}
are disjoint, so the connected closed submanifolds
B_{p,\beta},B_{p,\beta^{\prime}}
are disjoint and are thus distinct connected components of
B_{p}
.
∎

Moreover, the above
\beta
-labelling depends on
m,
but not on
k
:

Lemma 5.3
.

Suppose
T_{p_{1}}=k_{1}/m
and
T_{p_{2}}=k_{2}/m
have the same denominators, where
(k_{1},m)=(k_{2},m)=1
.
Then
B_{p_{1}}
and
B_{p_{2}}
are diffeomorphic, indeed diffeomorphic to the
B_{p}
with
T_{p}=1/m.

Proof.

As

B_{p_{1},\beta}=\Sigma_{p_{1}}\cap Y_{m,\beta}
and
B_{p_{2},\beta}=\Sigma_{p_{2}}\cap Y_{m,\beta},
we have

B_{p_{1},\beta}\cong B_{p_{2},\beta}

via the normalised gradient flow
\nabla H/\left\lVert\nabla H\right\rVert^{2}
that flows from one to the other. This holds since
H
is constant on
\Sigma_{p}
, and
Y_{m,\beta}
is preserved by the gradient flow of
H
(which is the action by
\mathbb{R}_{+}\subset\mathbb{C}^{*}
).∎

Consider
42
42

42

The orthogonal
\perp
is calculated with respect to the metric
g(\cdot,\cdot)=\omega(\cdot,I\cdot).
the complex codimension-one distribution
\xi:=(\langle X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\rangle\oplus\langle X_{H_{\lambda}}\rangle)^{\perp}
on
Y\setminus\mathfrak{F}.
Thus, for any point
x\in Y\setminus\mathfrak{F},
we have orthogonal splittings of tangent spaces

(60)

T_{x}Y=\langle X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\rangle\oplus\langle X_{H_{\lambda}}\rangle\oplus\xi,\ \qquad\ T_{x}\Sigma_{p}=\langle X_{H_{\lambda}}\rangle\oplus\xi.

Definition 5.4
.

Denote by
\phi_{\tau}^{X}
the flow of a vector field
X
for time
\tau\in\mathbb{R}.
When
X=X_{F}
is a Hamiltonian vector-field we
will abbreviate this flow by
\phi_{\tau}^{F}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

(\phi_{\tau}^{{H_{\lambda}}})_{*}X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}=X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}+\tau c^{\prime\prime}(H)\left\lVert\nabla(H)\right\rVert^{2}X_{H}.

Proof.

Let
y\in Y.
Abbreviate
\gamma(s)=\phi_{s}^{X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}}(y)=e^{2\pi s}\cdot y
, so
\gamma^{\prime}(s)=X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(\gamma(s))=(\nabla H)(\gamma(s))
, and

(\phi_{\tau}^{{H_{\lambda}}})_{*}(y)X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}={\phi_{\tau}^{{H_{\lambda}}}}_{*}\left(\left.{\tfrac{d}{ds}}\right|_{s=0}\gamma(s)\right)=\left.{\tfrac{d}{ds}}\right|_{s=0}(\phi_{\tau}^{{H_{\lambda}}}(\gamma(s)))=\left.{\tfrac{d}{ds}}\right|_{s=0}(\phi_{\tau c^{\prime}(H(\gamma(s)))}^{{H}}(\gamma(s))),

using
X_{H_{\lambda}}=c^{\prime}(H)X_{H}.

Let
u(s,t):=\phi_{t}^{H}(\gamma(s))
(which is
e^{2\pi(s+it)}\cdot x
).
The last derivative becomes:

\displaystyle\left.{\tfrac{d}{ds}}\right|_{s=0}u(s,\tau c^{\prime}(H(\gamma(s))))

\displaystyle={\tfrac{\partial u}{\partial s}}+\left(\left.{\tfrac{d}{ds}}\right|_{s=0}\tau c^{\prime}(H(\gamma(s)))\right)\tfrac{\partial u}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}+\tau c^{\prime\prime}(H(x))\left\lVert\nabla H(x)\right\rVert^{2}X_{H}.\qed

Now we prove the following non-degeneracy property of the submanifolds
B_{p,\beta},
which is analogous to the Morse--Bott non-degeneracy condition for critical submanifolds.
43
43

43

As we can think of
B_{p,\beta}
being the critical submanifolds for the action
1-
form
d\mathcal{A}_{H_{\lambda}}(x)(\xi)=\int\omega(\dot{x}-X_{H_{\lambda}},\xi).

Proposition 5.6
.

Given any point
y\in B_{p,\beta},
the 1-eigenspace of the linearised return map of the flow for
H_{\lambda}

is precisely the tangent space of
B_{p,\beta},
so

\text{Ker}\,(d_{y}\phi_{1}^{{H_{\lambda}}}-Id)=T_{y}B_{p,\beta}.

Proof.

As in Lemma
5.1
, let
T_{p}=k/m,
for coprime
k,m\in\mathbb{N}.

Then, on
\Sigma_{p}
,

\phi_{1}^{{H_{\lambda}}}=\phi_{T_{p}}^{{H}}=(\phi_{1/m}^{H})^{k}.

So, given
v\in T_{y}\Sigma_{p},
the condition
(\phi_{1}^{{H_{\lambda}}})_{*}(v)=v
is equivalent to
(\phi_{1/m}^{H})_{*}^{k}(v)=v,

and thus to
(\phi_{1/m}^{H})_{*}(v)=v,
due to
(k,m)=1.

Note
\phi_{1/m}^{H}
generates a
\mathbb{Z}/m
-action on
\Sigma_{p}
by isometries (since the metric is
S^{1}
-invariant), and its fixed
locus is
B_{p}
by Lemma
5.1
.
Thus, by
[
Bre72
, Ch.VI, Thm.2.2]
, there is a
\mathbb{Z}/m
-invariant tubular neighbourhood
\mathcal{N}B_{p,\beta}\subset\Sigma_{p}
of
B_{p,\beta}

arising as the image of the
\mathbb{Z}/m
-equivariant exponential map on a neighbourhood
NB_{p,\beta}

of the zero section of the normal bundle of
B_{p,\beta}
.
This identifies
NB_{p,\beta}
with
\mathcal{N}B_{p,\beta}
, making
\mathbb{Z}/m
act on
NB_{p,\beta}
by the linearisation
(\phi_{1/m}^{H})_{*}
.
Hence, if a vector
v\in T_{x}\Sigma_{p}
satisfies
(\phi_{1/m}^{H})_{*}(v)=v,

then
v\in(NB_{p,\beta})_{x}
is fixed by the
\mathbb{Z}/m
-action and so is the curve
exp(vt)\subset\mathcal{N}B_{p,\beta}.
As
B_{p,\beta}
is an isolated fixed locus in
\mathcal{N}B_{p,\beta}
, this curve has to lie in
B_{p,\beta},
so
v\in T_{y}B_{p,\beta}.
Thus
\text{Ker}((d_{y}\phi_{1}^{{H_{\lambda}}}-Id)|_{T_{y}\Sigma_{p}})=T_{y}B_{p,\beta}.

Note
\mathbb{R}X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
is a complementary subspace to
T_{y}\Sigma_{p}\subset T_{y}Y
, so the claim follows by Lemma
5.5
using that
c^{\prime\prime}(H)>0
for
c^{\prime}(H)=T_{p}.

∎

Lemma 5.7
.

The linearisation of the
S^{1}
-flow is complex linear with respect to a unitary trivialisation of
\xi
along any 1-orbit in
B_{p,\beta}.

Proof.

Follows immediately as the
S^{1}
-flow is
I
-linear and a unitary trivialisation is complex-linear.
∎

5.2.
The Morse–Bott spectral sequence for
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda}
)

We now build a spectral sequence analogously to
[
MR23
, Sec.7]
. There, the space at infinity was a positive symplectisation, involving a radial coordinate
R
, an exact symplectic form, contact hypersurfaces
R=\mathrm{constant}
and a Reeb flow (the Hamiltonian flow for
R
). In our setting, on
Y^{\mathrm{out}}
, we instead have the coordinate
H
, a typically non-exact symplectic form, level sets
H=\mathrm{constant}
and the
S^{1}
-flow (the Hamiltonian flow for
H
).

As the Hamiltonian
H_{\lambda}
from
Section

3.2
is autonomous, its 1-orbits are not isolated: they arise in Morse–Bott manifolds by
Section

5.1
, labelled
B_{p,\beta}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
. We will therefore use a
Morse–Bott Floer complex

BCF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
as described in
Appendix

A
, involving a choice of auxiliary Morse functions
f_{p,c}:B_{p,\beta}\to\mathbb{R}
and
f_{\alpha}:\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{R}
. To unify the notation, we write
f_{i}:M_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
for these auxiliary Morse functions, denoting the Morse–Bott manifolds by
M_{i}
. As explained in
Appendix

A
we will blur the distinction between the initial points of the
1
-orbits
x_{0}\in M_{i}
and their associated
1
-orbits
x(t)
,
x(0)=x_{0}
(in
Section

5.1
we distinguished them via the notation
B_{p}
and
\mathcal{O}_{p}
). We call
critical
1
-orbits
the
1
-orbits associated to the critical points
x_{0}\in\mathrm{Crit}(f_{i})\subset M_{i}
. These are the free generators of
BCF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
. By
A.2
,
there is an isomorphism

BHF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\cong HF^{*}(\widetilde{H}_{\lambda})

between the Morse–Bott Floer cohomology (for the unperturbed
H_{\lambda}
) and the Floer cohomology for any generic time-dependent compactly supported perturbation
\widetilde{H}_{\lambda}
of
H_{\lambda}
; moreover these isomorphisms are compatible with continuation maps. That compatibility ensures that the isomorphisms behave well in the limit constructions used when we energy-bracket as in
Section

4.3
.
So for all intents and purposes we may simply write
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
instead of
BHF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
. Also, up to continuation isomorphisms, there is no ambiguity in the meaning of
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)
, and we may period-filter as in
Section

4.5
.

We will be using the terminology introduced in
Appendix

A
(ledge, drop, crest, base, cascade, etc.). A
simple cascade
is a cascade with no drops, so it is a Morse trajectory inside some
M_{i}
for
f_{i}
.

Definition 5.8
.

A
vertical drop
is a drop whose crest and base lie in Morse–Bott submanifolds with the same period value
c^{\prime}(H)
, i.e. the asymptotics lie in the same slice
\Sigma_{p}
(see
Section

5.1
). A
self-drop
is a vertical drop whose crest and base lie in the same Morse–Bott submanifold.

Lemma 5.9
.

If there is a cascade solution joining critical
1
-orbits
x_{-}\in M_{i}
to
x_{+}\in M_{j}
for the Hamiltonian
H_{\lambda}
and the unperturbed almost complex structure
I
, then the filtration satisfies

\hskip 86.11084ptF(x_{-})\geq F(x_{+})\qquad(\Leftrightarrow\,\textrm{the periods satisfy }c^{\prime}(H(x_{-}))\leq c^{\prime}(H(x_{+})),

with equality if and only if the cascade is simple or all of its drops are vertical drops.
In particular,
F
remains constant on ledges and vertical drops, and vertical drops for
F\neq 0
are as in
3.11
.

The cascades for
I
with
F(x_{-})=F(x_{+})=F_{p}
are trapped in the neighbourhood of the slice
\Sigma_{p}
where
\phi
is constant, which only contains
1
-orbits from
\mathcal{O}_{p}
. For
F_{p}\neq 0
, the
\Psi
-image of such cascades lies in an
m
-torsion submanifold of
B
(
2.5
) where
T_{p}=c^{\prime}(H(x_{\pm}))=k/m\in\mathbb{Q}
for
k,m
coprime.
If a vertical drop
u
has asymptotics
u_{\pm}
in
B_{k/m,\beta_{\pm}}
,
then the
Y_{m,\beta_{\pm}}
have overlapping
\Psi
-projections in
B^{\mathrm{out}}=\Sigma\times[R_{0},\infty)
, as

\mathrm{Image}\,(\Psi(u))\subset\Psi(Y_{m,\beta_{-}})\cap\Psi(Y_{m,\beta_{+}})\neq\emptyset
.

Given
E>0
, for any perturbation
I^{\prime}
sufficiently close to
I
the cascades for
I^{\prime}
of energy at most
E
are close to those for
I
(in
C^{0}
and in
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}
) and any vertical drop
u
from
u_{-}
to
u_{+}
for
F\neq 0
satisfies

•

H(u_{-})=H(u_{+})
, and
u_{\pm}
are both non-constant
1
-orbits;

•

u_{\pm}
correspond to orbits of the
S^{1}
-flow of equal period
T:=c^{\prime}(H(u_{\pm}))
;

•

v=\Psi(u)
lies entirely in a region where
\phi
is constant;

•

v_{\pm}=\Psi(u_{\pm})
correspond to Reeb orbits of period
T
(but may not have the same
R
-value);

•

\mathrm{Im}(v)
is close to the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-orbit of some point
p\in B
;

•

\mathrm{Im}(v)
is close to some
m
-torsion submanifold of
B
, where
T=k/m\in\mathbb{Q}
for
k,m
coprime;

•

\mathrm{Im}(du)
is close to
\mathbb{C}\cdot X_{S^{1}}\oplus\ker\Psi_{*}
.

Thus, if that vertical drop
u
for
I^{\prime},E
goes from
B_{k/m,\beta_{-}}
to
B_{k/m,\beta_{+}}
,
then the
torsion submanifolds
Y_{m,\beta_{\pm}}
have overlapping
\Psi
-projections in
B^{\mathrm{out}}=\Sigma\times[R_{0},\infty)
, so

\Psi(Y_{m,\beta_{-}})\cap\Psi(Y_{m,\beta_{+}})\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

For the first part, we use
3.3
and
3.11
. Recall that a ledge lies in a Morse–Bott submanifold, and
F
is constant there. When
F(x_{-})=F(x_{+})
the ledges involve Morse–Bott manifolds with the same period
T_{p}=c^{\prime}(H_{p})
, so they lie in the same torsion submanifold of
Y
.
Cascades for
I
with only vertical drops cannot exit the
\phi=\textrm{constant}
neighbourhood of
\Sigma_{p}
, as it would enter the region where the filtration
1
-form is strictly negative on a drop, contradicting
F(x_{-})=F(x_{+})
.

The claim about
E_{0},I^{\prime}
follows by the Gromov-compactness argument from
Section

4.3
, and suitably rephrasing
3.11
after perturbation.
In more detail, in view of the energy-bracketing construction of
Section

4
, the only vertical drops for the perturbed
I^{\prime}
that get counted in the Floer complex must be close to (possibly broken and bubbled) vertical drops for the unperturbed
I
. Now
I
-holomorphic bubbles must lie entirely in the fibres of
\Psi
as
B
only admits constant
I_{B}
-holomorphic spheres. The filtration cannot detect those spheres. So a broken and bubbled vertical drop for
I
will project to a broken Floer trajectory in
B
. In view of the zero filtration difference, it follows that all components of that Floer trajectory in
B
are trapped in a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-orbit.

The first claim about the
B_{k/m,\beta_{\pm}}\subset Y_{m,\beta_{\pm}}
, follows from the fact that
\Psi(Y_{m,\beta_{\pm}})
are
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-invariant, and
\mathrm{Image}\,(v)
lies in a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-orbit. The second claim follows as a perturbed
v
is close to such a solution.
∎

As the almost complex structure needs to be perturbed to achieve transversality for cascade solutions, it is understood that we run the energy-bracketing construction of
Section

4
to ensure that the filtration inequality persists. Abbreviate our
period-filtered energy-bracketed Floer complex
by

C^{*}=BCF^{*}_{E,\lambda,\mathcal{P}}(I^{\prime})=BCF^{*}_{\mathcal{P}}(H_{\lambda},I^{\prime};\mathbb{K}_{E}),

using the notation from
Section

4.3
and
Section

4.4
, where
I^{\prime}
is a perturbation of
I
; the constant
E>0
prescribes the energy-bracketing which prescribes the choice of Novikov ring
\mathbb{K}_{E}=\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}/T^{E}\mathbb{K}_{\geq 0}
; and
\mathcal{P}\subset[0,\infty]
is a period bracket forcing generators to have the chosen period values
T_{p}=c^{\prime}(H_{p})
. For example,
\mathcal{P}=(0,\infty]
gives rise to the positive Floer complex where we quotiented out by the subcomplex of constant orbits.

Recall that
1
-orbits in
M_{i}
have the same filtration value
F_{p}
dependent only on the period value
T_{p}=c^{\prime}(H_{p})
associated with
M_{i}
(so
M_{i}\subset\mathcal{O}_{p}
), by
Section

5.1
.

Corollary 5.10
.

The Morse–Bott–Floer differential non-strictly increases the
F
-filtration
F_{p}=F(\mathcal{O}_{p})
, equivalently it non-strictly decreases the period-filtration
T_{p}=c^{\prime}(H_{p})
.

Proof.

This follows by the Gromov-compactness argument from
Section

4.3
, and the filtration inequality from
5.9
.
∎

Thus
C^{*}
admits a
filtration
, using the conventions of
[
MR23
, Sec.7]
:

F^{p}(C^{k}):=\{x\in C^{k}\mid F(x)\geq F_{p}\},

where
\mathbf{k=p+q}
denotes the
total degree
.
In particular,
F^{p}(C^{k})=0
for
p>0
since
F\leq 0
on all
1
-orbits.
The filtration determines an associated spectral sequence
E_{r}^{pq}
such that

E_{0}^{pq}=F^{p}(C^{k})/F^{p+1}(C^{k}).

Definition 5.11
.

By
slope
of the
p
-th column of
E_{r}^{pq}
we mean the number
c^{\prime}(H_{p})=T_{p}.

By construction,
E_{r}^{pq}=0
if the slope
T_{p}\notin\mathcal{P}
.

Proposition 5.12
.

The spectral sequence converges,

E_{r}^{pq}\Rightarrow H^{*}(C)=HF^{*}_{E,\lambda,\mathcal{P}}(I^{\prime}),

where
*
will agree with the total degree
k=p+q
. It satisfies
E_{r}^{pq}=0
for
p>0
. For
p=0
and
r=1
it recovers the quantum cohomology if
0\in\mathcal{P}
:

E_{1}^{0q}\cong QH^{q}(Y)\textrm{ if }0\in\mathcal{P}\qquad(E_{1}^{0q}=0\textrm{ if }0\notin\mathcal{P}).

Proof.

By construction, the filtration is exhaustive and bounded below, thus it converges.
The claim follows immediately, except for the final two statements.
The condition
0\in\mathcal{P}
means we do not quotient out by the generators with filtration value
F=0
.
These generators are precisely the constant
1
-orbits arising as the Morse–Bott submanifolds
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
.
Recall that the filtration ensures that these generate a subcomplex (see
Section

3.7
). In particular, because the filtration difference at the ends of a cascade is zero, by
5.9
and the construction of
H_{\lambda}
in
Section

3.2
, the cascades are trapped in a compact region of
Y^{\mathrm{in}}
where
c^{\prime}
was chosen to be small. It follows that
E_{1}^{0q}
agrees with the Floer cohomology of a Hamiltonian of arbitrarily small slope (i.e. the case when
\lambda
is smaller than any non-zero period), and recall this is isomorphic to
QH^{*}(Y)
via a PSS-isomorphism.
∎

We now compute
E_{1}^{pq}
for
p<0
.
We will now use the notation
B_{p},\mathcal{O}_{p}
from
Section

5.1
, and the construction of
local Morse–Bott Floer cohomology
from

Section

B.2
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
N_{i}
be a neighbourhood of
M_{i}\subset Y
disjoint from the other Morse–Bott submanifolds
M_{j}\neq M_{i}
.
Then cascades in
N_{i}
only involve self-drops, and only involve
1
-orbits in
M_{i}
.

If
\omega
is orbit-atoroidal on
M_{i}
(
B.2
), these cascades are all simple cascades, i.e. Morse trajectories in
M_{i}
. This holds if
\omega
is exact on
M_{i}
, or if
\omega
is aspherical on
\overline{\cup_{t\in(0,1]}\varphi_{t}(M_{i})}
(
B.3
).

There is a constant
E_{0}>0
such that every cascade for
H_{\lambda}
of energy at most
E_{0}
is a simple cascade.

Proof.

This follows by
B.4
and
B.5
.
∎

Corollary 5.14
.

For
p\leq 0
, and
T_{p}\in\mathcal{P}
,

E_{1}^{pq}=BHF^{*}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{O}_{p},H_{\lambda},I^{\prime};\mathbb{K}_{E}).

Assume
c_{1}(Y)=0
. For
p\leq 0
,
T_{p}\in\mathcal{P}
, and for sufficiently small energy
E=E_{0}>0
,

E_{1}^{pq}=BHF^{*}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{O}_{p},H_{\lambda},I^{\prime};\mathbb{K}_{E_{0}})\cong\bigoplus_{i\textrm{ with }F(M_{i})=F_{p}}H^{*-\mu_{H_{\lambda}}(M_{i})}(M_{i})\otimes\mathbb{K}_{E_{0}}

is the ordinary cohomology of the components
M_{i}
of
B_{p}
computed as Morse cohomology for the auxiliary Morse functions, with a grading shift by

(61)

\mu_{H_{\lambda}}(M_{i}):=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\,Y-\tfrac{1}{2}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}M_{i}-RS(M_{i},H_{\lambda})

where
RS(M_{i},H_{\lambda})
is the Robbin–Salamon index of one (hence any)
1
-orbit in
M_{i}
for
H_{\lambda}
.

If
T_{p}\notin\mathcal{P}
, then
E_{1}^{pq}=0
by definition.

Proof.

The induced differential on
E_{0}^{pq}
(whose cohomology is
E_{1}^{pq}
) only depends on cascades whose ends
x_{\pm}
are
1
-orbits with equal filtration value
F=F_{p}
. By
5.9
, their drops must all be vertical drops, and the cascades are trapped in a neighbourhood of
B_{p}
. So the local Morse–Bott Floer cohomology of
B_{p}
is well-defined by
Section

B.2
, yielding the first claim. The second claim follows by the last part of
5.13
, Theorem
B.6
and
D.3
.
∎

Consider now how the above
E_{1}
-pages
E_{1}^{pq}:=E_{1}(H_{\lambda})
depend on the slope
\lambda
. By
3.14
, as we increase the slope
\mu
, the
E_{1}
-columns associated with slope values up to
\lambda
, and their edge-differentials in the spectral sequence, will not vary for slopes
\mu\geq\lambda
. Also, there are continuation maps
E_{1}(H_{\lambda})\to E_{1}(H_{\mu})
which induce the identity map on the columns with slopes
\leq\lambda
. Thus, one can take the various (co)limits explained in
Section

4
, so that
I^{\prime}\to I
,
E\to\infty
and
\lambda\to\infty
,
implying the following corollary. Denote
H_{p,\beta}^{*}
the
E_{\infty}
-page of the energy-spectral sequence, whose
E_{1}
-page is the local Floer cohomology of
\mathcal{O}_{p}
, in
5.14
(see also
B.6
), after taking those same (co)limits.
From now on, we label the components
M_{i}
of
B_{p}
by
B_{p,\beta}
. Ordinary cohomology means with coefficients in
\mathbb{K}
, e.g.
H^{*}(Y):=H^{*}(Y,\mathbb{K})
(see
Section

2.3
). We deduce:

Corollary 5.15
.

There is a convergent spectral sequence of
\mathbb{K}
-modules,

E_{r}^{pq}(\varphi;\mathcal{P})\Rightarrow SH_{\mathcal{P}}^{*}(Y,\varphi),\textrm{ where }E_{1}^{pq}(\varphi;\mathcal{P})=\begin{cases}H^{q}(Y)&if $p=0,\textrm{ and }0\in\mathcal{P}$\\
\bigoplus_{\beta}H_{p,\beta}^{*}[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]&if $p<0,\textrm{ and }T_{p}\in\mathcal{P}$\\
0&otherwise,\end{cases}

Taking
\mathcal{P}=(0,\infty]
and
\mathcal{P}=[0,\infty]
, defines two convergent spectral sequences:

(62)

E_{+}(\varphi)_{r}^{pq}\Rightarrow SH_{+}^{*}(Y,\varphi),\textrm{ where }E(\varphi)_{1}^{pq}=\begin{cases}\bigoplus_{\beta}H_{p,\beta}^{*}[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]&if $p<0,$\\
0&otherwise,\end{cases}

(63)

E(\varphi)_{r}^{pq}\Rightarrow SH^{*}(Y,\varphi),\textrm{ where }E(\varphi)_{1}^{pq}=\begin{cases}H^{q}(Y)&if $p=0,$\\
\bigoplus_{\beta}H_{p,\beta}^{*}[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]&if $p<0,$\\
0&otherwise.\end{cases}

When
c_{1}(Y)=0
, as
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)=0
by
1.2
, the spectral sequence
E(\varphi)^{p,q}_{r}

in fact converges to zero; also, Proposition
6.4
implies that for sufficiently large
r
we have
E(\varphi)^{p,q}_{r}=0
for all
p,q
.

Corollary 5.16
.

For columns
p
of slope
T_{p}\leq\lambda
,
E(\varphi)_{r}^{pq}=E_{r}^{pq}(\varphi;[0,\lambda])
, and

E_{r}^{pq}(\varphi;[0,\lambda])\Rightarrow SH^{*}_{[0,\lambda]}(Y;\varphi)\cong HF^{*}(H_{\lambda}).

Moreover,
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})\cong HF^{*}(\lambda H)
, so when
H^{*}(Y)
lies in even degrees also
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
is supported in even degrees and agrees with the right-hand side of
Equation

9
.

Proof.

This follows by Corollary
3.14
, and the fact that Floer cohomology only depends on the choice of slope at infinity for the Hamiltonian, not on the specific construction of the Hamiltonian.
∎

Remark 5.17

(
Conventions about the spectral sequence Figures)
.

For illustrations of the previous Corollaries, see the figures in
Section

7
. In those figures, each dot indicates a rank one summand. The arrows indicate the edge homomorphisms from the
E_{1}
page onward that are necessary for the spectral sequence to converge to
SH^{*}(Y,\varphi)=0
(as those examples all have
c_{1}(Y)=0
). We call these the
iterated differentials
. They need to be suitably interpreted: one must take cohomology iteratively, by first considering arrows that only move one column to the left to determine the cohomology
E_{2}
, then the arrows that move two columns to the left define a differential on the resulting
E_{2}
and it computes
E_{3}
, etc. (cf. the proof of
6.10
).
We abusively place
H^{*}(B_{p,\beta})[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
in the
E_{1}
-columns rather than the correct
H_{p,\beta}^{*}[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
. Thus, within each column, there is a further energy-spectral sequence from
H^{*}(B_{p,\beta})[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
that converges to
H_{p,\beta}^{*}[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
. Loosely, it means there may be
vertical differentials
(of degree
1
) within the same column that have not yet been taken into account, which are not indicated in the Figures. This explains why certain boxes (such as the arrow from row 1 to row 2 in
Figure

13
) seem to allow vertical differentials. Vertical differentials can often be excluded a posteriori when all classes are needed to kill
H^{*}(Y)
, or by using
5.9
or
5.13
. However, for
\mathcal{S}_{32}
in
Figure

13
, in the
B_{1/3}
column we have seven components
B_{1/3,\beta}
whose cohomology is that of:
S^{3}
, two
S^{2}\times S^{1}
, four
S^{1}
. Here we cannot exclude a vertical differential from (one or two) top degree classes of the
S^{1}
’s, which might kill middle-top degree classes of the
S^{2}\times S^{1}
’s.
There can also be vertical differentials in the local Floer cohomology of some
S^{2}\times S^{1}
, whereas there cannot be a vertical differential for the four
S^{1}
because
\omega
is exact on
S^{1}
(using
5.13
).

Finally, we can relate the spectral sequence
E(\varphi)^{pq}_{r}
with
the filtration
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
from
1.1
.

Proposition 5.18
.

\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
is the subspace of the 0-th column
E(\varphi)_{1}^{0*}\cong H^{*}(Y)
that gets killed by images of iterated
differentials for
E(\varphi)_{r}^{pq}
defined on columns
p
of slope
T_{p}\leq\lambda.

Proof.

\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
is defined as the kernel of the continuation map
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{0}})\rightarrow HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
(where
\lambda_{0}>0
is a small slope), and we ensured that at the chain level
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{0}})\rightarrow CF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
is an inclusion of subcomplexes, so the cycles in
CF^{*}(H_{\lambda_{0}})
that get iteratively killed precisely yield that kernel.
∎

6.
Properties of the Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequences

6.1.
Conventions

We continue with the initial assumption from
Section

3
, and that
c_{1}(Y)=0
.

We will work with
E(\varphi)^{pq}_{r},
but analogous results hold for
E_{+}(\varphi)^{p,q}_{r}
and
E_{r}^{p,q}(\varphi;\mathcal{P})
after specifying cases about
p
, e.g. whether or not
T_{p}
lies in the period-bracket
\mathcal{P}
.

Ordinary cohomology is computed over
\mathbb{K}
coefficients (see
Section

2.3
), so
H^{*}(Y):=H^{*}(Y,\mathbb{K})
.

Morse–Bott submanifolds
will refer to the connected manifolds
B_{p,\beta}
of 1-orbits of
H_{\lambda}
.
Recall by
5.2
that
these are precisely the smooth submanifolds

B_{p,\beta}=\{c^{\prime}(H)=T_{p}=\tfrac{k}{m}\}\cap Y_{m,\beta}

obtained by slicing the non-compact
m
-torsion submanifolds,
where
k,m
are coprime positive integers.

The column
p
of the spectral sequence is called
the time-
T_{p}
column
.
For example, the time-
1
column of the
E_{1}
-page is the degree-shifted cohomology
H^{*}(\Sigma)
of the whole slice
\Sigma:=\{c^{\prime}(H)=1\}
.

We
number the rows of the spectral sequence by the total degree

p+q,
rather than by
q
.

The gradings will be written in terms of the function
\mathbb{W}(x)
, from
Section

2.4
.

It is helpful to read the following results while comparing them with the examples in
Section

7
, keeping in mind the conventions explained in
5.17
.

6.2.
Grading of the Morse–Bott manifolds of
1
-orbits
B_{p,\beta}

Proposition 6.1
.

The grading of a
Morse–Bott submanifold
B_{p,\beta}
is an even integer equal to

(64)

\textstyle\begin{split}\mu(B_{p,\beta})&=\textstyle\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-\frac{1}{2}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}B_{p,\beta}-\frac{1}{2}-\sum_{i}\mathbb{W}(T_{p}w_{i})\\
&\textstyle=\mathrm{codim}_{\mathbb{C}}\,Y_{m,\beta}-\sum_{i}\mathbb{W}(T_{p}w_{i}),\end{split}

where
w_{i}
are the weights
of the convergence point
y_{0}
of an arbitrary point
y\in B_{p,\beta}.

For the hypersurface
\Sigma=\{c^{\prime}(H)=1\}
corresponding to the period
T_{p}=1
:

(65)

\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\mu(\Sigma)=-2\mu,\qquad\textrm{and }\qquad\mu(\{T_{p}=N\})=-2N\mu\quad\textrm{ for }N\in\mathbb{N},

where
\mu=\sum w_{i}
is the Maslov index of the
S^{1}
-action (
Section

2.4
).

Proof.

We are refining the computation from
C.9
, and the proof idea is very similar to
[
Oan04
, Sec.3.3]
. In short: the difference between the Hamiltonian flows of
H_{\lambda}
and
T_{p}H
causes a symplectic shear which gives the
-\frac{1}{2}
term in (
64
), and the Robbin–Salamon index of
B_{p,\beta}
with respect to the Hamiltonian
T_{p}H
will yield the
\sum_{i}\mathbb{W}(T_{p}w_{i})
-part, like in the proof of
[
RŽ23
, Prop.5.2]
.

For an arbitrary point
y\in B_{p,\beta},
recall the orthogonal splittings
T_{y}Y=\langle X_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\rangle\oplus\langle X_{H_{\lambda}}\rangle\oplus\xi
and
T_{y}\Sigma_{p}=X_{H_{\lambda}}\oplus\xi
from (
60
). Using this splitting, define the matrices

\small F_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&\\
{\tau c^{\prime\prime}(H)\left\lVert\nabla H\right\rVert^{2}}&1&\\
&&{(\phi_{t}^{H_{\lambda}})_{*}|_{\xi}}\end{bmatrix},\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\chi(t)=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&\\
{\tau c^{\prime\prime}(H)\left\lVert\nabla H\right\rVert^{2}}&1&\\
&&Id_{2d-2}\end{bmatrix}.

We claim that
F_{t}
is the matrix for the linearised flow
(\phi_{t}^{H_{\lambda}})_{*}
of
H_{\lambda}
. Indeed the first column is due to Lemma
5.5
; the second column is immediate; and the rest is due to the fact that the flow
\phi_{t}^{H_{\lambda}}
restricted to
\Sigma_{p}
is equal to
\phi_{t}^{T_{p}H},
hence preserves the metric and thus the orthogonals.

As
\phi_{t}^{H_{\lambda}}
preserves
X_{H_{\lambda}},
it preserves its orthogonal
\xi.

Thus, we have that
(\phi_{t}^{H_{\lambda}})_{*}=\chi(t)\circ(\phi_{t}^{T_{p}H})_{*}.

Letting
\Psi(t)=(\phi_{t}^{T_{p}H})_{*}
as in the proof of
[
CFHW96
, Prop.2.2]
, there is a homotopy

K(s,\,t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\chi(st)\Psi(\frac{2t}{s+1}),&t\leq\frac{s+1}{2}\\
\chi\big((s+2)t-(s+1)\big)\Psi(1),&\frac{s+1}{2}\leq t\end{array}\right.

of paths with fixed ends between the path
(\phi_{t}^{H_{\lambda}})_{*}
and the concatenation of

(\phi_{t}^{T_{p}H})_{*}
and
\chi(t)\circ(\phi_{1}^{T_{p}H})_{*}.

Hence, by Theorem
2.11
.(
2
),(
6
) we have
RS(x,H_{\lambda})=RS(x,T_{p}H)+\frac{1}{2},
where
x=x(t)
denotes
the 1-orbit of
H_{\lambda}
and
T_{p}H,
starting at
y=x(0).

Thus, it remains to compute the index
RS(x,T_{p}H).

We have to pick a symplectic trivialisation of
x^{*}TY
first.
By Lemma
2.7
,
a choice of unitary basis
v_{i}
of tangent vectors at
y_{0}
with weights
w_{i}
yields a symplectic trivialisation
v_{i}(z)
of
\psi_{y}^{*}TY
, where

\psi_{y}
is the canonical holomorphic capping disc from
2.7
.
As
T_{p}=k/m
for coprime
k,m
, the
S^{1}
-orbit
S^{1}\cdot y
is an
m
-fold cover of a closed
S^{1}
-orbit
\hat{y}
of minimal period
1/m
, and
v_{i}(z)
is
\mathbb{Z}/m
-equivariant by
2.7
. In particular,
v_{i}(z)
determines a unitary trivialisation of
\hat{y}^{*}TY
. As
T_{p}=k/m
,
x(t)
is a
k
-fold cover of
\hat{y}
, so
v_{i}(z)
determines a unitary trivialisation of
x^{*}TY
.
Thus we obtain a canonical unitary and symplectic trivialisation for
x^{*}TY
determined by the choice of
v_{i}
.

By continuity of Robbin–Salamon indices, Theorem
2.11
,
RS(x,T_{p}H)=RS(y_{0},T_{p}H)
where
y_{0}=\lim_{t\to 0}t\cdot y
is the convergence point, viewing
y_{0}
as a constant
1
-orbit for the Hamiltonian
T_{p}H
.
On the weight decomposition
T_{y_{0}}Y=\oplus\mathbb{C}_{w_{i}}
the linearisation of the flow of
T_{p}H
is rotation on
\mathbb{C}_{w_{i}}\cong\mathbb{C}
with speed
T_{p}w_{i}
, which contributes
\mathbb{W}(T_{p}w_{i})
to the RS-index by
2.11
(
5
). In summary:

\textstyle RS(x,H_{\lambda})=RS(x,T_{p}H)+\frac{1}{2}\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad RS(x,T_{p}H)=RS(y_{0},T_{p}H)=\sum_{i}\mathbb{W}(T_{p}w_{i}).

The grading of the Morse–Bott manifold
B_{p,\beta}
corresponds to the grading of the orbit that represents the minimum of the auxiliary Morse function
f_{p,\beta}:B_{p,\beta}\to\mathbb{R}.

By the argument in
[
Oan04
, Sec.3.3]
, the Morse term in the Robbin–Salamon index formula for an orbit is equal to half of the signature of the Hessian
\nabla f_{p,\beta}
at the corresponding critical point; thus at a minimum it is equal to
\frac{1}{2}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}B_{p,\beta}.
By our grading conventions from
Section

2.4
, the formula (
64
) follows.

To prove that
\mu(B_{p,\beta})
is even, it suffices to show that
\sum_{i}\mathbb{W}(T_{p}w_{i})\equiv\mathrm{codim}_{\mathbb{C}}\,Y_{m,\beta}\;(\textrm{mod 2})
.
Recall
T_{p}=k/m
, for coprime
k,m
, so

T_{p}w_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
precisely when
m|w_{i}.
By
2.5
,
T_{y_{0}}Y_{m,\beta}=\oplus H_{ma}\subset T_{y_{0}}Y

is summing over the same set of weights.
Finally, by (
32
),
\mathbb{W}(T_{p}w_{i})
is even precisely when
T_{p}w_{i}\in\mathbb{Z},
or equivalently when
m|w_{i},
so
\sum\mathbb{W}(T_{p}w_{i})
modulo
2
is the complex dimension of the vector space complement of
T_{y_{0}}Y_{m,\beta}\subset T_{y_{0}}Y
.

Using (
32
),
\sum\mathbb{W}(2\pi w_{i})=\sum 2w_{i}=2\mu
and
\tfrac{1}{2}(\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\Sigma+1)=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y
, and the final claim follows.
∎

Corollary 6.2
.

Let
T_{p}=\tfrac{k}{m}
with
k,m
coprime, and
T_{p}^{\pm}
non-critical times just above/below
T_{p}
. Let
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
be the minimal component of
Y_{m,\beta}
, and
\mathrm{rk}(Y_{m,\beta})
the rank from
2.15
. Then

(66)

\mu(B_{p,\beta})=\mu_{T_{p}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})-\mathrm{rk}(Y_{m,\beta})=\mu_{T_{p}^{+}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}),

which is the lowest degree in which
H^{*}(B_{p,\beta})[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
is supported, and the top degree is:

\mu(B_{p,\beta})+\dim_{\mathbb{R}}B_{p,\beta}=\mu_{T_{p}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})+\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y_{m,\beta}+\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}-1=\mu_{T_{p}^{-}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})+2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}-1.

For
T_{p}<1
it is supported in degrees
\geq 2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-2\mu-t
.

If
Y
is compatibly-weighted (
2.15
, e.g. all CSRs), then

H^{*}(B_{p,\beta})[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
is supported strictly below degree
t:=\max\{n\in\mathbb{N}:H^{n}(Y)\neq 0\}
.

For a CSR of weight
s=1
, for
T_{p}<1
it is supported in
[0,t-1]
, and for
s=2
in
[-t,t-1]
.

Proof.

Abbreviate
|V|:=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V
.
In each of the two displayed equations in the claim, the first equality
follows by combining Equations (
64
)
and (
35
), using
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}B_{p,\beta}=2|Y_{m,\beta}|-1
;
the second equality uses
2.18
.(
2
) so

\mu_{T_{p}^{+}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=\mu_{T_{p}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})-\text{rk}(Y_{m,\beta})
and

\mu_{T_{p}^{-}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})=\mu_{T_{p}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})+F_{m}^{\alpha}
, and we use

\text{rk}(Y_{m,\beta})=|Y_{m,\beta}|-|\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}|
.
So

\mu_{T_{p}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})+|Y_{m,\beta}|+|\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}|-1=\mu_{T_{p}^{-}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})+2|\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}|-1.

When
Y
is compatibly-weighted,
\mu_{T_{p}^{-}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})\leq\mu_{\alpha}
by

2.16
,
and
\mu_{\alpha}+2|\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}|\leq t
by
Equation

6
, so
\mu_{T_{p}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})+|Y_{m,\beta}|+|\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}|-1\leq t-1.

For the claim about
T_{p}<1
, we use
6.5
:
t-1
is reflected to
2(|Y|-\tfrac{1}{2}-\mu)-(t-1)=2|Y|-2\mu-t
. For CSRs we have
2\mu=s\cdot|Y|
and
t\leq|Y|
, with
t=|Y|
when
s=1
(using
2.19
(
4
)).
∎

6.3.
The columns of the
E_{1}
-page are 1-periodic up to downward
2\mu
-shifts

Corollary 6.3
.

If
T_{p}=T_{p^{\prime}}+N,
for
N\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\mu(B_{p,\beta})=\mu(B_{p^{\prime},\beta})-2N\mu.

Proof.

We use
2.10
(
\mu=\sum w_{i}
),

Equation

64
, and the property
\mathbb{W}(x+N)=\mathbb{W}(x)+2N
:

∎

\textstyle\mu(B_{p^{\prime},\beta})\!-\!\mu(B_{p,\beta})=\textstyle\sum[\mathbb{W}(T_{p^{\prime}}w_{i})-\mathbb{W}(T_{p}w_{i})]=\sum[\mathbb{W}((T_{p}+N)w_{i})-\mathbb{W}(T_{p}w_{i})]=\sum 2Nw_{i}=2N\mu.

Corollary 6.4
.

(Periodicity)
The
E_{1}
-page of the spectral sequence
E(\varphi)^{pq}_{r}
is time-1 periodic up to shifting the rows downwards by
2\mu
rows.
For a weight-
s
CSR,
2\mu=s\cdot\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y
by
2.19
(
4
).

Thus, in order to compute the
E_{1}
-page of
E(\varphi)^{pq}_{r},
it is enough to compute the columns
p
with period
T_{p}\leq 1
. We show now that it suffices to consider
T_{p}\leq\frac{1}{2}
due to a local central symmetry.

6.4.
Local central symmetry in the
E_{1}
-page

In Figure
5
the 4-star symbol lies at the point “
(\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2})
”. In general, the
point “
(\frac{1}{2},n-\frac{1}{2})
”
in the table for the spectral sequence lies on the column with time
T_{p}=\frac{1}{2},
and on the line between rows
n
and
n-1.
If a column with
T_{p}=\frac{1}{2}
does not exist, then it lies on the line between the two columns whose periods are closest to
\frac{1}{2}
.

Corollary 6.5

(Local central symmetry)
.

There is a central symmetry of the sub-block of the
E_{1}
-page of
E(\varphi)^{pq}_{r}
consisting of columns
p
with
0<T_{p}<1,
about the point

\textstyle\left(\frac{1}{2},\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-\frac{1}{2}-\mu\right).

If
Y
is a weight-1 CSR it is the point
(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-\frac{1}{2})
, for a weight-2 CSR it is
(\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2})
.

Proof.

By (
64
), the top-dimensional class
H^{top}(B_{p,\beta})
lies in the row

\textstyle r_{1}:=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}B_{p,\beta}+\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-\frac{1}{2}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}B_{p,\beta}-\frac{1}{2}-\sum_{i}\mathbb{W}(T_{p}w_{i}).

For
T_{p}<1
, in the column
p^{\prime}
such that
T_{p^{\prime}}=1-T_{p}<1
, there is a Morse–Bott manifold
B_{p^{\prime},\beta}
diffeomorphic to
B_{p,\beta}
by Lemma
5.3
.
By (
64
), its bottom-dimensional class
H^{0}(B_{p^{\prime},c})
lies in the row

\textstyle r_{2}:=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-\frac{1}{2}\dim_{\mathbb{R}}B_{p^{\prime},\beta}-\frac{1}{2}-\sum_{i}\mathbb{W}(T_{p}^{\prime}w_{i}).

Thus
\frac{r_{1}+r_{2}}{2}=\frac{1}{2}(2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-1-\sum_{i}(\mathbb{W}(T_{p}w_{i})+\mathbb{W}(T_{p^{\prime}}w_{i}))=\frac{1}{2}(2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-1-\sum_{i}2w_{i})=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-\frac{1}{2}-\mu,
where
we used
\mathbb{W}(-a)=-\mathbb{W}(a)
and
\mathbb{W}(N+a)=2N+\mathbb{W}(a)
.
So
H^{top}(B_{p,\beta})
and
H^{0}(B_{p^{\prime},\beta})
match-up via the
central symmetry about the point
(\frac{1}{2},\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-\frac{1}{2}-\mu).
In the other degrees, the ranks match-up due to Poincaré duality and the fact that
B_{p,\beta}\cong B_{p^{\prime},\beta}.

∎

Corollary 6.6

(Symmetries revisited)
.

The periodicity in
6.4
and local central symmetry in
6.5
also hold for the groups
H_{p,\beta}^{*}[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
from
5.15
.

Proof.

Let
\Sigma_{p}:=\{H=H_{p}\}=\{c^{\prime}(H)=T_{p}\}
.
The periodicity is a consequence of the method from
Section

C.5
for
G:=c(H)
,
P:=H
,
p:=S^{1}
-flow (the flow for
H
). We get
p^{*}I=I
(as the
S^{1}
-flow is pseudoholomorphic) and
p^{*}G=\widetilde{c}(H)-H
, and a bijection of the local Floer complex for
(\widetilde{c}(H)-H,I)
to the local Floer complex for
(\widetilde{c}(H),I)
, via
x\mapsto p\circ x
that decreases the grading by
2\mu.
More precisely, we work with Morse–Bott Floer complexes (see
Appendix

A
), we use the same auxiliary Morse–Bott functions for
B_{T_{p},\beta}
and
B_{1+T_{p},\beta}
, and the above bijection ensures the required bijections of the drops in the cascades. So the generators and Morse–Bott Floer differentials are identified for the local Morse–Bott Floer complexes for
\Sigma_{p}
and
\Sigma_{p^{\prime}}
where
T_{p^{\prime}}=1+T_{p}
.

We now compare the local Floer cohomologies of a neighbourhood
N
of
c^{\prime}(H_{p})=T_{p}
and a neighbourhood
N^{\prime}
of
c^{\prime}(H_{p^{\prime}})=T_{p^{\prime}}:=1-T_{p}
.
Using the flow
\psi_{t}
of
\tfrac{\nabla H}{\|\nabla H\|^{2}}
we can identify
N\to N^{\prime}
.
The flow yields a family
\psi_{t}^{*}I,\psi_{t}^{*}\omega
of data on
N
, telling us how to deform the data
I,\omega
so that local Floer cohomology of
N
can be identified with that of
N^{\prime}
if we were using the same Hamiltonian under the identification
N\to N^{\prime}
(which we are not). On
N
we are using the Hamiltonian
c(H)
, and using a Morse–Bott–Floer model involving auxiliary Morse functions
f_{\beta}:B_{p,\beta}\to\mathbb{R}
(
Appendix

A
).
The local Morse–Bott Floer cohomology of
(N,c(H))
is invariant up to isomorphism under changing the precise choice of
c
provided that we keep, say, our Morse–Bott manifolds of
1
-orbits fixed and ensure the slope
c^{\prime}(H_{p})=T_{p}
and
c^{\prime\prime}(H_{p})>0
there, call this “
T_{p}
-data”.
If we instead have
c^{\prime\prime}(H_{p})<0
, call it “
T_{p}
-antidata”.
Observe that the local Morse–Bott Floer cohomology for
T_{p}
-data
c(H)
and for
T_{p}
-antidata
\widetilde{c}(H)
are isomorphic
44
44

44

This is because if we locally concatenate
c(H)
with
\widetilde{c}(H)
, one can homotope the concatenation to a Hamiltonian with a slope less than
T_{p}
whose local Floer cohomology therefore vanishes. Thus the (degree one) Morse–Bott Floer differential from the complex for the
T_{p}
-data must map quasi-isomorphically to the complex for the
T_{p}
-antidata.
via a grading shift
d\mapsto d+1
. This shift by
+1
can also be explained in terms of the shear in (
102
) (see also the proof of
6.1
): for the
T_{p}
-data we get a contribution of
-\tfrac{1}{2}
to the grading, whereas for the
T_{p}
-antidata we get
+\tfrac{1}{2}.

It remains to compare slope
(1-T_{p})
-data on
N
and slope
T_{p}
-data on
N
. By the shifting argument at the beginning of the proof, slope
(1-T_{p})
-data has the same local Floer cohomology as
-T_{p}
-data after changing degrees by
d\to d+2\mu
.
Suppose
\gamma(H)
is the
-T_{p}
-data obtained.

We have a canonical duality isomorphism
of local Morse–Bott Floer complexes:

BHF^{*}_{loc}(\Sigma_{p},\gamma(H),f_{\beta})\cong BHF^{2\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-*}_{loc}(\Sigma_{p},-\gamma(H),-f_{\beta}),

which sends
x\in\mathrm{Crit}(f_{\beta})
to
x\in\mathrm{Crit}(-f_{\beta})
, and the Morse--Bott Floer differentials coincide.
45
45

45

We reverse the flow-direction of ledges, and we reverse both coordinates for drops,
u(s,t)\to u(-s,-t)
.

Note that
-\gamma(H)
is
-T_{p}
-antidata, since
-\gamma^{\prime\prime}(H_{p})<0
. As explained previously, we can identify this with the local Morse–Bott Floer cohomology of
T_{p}
-data after index shifting
d\to d-1.

In conclusion, going from
(1-T_{p})
-data to
T_{p}
-data arose from quasi-isomorphisms at local Morse–Bott Floer chain level subject to shifting degrees by

d\to d+2\mu\to 2\,\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-(d+2\mu)\to 2\,\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-(d+2\mu)-1=2\,\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-d-2\mu-1,

which is a reflection of
d
about
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y-\tfrac{1}{2}-\mu
of
d
in
\mathbb{R}
, as required in
6.5
.
∎

6.5.
Bounds on where the
E_{1}
-page is supported

Definition 6.7
.

The
block number
is the integer part
N:=\lfloor T_{p}\rfloor
of the period.

Pillars

are columns of
E(\varphi)_{1}^{pq}
for integer periods
T_{p}
: the
0
-pillar is
H^{*}(Y)
, the
N
-pillar is
H^{*}(\Sigma)[2N\mu]
for
N\geq 1
.

The
N
-block
is the sub-block of
E(\varphi)_{1}^{pq}
of classes with block number
N
excluding the pillars, so
N<T_{p}<N+1
.
A pillar/block is
supported
in
[a,b]
if it vanishes for grading values outside of
[a,b]
.

Lemma 6.8
.

Suppose all
\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})\leq\mu_{\alpha}
(e.g. any compatibly-weighted
Y
,
2.15
, e.g. CSRs).

Let
y:=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y
and
t:=\max\{n\in\mathbb{N}:H^{n}(Y)\neq 0\}.

Then
t\leq 2y-2
is even, and we have:

lower bound on support

upper bound on support

0
-pillar

0

t

0
-block

2y-2\mu-t

t-1

1
-pillar

-2\mu

2y-1-2\mu

1
-block

2y-4\mu-t

t-1-2\mu

N
-pillar

-2N\mu

2y-1-2N\mu

N
-block

2y-2(N+1)\mu-t

t-1-2N\mu

The
0
-block (resp.
1
-pillar) after adding
-2N\mu
to the grading is the
N
-block (resp.
(N+1)
-pillar).

The blocks have a local central symmetry analogous to the
0
-block (
6.5
).

For a weight-
s
CSR we have
2\mu=sy
and
t\leq y
(with
t=y
for
s=1
), so:

For
s=2
: the
0
-block is supported in
[-t,t-1]
; the
1
-pillar in
[-2y,-1]
.

For
s=1
:
0
-block
\subset[0,y-1]
;
1
-pillar
\subset[-y,y-1]
;
1

\subset[-y,-1]
;
2
