\subset[-2y,-1]
.

Proof.

In
Equation

6
,
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
is a closed
I
-pseudoholomorphic submanifold, so it is even-dimensional, and
\mu_{\alpha}
is even, so
t
is even (and
t\neq 2y
as
Y
is non-compact), so
t\leq 2y-2
.
For
N\geq 1
, the
N
-th pillar arises from an energy-spectral sequence starting with

H^{*}(\Sigma)[2N\mu]
, so is supported in
[-2N\mu,-2N\mu+\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\Sigma]
, and
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\Sigma=2y-1
.
The rest follows by
6.2
and
6.4
.
∎

6.6.
What classes in the
E_{\geq 1}
-pages can hit
H^{*}(Y)

By
6.4
and
5.18
, for the purposes of computing the filtration
F^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
we only need to consider columns up to a certain time:

Corollary 6.9
.

Suppose all
\mu_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})\leq\mu_{\alpha}
(e.g. any compatibly-weighted
Y
,
2.15
, e.g. CSRs).

The only
N
-blocks that can potentially hit the 0-pillar via iterated differentials satisfy
N\leq t/2\mu
.
The only
N
-pillars that can potentially hit the 0-pillar via iterated differentials satisfy
N\leq y/\mu
.
For a weight-
s
CSR, those two conditions become

N\leq t/sy\leq 1/s
and
N\leq 2/s

respectively.

Proof.

This will follow by
6.8
. For the
N
-block, the highest possible grading
t-1-2N\mu
is strictly less than
-1
if
N>t/2\mu
, so the iterated differentials (which increase the total grading by
1
) from such an
N
-block cannot hit the
0
-pillar. Higher numbered blocks lie in lower gradings, so the same argument applies. Similarly, for the
N
-pillar we have
2y-1-2N\mu<-1
if
N>y/\mu
.
∎

When
H^{*}(Y)
lies in even degrees, the only non-zero iterated differentials go from odd-numbered rows to even rows (as illustrated by the figures in
Section

7
):

Proposition 6.10
.

Suppose
H^{*}(Y
) lies in even degrees (
\Leftrightarrow
all
H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha})
lie in even degrees), e.g. any CSR.
Then from the
E_{1}
-page onwards, the spectral sequence
E(\varphi)^{pq}_{r}
splits horizontally into a direct sum of two-row spectral sequences. Also, on any given odd degree class
x
of the
E_{1}
-page, some iterated differential is eventually injective on
x
, so kills some class in a column strictly to the left of
x
.

Proof.

In this proof, write
E_{r}(H_{\lambda})
to mean the
E_{r}
-page for the spectral sequence for
H_{\lambda}
.
By induction on the number of the column to the right of the
0
-pillar, we will prove that: all iterated differentials
d_{j}
vanish on even classes, and
for any odd degree class
x
we have
d_{1}(x)=0,

d_{2}(x)=0
,
\ldots
,
d_{r-1}(x)=0
,
d_{r}(x)\neq 0
for some
r\geq 1
possibly equal to
1
, in particular
x
kills some
non-trivial class in a column strictly to the left of
x
via an iterated differential.
The initial step of the induction holds since
the
0
-pillar is supported in even degrees, and the columns to the left of the
0
-pillar are zero.
Suppose that
the induction holds up to columns with
T_{p}<b
,
and consider
b<\lambda
with
E_{1}(H_{b})
and
E_{1}(H_{\lambda})
differing in just one column, so a column arising for time
b<T_{p}<\lambda
. We call “new” that column and its classes, and “old” the columns to the left and their classes.
If there is an odd new class
x\neq 0
with
d_{j}(x)=0
for all
j\geq 1
,
then
[x]
would be a non-trivial class in all
E_{j}(H_{\lambda})
(as all columns in
E_{1}(H_{\lambda})
to the right of the new column are zero, so
x
is not in the image of any
d_{j}
). This would yield a non-trivial odd class in
HF^{*}(H_{\lambda})
, contradicting

Equation

9
.
Now let
y
be an even new class; we want to show
d_{j}(y)=0
for all
j\geq 1
.
By contradiction, consider the smallest
j\geq 1
for which that fails. Then
d_{j}(y)=x
kills an odd old class
[x]
that survived to
E_{j}(H_{b})
.
By the inductive hypothesis for
H_{b}
,
d_{j-1}(x)=\cdots=d_{r-1}(x)=0
,
d_{r}(x)\neq 0
(with
r\geq j
possibly equal to
j
).
The iterated differentials of
x
for
H_{b}
agree with those for
H_{\lambda}
, indeed
(E_{i}(H_{b}),d_{i})\subset(E_{i}(H_{\lambda}),d_{i})
is a subcomplex.
46
46

46

by the
F
-filtration, for
x\in E_{*}(H_{b})
,
d_{i}(x)
only involves Floer trajectories trapped in the region where
H_{\lambda}=H_{b}
.

The exact class

d_{j}(y)\in E_{r}(H_{\lambda})
is zero so
d_{r}(d_{j}(y))=d_{r}(x)\in E_{r}(H_{\lambda})
must be zero. As
d_{r}(x)
is not zero in
E_{r}(H_{b})
, there is a new class
w
which survives to
E_{r}(H_{\lambda})
with
d_{r}(w)=d_{r}(x)
. This is impossible:
d_{r}(w)
cannot reach the column where
d_{r}(x)
lives (
w
lies in a column strictly to the right of
x
).
∎

6.7.
Hitting the unit

Using
5.18
and results from our previous paper, we can deduce which class in the spectral sequence kills the unit in
certain special cases of CSRs.

Proposition 6.11
.

The unit
1\in H^{0}(Y)
in the 0-pillar gets killed by

(1)

The top class of the 2-pillar, when
Y
is a weight-1 CSR.

(2)

The top class of the 1-pillar, when
Y
is a weight-2 CSR and its minimum
47
47

47

The minimum of the
S^{1}
-moment map.

is
\mathfrak{F}_{\min}=\{point\}.

Proof.

Follows directly from
5.18
and

\mathscr{F}_{2^{-}}=H^{\geq 2}(Y),

\mathscr{F}_{2}(Y)=H^{*}(Y),

[
RŽ23
, Ex.1.45]
for the (
1
) part;

\mathscr{F}_{1}=H^{*}(Y)
,
1\notin\mathscr{F}_{1^{-}}

[
RŽ23
, Cor.7.22]
for the (
2
) part.
∎

6.8.
Computing the
E_{1}
-columns arising from hypersurface sections of vector bundles

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
M
be an open manifold, and
H:M\to\mathbb{R}
an exhausting function whose critical locus is compact.
Let
\Sigma_{c}=H^{-1}(c)
denote the level set.
Then, for sufficiently large
c\in\mathbb{R}
, the cohomology
H^{*}(\Sigma_{c})
is independent of
c
and the choice of
H
. In particular, if
M
is a vector bundle and
S(M)
a sphere subbundle,
H^{*}(\Sigma_{c})\cong H^{*}(S(M))
for large
c
.

Proof.

For large
c
, the critical locus of
H
is contained in
H<c
(as
H
is exhausting).
Thus, for large
c,c^{\prime}
we have a diffeomorphism
\Sigma_{c}\cong\Sigma_{c^{\prime}}
defined by the flow of
\nabla H/\|\nabla H\|^{2}
.
Denote by
\Sigma_{c,\infty}=H^{-1}[c,\infty)\subset M
the subset at infinity diffeomorphic to
\Sigma_{c}\times[0,\infty)
obtained by flowing for positive time with
\nabla H/\|\nabla H\|^{2}
. Thus
H^{*}(\Sigma_{c,\infty})\cong H^{*}(\Sigma_{c})
(for large
c
).
Given two exhausting functions
H^{\prime}
and
H^{\prime\prime}
, respectively with level sets
\Sigma_{c}^{\prime}
,
\Sigma_{c}^{\prime\prime}
, we can choose
c
-values to get nested sets

\Sigma_{c^{\prime},\infty}^{\prime}\subset\Sigma_{c^{\prime\prime},\infty}^{\prime\prime}\subset\Sigma_{C^{\prime},\infty}^{\prime}\subset\Sigma_{C^{\prime\prime},\infty}^{\prime\prime},

where the
c
-values are large so that the critical loci of
H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime}
lie in lower sublevel sets.
The composite of the first two inclusions is isotopic to a diffeomorphism (by using the flow by
\nabla H^{\prime}/\|\nabla H^{\prime}\|^{2}
). Similarly, for the composite of the last two inclusions (using
H^{\prime\prime}
). Thus those two composite maps induce isomorphisms on the cohomology level. Thus
H^{*}(\Sigma_{c^{\prime\prime},\infty}^{\prime\prime})\leftarrow H^{*}(\Sigma_{C^{\prime},\infty}^{\prime})
is both injective and surjective, so it is an isomorphism. By the first part, up to isomorphism the choices of large
c^{\prime\prime},C^{\prime}
do not matter.
The second claim follows: let
g
be the fibre-wise metric on
M
defining
S(M)
, then
H:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
H(b,\xi)=g(\xi,\xi)
is an exhausting
function and
S(M)
is a level set.
∎

Applying
6.12
to
M=Y_{m,\beta}
and
H|_{Y_{m,\beta}}^{-1}(H_{p})=\Sigma_{p}\cap Y_{m,\beta}=B_{p,\beta}\subset Y_{m,\beta},
we get:

(1)

If
Y_{m,\beta}=\mathcal{H}_{m}
is a torsion bundle (
2.5
)
over
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha},
then

H^{*}(B_{p,\beta})\cong H^{*}(S(\mathcal{H}_{m})),

which is calculable by considering the Gysin sequence for the sphere bundle
S(\mathcal{H}_{m})\to\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
.

(2)

More generally, if
\mathrm{Core}(Y_{m,\beta})=Y_{m,\beta}\cap\mathrm{Core}(Y)
is a smooth manifold (not necessarily fixed under the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action), and
Y_{m,\beta}
is a smooth vector bundle over it,

H^{*}(B_{p,\beta})\cong H^{*}(S(Y_{m,\beta})),

which is calculable by considering the Gysin sequence for the sphere bundle
S(Y_{m,\beta})\to\mathrm{Core}(Y_{m,\beta})
.

Remark 6.13
.

In all the examples we consider in
Section

7
, only (1) and (2) occurred for all
Y_{m,\beta}
.

6.9.
Computing the integer-time columns of the
E_{1}
-page

When
H^{*}(Y)
is supported in degrees
[0,\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y]
, we can compute the time-1 column of the
E_{1}
-page,
H^{*}(\Sigma)[2\mu]
.
By
[
RŽ23
]
, this assumption holds if
\mathrm{Core}(Y)\subset Y
has the homotopy type of a CW complex of dimension
\leq\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y
.
This holds for example for all CSRs and
Moduli spaces of Higgs bundles.

Proposition 6.14
.

Assume that the Novikov field
\mathbb{K}
is defined over a base field
\mathbb{B}
of characteristic
0
. Let
n=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y
, so
\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\Sigma=2n-1
.
Suppose that
H^{*}(Y)
is supported in degrees
0\leq*\leq n
. Then

\small H^{k}(\Sigma)\cong\begin{cases}H^{2n-1-k}(Y)&\ $k\geq n+1,$\\
H^{n-1}(Y)\oplus\ker\psi&\ $k=n,\ n-1,$\\
H^{k}(Y)&\ $k\leq n-2$.\end{cases}

where
\psi:H_{n}(Y)\rightarrow H_{n}^{lf}(Y)\cong H_{n}(Y,Y_{\infty})

is the natural map for the pair
(Y,Y_{\infty})
,
and
Y_{\infty}:=H^{-1}[H_{p},\infty)
is the outside of
\Sigma_{p}=\partial Y_{\infty}
.

In particular, if the intersection form
H_{n}(Y)\times H_{n}(Y)\rightarrow\mathbb{K}
vanishes, then
\ker\psi=H_{n}(Y)\cong H^{n}(Y)
,
and if it is non-degenerate, then
\ker\psi=0.

Proof.

Let
M:=\{H\leq H_{p}\}
, so
\partial M=\Sigma_{p}\cong\Sigma
. Thus
Y
is homotopy equivalent to
M
, as it deformation retracts onto
M
via the (rescaled)
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-action.
We have the long exact sequence for the pair
(M,\partial M)

(67)

\dots\rightarrow H_{k+1}(M,\partial M)\rightarrow H_{k}(\partial M)\rightarrow H_{k}(M)\rightarrow H_{k}(M,\partial M)\rightarrow\cdots

By Poincaré–Lefschetz duality
over the field
\mathbb{K}
,
H_{k}(M,\partial M)\cong H^{2n-k}(M),
hence
for
k\leq n-2

the outside terms in (
67
) vanish by the assumption on
H^{*}(Y)
. Thus
H^{k}(\partial M)\cong H^{k}(M)
(by universal coefficients). By Poincaré duality for the closed orientable
(2n-1)
-manifold
\partial M=\Sigma
and universal coefficients, we have
H^{k}(\Sigma)\cong H_{2n-1-k}(\Sigma)\cong H^{2n-1-k}(\Sigma)
. Thus, for
k\geq n+1
,
H^{k}(\Sigma)\cong H^{2n-1-k}({Y})
.

Using (
67
) for
k=n
and
k=n-1
:

H_{n+1}(M)=0\to H_{n+1}(M,\partial M)\rightarrow H_{n}(\partial M)\rightarrow H_{n}(M)\xrightarrow{\psi}H_{n}(M,\partial M)\rightarrow H_{n-1}(\partial M)\rightarrow H_{n-1}(M).

As
H_{n}(\partial M)/H_{n+1}(M,\partial M)\cong\ker\psi
, the claim about
\psi
follows by using universal coefficients, and the Poincaré duality isomorphisms
H_{n+1}(M,\partial M)\cong H^{n-1}(M)
and

H^{n-1}(\partial M)\cong H_{n}(\partial M)
(
\cong H^{n}(\partial M)
).

The last claim uses the following relation between intersection pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and Poincaré duality:

(68)

Corollary 6.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a weight-
s
CSR,
n:=dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}
. The cohomology of its hypersurface
\Sigma
is

\small H^{k}(\Sigma)\cong\begin{cases}H^{2n-1-k}(\mathfrak{M})&\ $k\geq n+1,$\; (in particular vanishes if $k$ is even)\\
0&\ $k=n-1,n$\\
H^{k}(\mathfrak{M})&\ $k\leq n-2$, \; (in particular vanishes if $k$ is odd).\end{cases}

In terms of the
H_{p,\beta}^{*}[-\mu(B_{p,\beta})]
from
5.15
,

H_{N}^{*}[-\mu(B_{N})]\cong H^{*}(\Sigma)[Ns\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}]
for
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

By
2.19
(
7
)(
8
),

H^{*}(\mathfrak{M})
is supported in even degrees only, with

0\leq*\leq\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}
, and its intersection form is non-degenerate.
Thus
6.14

applies.
The last claim follows because the energy-spectral sequence collapses on the
E_{1}
-page since there can be no vertical differentials for grading reasons, as there is a
0
-gap between any two degrees in which
H^{*}(\Sigma)
is supported.
We also used that
2\mu=s\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}
(
2.19
(
4
)).
∎

Proposition 6.16
.

Let
(\mathfrak{M},\varphi)
be a weight-1 CSR. Then the
\varphi
-filtration satisfies

\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda<1}\subsetneq H^{\geq 2}(\mathfrak{M}),\qquad\quad\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{1}=H^{\geq 2}(\mathfrak{M}),\qquad\quad\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{2}=H^{*}(\mathfrak{M}),

and
H^{top}(\Sigma)[\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}]
kills the last surviving
H^{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}}(\mathfrak{M})
class.

Proof.

By
6.9
, only the
0
- and
1
-blocks, and the
1
- and
2
-pillars can hit
H^{*}(\mathfrak{M})
. So
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{2}=H^{*}(\mathfrak{M})
.
The
1
-block and
2
-pillar lie in grading
\leq-1
, so

H^{\geq 2}(\mathfrak{M})\subset\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{1}
.
It remains to show that
H^{0}(\mathfrak{M})
was neither killed by the
0
-block nor by the
1
-pillar. For the
0
-block, this is immediate since it lies in grading
\geq 0
.
The
1
-pillar cannot kill the unit by
6.15
, as

H^{*}(\Sigma)[\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}]
in degree
-1

equals

H^{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}-1}(\Sigma),
which vanishes
by
6.15
.
For the final claim, the top class of the
1
-pillar is in grading
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}-1
, and it must kill a class to the left by
6.10
.
By
6.8
, the
0
-block lies in grading
\leq\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}-1
. So it must kill a class in the
0
-pillar in
H^{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{M}}(\mathfrak{M})
.
∎

7.
Examples of spectral sequences

We will illustrate the Morse–Bott–Floer spectral sequences
E(\varphi)^{pq}_{r}
from
Section

5
for three classes of CSRs, and for Higgs moduli spaces.
Via
5.18
, we deduce the rank-wise filtrations
\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{\varphi}
of their cohomologies, and then if needed use
Equation

10
to get the actual cohomology classes.

Remark 7.1
.

Conventions.
We assume that all cohomologies are computed over the Novikov field
\mathbb{K}
, over a base field
\mathbb{B}
(
Section

2.3
) of
characteristic zero
, so
2.19
(
7
) and

6.14

apply. Interesting torsion phenomena arise in non-zero characteristics, but it is outside the scope of this paper.
We continue with the conventions from
Section

6.1
and
5.17
, but in the Figures we relabel
B_{p}
to
B_{T_{p}}
, as
T_{p}
helps indicate the torsion subgroup of the Morse–Bott submanifold.
In spectral sequence pictures, we include just the columns that may hit non-zero classes in the
0
-column via iterated differentials, as these determine
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}.
Sometimes we include additional columns beyond that. We will frequently use the
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
-duality, (
36
).
We abbreviate a field summand
\mathbb{K}
in degree
m
by

\mathbb{K}_{m}:=\mathbb{K}[-m].

7.1.
Springer resolutions

Given a decomposition
p=(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})
of
n,
48
48

48

Meaning integers
p_{i}\geq 0
with
p_{1}+\dots+p_{n}=n.

the associated
p
-partial flag variety is

\mathcal{B}_{p}=\{F:=(0=F_{0}\subset F_{1}\subset\dots\subset F_{n-1}\subset F_{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n})\mid\dim F_{i}/F_{i-1}=p_{i}\}.

If a decomposition
p
is also a partition,
49
49

49

meaning all
p_{i}>0
and they are weakly-descending ordered (
p_{i}\geq p_{i+1}
).
one also defines the adjoint orbit
\mathcal{O}_{p}\subset\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
of nilpotent matrices whose Jordan normal form decomposes into blocks according to
p.
50
50

50

A nilpotent matrix has zero eigenvalues, so
p
completely determines a Jordan normal form with block sizes
p_{i}
.

Given a decomposition
p
, its associated
generalised Springer resolution
is the map

(69)

\nu_{p}:\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{p}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{O}_{p_{+}^{*}}},\qquad\{(e,F)\mid F\in\mathcal{B}_{p},\ eF_{i}\subset F_{i-1}(\forall i=1\dots n)\}\mapsto e.

Here,
\mathcal{O}_{p_{+}^{*}}\subset\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
is the nilpotent adjoint orbit whose matrices have Jordan partition
p_{+}^{*},
where
p_{+}
is a weakly-descending permutation of
p
with
p_{i}=0
removed and
p_{+}^{*}
is its dual partition.
51
51

51

defined e.g. by flipping the corresponding Young diagrams.

It is known that
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{p}\cong T^{*}\mathcal{B}_{p}
(with projection to
\mathcal{B}_{p}
given by
(e,F)\mapsto F
). Moreover, the map (
69
) is known to be a
weight-1 CSR
, with the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
\varphi
that contracts the fibres on
T^{*}\mathcal{B}_{p}
and
dilates matrices in
\overline{\mathcal{O}_{p_{+}^{*}}}.

Thus, the action on
T^{*}\mathcal{B}_{p}
is free outside the fixed locus
\mathcal{B}_{p},
hence there are no torsion points, so the spectral sequence
E(\varphi)^{pq}_{r}
is very simple.
The
0
-th column is the cohomology
H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{p})
without a shift, whereas the
1
st and
2
nd columns are the cohomologies of the sphere bundle

H^{*}(S(T^{*}\mathcal{B}_{p}))
with shifts of
d:=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{B}_{p}
and
2d
rows downward, by (
65
) (using
6.12
and
6.4
).
Recall
H^{*}(S(T^{*}\mathcal{B}_{p}))
can be computed by
6.15
.

Example 7.2
.

T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{O}_{11}}.
(See
Figure

3
)

The first three columns
52
52

52

We number the columns starting from
0
, so we mean “the first three columns after the 0-th column.”
are
H^{*}(S(T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}))=H^{*}(\mathbb{R}P^{3})=\mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{3}
(recall
\mathrm{char}(\mathbb{B})=0
), with a shift down by
2
,
4
and
6.

The filtration
\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{\lambda}
is equal to:

0\subset\mathbb{K}_{2}\subset\mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{2}=H^{*}(T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}).

So, writing
H^{*}(T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1})=\mathbb{K}[x]/x^{2},\ |x|=2,
the filtration is
0\subset\langle x\rangle\subset H^{*}(T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}).

Figure 3.
Spectral sequence for
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}

Example 7.3
.

T^{*}\mathcal{B}_{p}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{O}_{p_{+}^{*}}}.

The first three columns are
H^{*}(S(T^{*}\mathcal{B}_{p}))
shifted down by
d=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{B}_{p}
,
2d
and
3d
. Only the first two can hit the 0-th column via iterated differentials. The second column can hit only
H^{0}(T^{*}\mathcal{B}_{p})
. As
H^{*}(S(T^{*}\mathcal{B}_{p}))
has no mid-degree classes, rows
0
and
-1
of the first column are zero, so no class in it can hit
1\in H^{0}(T^{*}\mathcal{B}_{p}),
thus it gets killed by the second column.
Altogether, the filtration is

(70)

0\subset H^{\geq 2}(T^{*}\mathcal{B}_{p})\subset H^{*}(T^{*}\mathcal{B}_{p}).

For example, for the decomposition
p=(1,n)
of
n+1
, we have
\mathcal{B}_{p}=\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n}
,
p_{+}^{*}=(2,1,1,\ldots,1)
(with
n-1
copies of
1
), and (
69
) becomes
T^{*}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{O}_{21\dots 1}}=\{A\in\mathfrak{sl}_{n+1}\mid A^{2}=0,\ \text{rk}(A)=1\}
,
and the filtration is
0\subset H^{\geq 2}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n})\subset H^{*}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n})\cong H^{*}(T^{*}\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n})
.

Remark 7.4
.

Repeating the argument verbatim, the same filtration (
70
) holds for Springer resolutions of
any
type, so
T^{*}(G/P),
for
G
reductive and
P
parabolic. We discussed type A here, as we later consider
Slodowy varieties of type A, in
Section

7.3
.
Notice also that the filtration (
70
) is a consequence of
6.16
, but we compute it here directly for the readers’ convenience.

In order to get filtrations finer than (
70
) on the cohomology of a flag variety,
we can compose the action
\varphi
that contracts the fibres of
T^{*}\mathcal{B}_{p}
with a 1-parameter subgroup
G=(G_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{C}^{*}}
of the maximal torus
T\leq GL(n,\mathbb{C}),

as it induces a holomorphic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on
T^{*}\mathcal{B}_{p}
that commutes with
\varphi.

The subgroups
G\leq T
form a lattice isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{n},
via the map

h=(h_{1},\dots,h_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\mapsto t^{h}:=\text{diag}(t^{h_{1}},\dots,t^{h_{n}})\leq T.

However, to keep the composition of
\varphi
and
G
contracting, it reduces the choice to a finite convex subset of this lattice.
More precisely, fixing an integer
s>0
there is a finite convex subset

K_{s}:=\{h\mid t^{h}\varphi^{s}\text{ is a contracting action on }T^{*}\mathcal{B}_{p}\}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

It is not hard to prove that

K_{1}=\emptyset
,
K_{s}\subset K_{s^{\prime}}
for
s<s^{\prime}
and
\cup_{s}K_{s}=\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

Moreover, we obtain:

Proposition 7.5
.

Given any integer
s>0,
there is a map

K_{s}\rightarrow\{\text{filtrations of }H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{p})\text{ by cup-ideals}\},\ h\mapsto\mathscr{F}_{\lambda}^{t^{h}\varphi^{s}}.

Example 7.6
.

T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{O}_{21\dots 1}}
, with a twisted action.
(See
Figure

4
)

Let
h=(0,1,\dots,n-1).

The action
t^{h}\varphi^{n}|_{\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}}=t^{h}\curvearrowright\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}
is
[z_{0},tz_{1},\dots,t^{n-1}z_{n-1}].
The fixed points are
\mathfrak{F}_{i}:=[0,\dots,1,\dots,0],\ i=0,\dots,n-1,
where
1
is in the
i
-th position.
There is a
(t\rightarrow\infty)

\mathbb{C}^{*}
-flowline from
\mathfrak{F}_{i}
to
\mathfrak{F}_{j}
if and only if
i<j,
and it is
\mathbb{Z}/(j-i)
-torsion.
This way we get the weight decompositions of
\mathfrak{F}_{i}
in
\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}.
For example, the weights of
\mathfrak{F}_{n-1}
are
(-1,-2,\dots,-(n-1)).

To get the full decompositions in
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}
, we use the
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
-pairing

H_{k}\leftrightarrow H_{n-k}
((
36
), here the action has weight
s=n
).
This yields additional weights

(n+1,n+2,\dots,2n-1)
to
\mathfrak{F}_{n-1}.

Similarly,
53
53

53

One can also compute the weights in coordinates. Near
\mathfrak{F}_{i}\subset\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
we have local coordinates
w_{j}=z_{j}/z_{i}
, for
j=0,1,\ldots,\widehat{i},\ldots,n-1
, whose weights are
(-i,-i+1,\ldots,-1,\widehat{0},1,\ldots,n-1-i)
.
Those induce opposite weights on the cotangent fibre basis
dw_{j}
, but we also need to add weight
n
coming from
\varphi^{n}
, so:
(n+i,n+i-1,\ldots,n+1,\widehat{n},n-1,\ldots,i+1)

\mathfrak{F}_{i}
: they yield torsion submanifolds that leave the core. For example, for
n=4

\mathfrak{F}_{0}
:
((1,2,3),(3,2,1))
,
\mathfrak{F}_{1}
:
((-1,1,2),(5,3,2))
,
\mathfrak{F}_{2}

((-2,-1,1),(6,5,3))
,
\mathfrak{F}_{3}

((-3,-2,-1),(7,6,5))
. For example,
\mathfrak{F}_{1}
is contained in a rank one
3
-torsion bundle,
\mathfrak{F}_{0}

3
-torsion bundle,
and
\mathfrak{F}_{2}
is contained in a rank two
3
-torsion bundle caused by the weights
3,6
(using that
\mathfrak{F}_{0},\mathfrak{F}_{1},\mathfrak{F}_{2}
are all
3
-minimal). This causes the
B_{1/3}
column to contain the cohomology of a copy of
S^{1}
and two copies of
S^{3}
.

we deduce the weights for the other
\mathfrak{F}_{i},
and we observe that for each integer
k\in[n+1,2n-1]
and
i\in[k-n,n-1]
, there is a 1-dimensional
k
-weight space in
\mathfrak{F}_{i}
.
Each one of them yields a
\mathbb{Z}/k
-torsion line bundle
\mathcal{H}_{k}\rightarrow\mathfrak{F}_{i}

in
T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{n-1},
whose corresponding Morse–Bott submanifold is
B_{1/k,i}\cong S^{1}.

By (
64
),

\mu(B_{1/k,i})=2(k-n-1).

Thus, for
c=1,\dots,n-1,
the
c
-th column or equivalently, time
-\frac{1}{2n-c}
column of the spectral sequence consists
of
c
copies of
H^{*}(S^{1})[2n-2-2c],

and a certain linear combination of its
H^{1}(S^{1})
-classes is killing the
H^{2n-2c}(\mathbb{C}P^{n-1})
class in the
0
-th column.
There are no other columns between these, as
\frac{1}{n+1}<\frac{2}{2n-1}.

Thus, these columns kill all the cohomology except for the unit
1=H^{0}(\mathfrak{F}_{0})=H^{0}(\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}),
which due to (
10
)
gets killed by
time
t\geq\frac{1}{n-1}
columns.
54
54

54

as the weights of
\mathfrak{F}_{0}
are
(1,2,\dots,n-1),(n-1,\dots,2,1)
.

In fact, a careful estimate of indices
implies that it has to be killed precisely at time-
\frac{1}{n-1}.
55
55

55

By
[
RŽ23
, Lemma 5.17]
, for any weight-
s
CSR, by time-

all
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
go below grading 0 if
h_{1}^{\alpha}+\sum_{j\geq 2}h_{(s-1)j}^{\alpha}\neq 0
: in our case

, and for all

except

, and for

we have

.

Altogether, the filtration on
H^{*}(T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{n-1})=\mathbb{K}[x]/x^{n},\ |x|=2
is

0\subset\mathscr{F}_{\frac{1}{2n-1}}=\langle x^{n-1}\rangle\subset\mathscr{F}_{\frac{1}{2n-2}}=\langle x^{n-2}\rangle\subset\cdots\subset\mathscr{F}_{\frac{1}{n+1}}=\langle x\rangle\subset\mathscr{F}_{\frac{1}{n-1}}=H^{*}(T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{n-1}),

so the Schubert cells in

\mathbb{C}P^{n}
are
killed by certain (linear combinations of) torsion

-orbits.

7.2.
Resolutions of ADE singularities

The CSRs of the lowest dimension are resolutions of ADE singularities.
56
56

56

also known as simple surface singularities, ADE singularities, Kleinian singularities or rational double points.
They are the minimal resolutions of quotient singularities

\pi_{\Gamma}:X_{\Gamma}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma,

for any finite subgroup
\Gamma\leq SL(2,\mathbb{C}).
Such groups
\Gamma
(up to conjugation) are in bijection with ADE Dynkin graphs
Q_{\Gamma}
. This
McKay graph

Q_{\Gamma}
57
57

57

more precisely, the quiver obtained by doubling the edges of
Q_{\Gamma},
and directing them in both ways.
encodes representation-theoretic properties of
\Gamma
.
The core
\pi_{\Gamma}^{-1}(0)
is a union of 2-spheres intersecting transversely according to

Q_{\Gamma}
.
58
58

58

The vertices of
Q_{\Gamma}
correspond to the spheres; an edge in
Q_{\Gamma}
corresponds to the intersection between the spheres.
Any
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on
X_{\Gamma}
preserving the core will induce a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on each sphere, so the intersection points of those spheres must lie in the fixed locus
\mathfrak{F}
.
The Dynkin graph
A_{n}
corresponds to the cyclic group
\mathbb{Z}/(n+1)
; the graph
D_{n}
corresponds to the binary dihedral group
BD_{4(n-2)}
; the graphs
E_{6},E_{7}
and
E_{8}
correspond to the binary tetrahedral, octahedral and icosahedral groups.

Klein showed that the coordinate ring
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma]=\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{C}^{2}]^{\Gamma}
is generated by three polynomials, hence the variety
\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma

embeds into
\mathbb{C}^{3}
via a polynomial map
(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto(f,g,h)\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
(see e.g.
[
Dol07
, Sec.1.2]
). Using
(X,Y,Z)
as coordinates on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, the embeddings for types A and D are:

(71)

\displaystyle\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}/n:\ \ \ (z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto(z_{1}^{n},z_{2}^{n},z_{1}z_{2}),\text{the image is }XY-Z^{n}=0,

\displaystyle\Gamma=BD_{4n}:\ (z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto((z_{1}^{2n}-z_{2}^{2n})z_{1}z_{2},z_{1}^{2n}+z_{2}^{2n},z_{1}^{2}z_{2}^{2}),\text{the image is }X^{2}-Y^{2}Z+4Z^{n+1}=0.

The
standard action
on
X_{\Gamma}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}/\Gamma
is induced from the dilation action

t\cdot(z_{1},z_{2})=(tz_{1},tz_{2})
on
\mathbb{C}^{2}.

It is a
weight-2
conical
59
59

59

i.e. contracting and acting with weight-2 on the holomorphic-symplectic form, see
Section

2.5
.

action.

7.2
is the
A_{1}
-singularity, i.e.
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}/2
case.

We first consider type
A
. In the
\mathbb{C}^{3}
coordinates of (
71
), the standard action is

t\cdot X=t^{k}X,\ \ \text{ }t\cdot Y=t^{k}Y,\ \ \text{ }t\cdot Z=t^{2}Z.

The torsion subvarieties are the (complex) lines
l_{1}=(0,Y,0)
and
l_{2}=(X,0,0),

which have
\mathbb{Z}/k
-isotropy.
The torsion submanifolds in
X_{\mathbb{Z}/k}
are the two
\mathbb{Z}/k
-torsion lines,
\widetilde{l_{1}}
and

\widetilde{l_{2}}
that converge
to the two outer fixed points of the core.
For
p=-1,\dots,-(k-1)
, the intersections
B_{p,i}=\widetilde{l}_{i}\cap\Sigma_{p}
with each slice
\Sigma_{p}

yields two Morse–Bott submanifolds

B_{p,1}\cong B_{p,2}\cong S^{1}.

By Lemma
6.12
, the hypersurface
B_{k}
has
H^{*}(B_{k})\cong H^{*}(S^{3}/(\mathbb{Z}/k))\cong H^{*}(S^{3})\cong\mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{3}.

As the core of
X_{\Gamma}
is an
A_{k-1}
-tree of 2-spheres,

H^{*}(X_{\Gamma})=\mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{2}^{k-1}.

To compute the shifts
\mu(B_{p,i})
we consider the weight decomposition at the
two outer fixed points.
The weight decomposition at any fixed point is
\mathbb{C}_{k}\oplus\mathbb{C}_{2-k},
for some
k
, due to the duality
H_{k}\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\mathbb{C}}}H_{2-k}
induced by the weight-2 action. The next step depends on the parity of
k
.

Example 7.7
.

\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}/k,
for odd
k\geq 3
. (
A_{k-1}
-singularity)

For this parity,
\tfrac{k-1}{2}
is an integer, and by (
64
) the shifts are:

\mu(B_{p,1})=\mu(B_{p,2})=1-\mathbb{W}(\tfrac{-p}{k}k)-\mathbb{W}(\tfrac{-p}{k}(2-k))=1+\mathbb{W}(\tfrac{2p}{k})=\begin{cases}-2,&$p<-\frac{k-1}{2},$\\
0,&$p\geq-\frac{k-1}{2}.$\end{cases}

Hence, the first
\frac{k-1}{2}
columns of
E_{1}
consist of two copies of
H^{*}(S^{1})
; the next
\frac{k-1}{2}
columns consist of two copies of
H^{*}(S^{1})
shifted down by 2; the next column is
H^{*}(S^{3}/(\mathbb{Z}/k))\cong H^{*}(S^{3})
shifted down by
2\mu=s\cdot\dim_{\mathbb{C}}X_{\Gamma}=2\cdot 2=4
. The classes
H^{2}(X_{\Gamma})
in the 0-th column will vanish two by two at each page, and by
6.11
(
2
) the unit
1\in H^{0}(X_{\Gamma})

gets killed precisely by time-1 column, thus vanishes on page
E_{k+1}
.
The filtration is rank-wise

(72)

0\ \subset\ \mathbb{K}_{2}^{2}\ \subset\ \mathbb{K}_{2}^{4}\ \subset\cdots\ \subset\ \mathbb{K}_{2}^{k-1}\ \subset\ \mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{2}^{k-1}=H^{*}(X_{\mathbb{Z}/k}).

Figure

5
shows the spectral sequence for
k=5.

Figure 5.
Spectral sequence for
X_{\mathbb{Z}/5}

Example 7.8
.

\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}/k,
for even
k\geq 2
. (
A_{k-1}
-singularity)

This time the action is even. By considering its square-root of weight-1, the time-
1
column of the
E_{1}
-page is
H^{*}(\Sigma_{p})[2],
and later columns repeat with shift
2\cdot 1=2
. The classes in
H^{2}(X_{\Gamma})
vanish two by two until the last one is hit by the top class of the time-
1
column, and
1\in H^{0}(X_{\Gamma})
is killed
by time-
2
column (thus vanishes on page
E_{k+1}
) due to
6.11
(
1
).
The filtration is rank-wise

0\ \subset\ \mathbb{K}_{2}^{2}\ \subset\ \mathbb{K}_{2}^{4}\ \subset\cdots\ \subset\ \mathbb{K}_{2}^{k-2}\ \subset\ \mathbb{K}_{2}^{k-1}\ \subset\ \mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{2}^{k-1}=H^{*}(X_{\mathbb{Z}/k}).

Figure

6
shows the spectral sequence for
k=6.

Figure 6.

Example 7.9
.

\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}/3,
with a non-standard action. (
A_{2}
-singularity)

We consider the non-standard weight-
2
action induced by

t\cdot X=tX,\text{ }t\cdot Y=t^{2}Y,\text{ }t\cdot Z=tZ.

Here, there is only a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-torsion line
l_{1}=(0,Y,0).
Its resolution
\widetilde{l_{1}}
is a

\mathbb{Z}/2
-torsion line that converges to a fixed point
\mathfrak{F}_{1}
with weight decomposition
\mathbb{C}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{C}_{-1}.
By (
64
) the shift of the Morse–Bott submanifold
B_{1/2}=\widetilde{l_{1}}\cap\Sigma_{1}
is
\mu(B_{1/2})=0.

The time-1 column has shift
\mu(B_{1})=-2.
The later columns we get by periodicity (
6.4
).
As in the previous example, the unit gets killed by
H^{top}(B_{2})
.

Figure 7.
Spectral sequence for
X_{\mathbb{Z}/3}
with a non-standard action.

The spectral sequence is shown on
Figure

7
. From it, we obtain the filtration (rank-wise)

0\ \subset\ \mathscr{F}_{1/2}=\mathbb{K}_{2}\ \subset\ \mathfrak{F}_{1}=\mathbb{K}_{2}^{2}\ \subset\ \mathfrak{F}_{2}=\mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{2}^{2}=H^{*}(X_{\mathbb{Z}/3}),

and together with
Equation

10
we get
\mathscr{F}_{1/2}=H^{0}(\mathfrak{F}_{1})[2]=[S_{2}^{2}]
which determines the filtration
completely

0\ \subset\ \mathscr{F}_{1/2}=[S_{2}^{2}]\ \subset\ \mathscr{F}_{1}=\mathbb{K}_{2}^{2}\ \subset\ \mathscr{F}_{2}=\mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{2}^{2}=H^{*}(X_{\mathbb{Z}/3}),

which is different from the filtration obtained by the standard action in Example
7.7
, for
k=3
:

0\ \subset\ \mathscr{F}_{1/3}=\mathbb{K}_{2}^{2}\ \subset\ \mathscr{F}_{1}=\mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{2}^{2}=H^{*}(X_{\mathbb{Z}/3}).

So these
yield two different Floer-theoretic presentations of the ordinary cohomology of
X_{\mathbb{Z}/3},
and determine two different filtrations by ideals on
H^{*}(X_{\mathbb{Z}/3})
.

Now consider type D. The standard action on a
D_{n+2}
-singularity, in the
\mathbb{C}^{3}
coordinates of (
71
), is

t\cdot X=t^{2n+2}X
,
t\cdot Y=t^{2n}Y
,
t\cdot Z=t^{4}Z.

For convenience, we will consider its weight-1 square root:

(73)

t\cdot X=t^{n+1}X,\ \text{ }t\cdot Y=t^{n}Y,\ \text{ }t\cdot Z=t^{2}Z.

Example 7.10
.

\Gamma=BD_{8k}
(
D_{2k+2}
-singularity),
k\geq 1.

(See
Figure

8
)

For
n=2k
, (
73
) is

t\cdot X=t^{2k+1}X
,
t\cdot Y=t^{2k}Y
,
t\cdot Z=t^{2}Z.

There are three torsion lines

\alpha=(0,Y,0),\ \beta_{1}=(0,2Z^{k},Z),\ \beta_{2}=(0,-2Z^{k},Z),

with respective torsion groups
\mathbb{Z}/2k,\mathbb{Z}/2,\mathbb{Z}/2
.
As it is a weight-1 action, it has a (unique) fixed core component
[
Ž22
]
, which
is the sphere corresponding to the unique trivalent vertex of the graph
D_{2k+2}.
60
60

60

Because that sphere carries three intersection points fixed by the action, so the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action must fix the whole sphere.

The resolutions
\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\beta_{1}},\widetilde{\beta_{2}}
of the torsion lines
\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
are lines again, and they converge to the fixed points of the spheres that correspond to the leaves of the graph
D_{2k+2}
(because these are the leaves of the attraction graph, and by Proposition
2.9
each such leaf has a torsion bundle that converges to it).

Using formula (
64
), as in the previous examples, one can calculate the degree shifts
\mu(B_{p,c})
of the Morse Bott submanifolds
B_{p,c}\cong S^{1}
which are intersections of the lines
\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\beta_{1}},\widetilde{\beta_{2}}
with the slices
\Sigma_{p}
. They all have zero shift, for
T_{p}<1.
Then,
B_{1}=\Sigma_{1}
has a shift down by 2 (as the action has weight 1), and the other columns are determined by Corollary
6.4
.
The filtration is rank-wise:

\displaystyle\text{for }k=1:\ 0\subset\mathbb{K}_{2}^{3}\subset\mathbb{K}_{2}^{4}\subset\mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{2}^{4}=H^{*}(X_{\Gamma}),

\displaystyle\text{for }k\geq 2:\ 0\subset\mathbb{K}_{2}\subset\dots\subset\mathbb{K}_{2}^{k-1}\subset\mathbb{K}_{2}^{k+2}\subset\mathbb{K}_{2}^{k+3}\dots\subset\mathbb{K}_{2}^{2k+1}\subset\mathbb{K}_{2}^{2k+2}\subset H^{*}(X_{\Gamma}).

In both cases, the jump in rank by
3
occurs at the time-
\frac{1}{2}
column, as all three lines are
\mathbb{Z}/2
-torsion.

(

,

).

Example 7.11
.

\Gamma=BD_{8k+4}
(

-singularity),
k\geq 1.
(See

9
)

For
n=2k+1
, (
73
) is

t\cdot X=t^{2k+2}X
,

,

There are three torsion lines

\alpha=(0,Y,0),\ \beta_{1}=(2iZ^{k+1},0,Z),\ \beta_{2}=(-2iZ^{k+1},0,Z),

with respective isotropies
\mathbb{Z}/(2k+1),\mathbb{Z}/2,\mathbb{Z}/2
. Again they give rise to circles
B_{p,\beta}
with zero shifts. The novelty now is the time
\frac{1}{2}
-column: there are

\frac{1}{2}
jumps by rank 2 in degree 2. Thus, the filtration is rank-wise

0\subset\mathbb{K}_{2}\subset\dots\subset\mathbb{K}_{2}^{k}\subset\mathbb{K}_{2}^{k+2}\subset\mathbb{K}_{2}^{k+3}\subset\dots\subset\mathbb{K}_{2}^{2k+3}\subset H^{*}(X_{\Gamma}).

(

,

).

Example 7.12
.

D_{n}
via the attraction graph.

It soon becomes desirable to obtain descriptions of the Morse–Bott manifolds that avoid the coordinate presentations like (
71
), instead using the attraction graph (
2.8
).
We illustrate this for
D_{n}
.
The core consists of a Dynkin tree
D_{n}
, each vertex corresponds to a sphere, and each edge corresponds to an intersection of spheres which in turn is a fixed point of the action,
and the sphere
C
at the unique trivalent vertex is fixed (as it carries three fixed points).
At each fixed point,
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
-duality implies that the weight decomposition is
H_{k}\oplus H_{2-k}
, each summand having
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}=1
.
If a sphere in the core is fixed, it has a weight
k=0
, thus
H_{0}\oplus H_{2}
and the Morse–Bott index is
0
. By
Equation

6
there can be only one fixed sphere, which is
C
, so the other spheres must carry exactly two fixed points (by considering the rank in
Equation

6
).
By
2.7
, it is now easy to write out the weight decompositions at all fixed points, and the attraction graph is again a
D_{n}
-Dynkin graph.
It is preferable to use the square-root of the action, which exists as there is a fixed component
C
of maximal (
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
-Lagrangian) dimension in the core.
This is based on the analytic continuation of the square-root that exists on the torsion bundle

\mathcal{H}_{2}\rightarrow C,

see
[
Ž22
, Lem.5.4. and Prop.5.5]
.
Now, as the square-root is a weight-1 conical action,

\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
-duality gives
H_{k}\oplus H_{1-k}
decompositions.
We have
H_{0}\oplus H_{1}
at
C
, e.g. at intersections points
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}
with the adjacent spheres
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
; at the second fixed points
q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}
of
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
we get
H_{-1}\oplus H_{2}
by
2.7
(
C_{i}
defines a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-flow from
p_{i}
to
q_{i}
).
For
D_{4}
, the
q_{i}
are leaf nodes in the attraction graph, and their
H_{2}
defines a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-torsion line bundle
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, yielding Morse–Bott manifolds
S^{1}
when intersecting with slices.
For
D_{n}
with
n>4
, the same applies to two of those
q_{i}
. The third corresponds to where additional edges arise in the
D_{n}
Dynkin diagram: they correspond to fixed points with
H_{-2}\oplus H_{3}
,
H_{-3}\oplus H_{4}
,
\ldots
,
H_{-(n-3)}\oplus H_{n-2},
yielding a
\mathbb{Z}/(n-2)
-torsion line bundle at the leaf (and so again
S^{1}
Morse–Bott manifolds). So we recover Examples
7.10
and
7.11
without needing (
71
).

7.3.
Slodowy varieties of type A

We will now consider resolutions of ordinary Slodowy varieties of type A, with the Kazhdan
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action.
Recall that the nilpotent cone
\mathcal{N}
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
is the variety of all nilpotent matrices, and it has an associated (ordinary) Springer resolution,

(74)

\nu:\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}\rightarrow\mathcal{N},\ \{(e,F=(0=F_{0}\subset F_{1}\subset\dots\subset F_{n-1}\subset F_{n}=\mathbb{C}^{n}))\mid eF_{i}\subset F_{i-1}\}\mapsto e,

which is a special case of the map (
69
), when
p=(1,\dots,1),
as then
\mathcal{N}=\overline{\mathcal{O}_{n}}.

Recall
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}\cong T^{*}\mathcal{B},
where
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{1\dots 1}
is the ordinary flag variety in
\mathbb{C}^{n}.
The fibres of the map (
74
) are called
Springer fibres
. It is a standard fact that
they are equidimensional projective varieties, whose irreducible components are labelled bijectively by Standard Young Tableaux
61
61

61

Young diagrams filled with numbers that strictly increase row-wise and column-wise.

of the shape that corresponds to the Jordan partition of the nilpotent matrix,
[
Spa76
]
. Given a nilpotent matrix
e\in\mathcal{N},
by the Jacobson-Morozov theorem there is an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triple
(e,f,h)
of nilpotent matrices. Choosing such a triple, one constructs the
Slodowy slice

S_{e}=\{x\in\mathfrak{sl}_{n}\mid[x-e,f]=0\}=e+\text{ker}(\text{ad}f).

The
Slodowy variety
and its resolution are defined as

\mathcal{S}_{e}:=S_{e}\cap\mathcal{N},

\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{e}:=\nu^{-1}(\mathcal{S}_{e}).

On the Slodowy slice and Slodowy variety, the
Kazhdan action
is
t\cdot x=t^{2}\text{Ad}(t^{-h})x.

Its lift
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{e}
is given by

(75)

t\cdot(x,F)=(t^{2}\mathrm{Ad}(t^{-h})x,t^{-h}F).

The map
\nu:\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{e}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{e}
is a
weight-2 CSR
with that action. Its core is the Springer fibre
\mathcal{B}^{e}=\nu^{-1}(e).

Picking a Jordan basis
(v_{i})
for the triple
(e,f,h),
that action on a matrix
x=(X_{ij})
becomes

(76)

t\cdot(X_{ij})=(t^{2+h_{j}-h_{i}}X_{ij}),

whereas the action on flags is induced by the action on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
given by

(77)

t\cdot v_{i}=t^{-h_{i}}v_{i},

where
h=\text{diag}(h_{1},\dots,h_{n})
in the basis
v_{i}.
Given a partition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k}),
we will use the nilpotent element
e_{\lambda}:=\text{diag}(J_{\lambda_{1}},\dots,J_{\lambda_{k}})

given by the standard Jordan canonical form for the partition
\lambda
(so
\lambda_{i}\times\lambda_{i}
Jordan blocks with eigenvalue zero). There is a choice of an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triple
(e_{\lambda},f_{\lambda},h_{\lambda})
such that they have the same Jordan basis and
h_{\lambda}
becomes diagonal in this basis. One can also explicitly compute the corresponding Slodowy slice
S_{\lambda}:=S_{e_{\lambda}}.
For the details of this, see
[
Ž20
, Sec.5.2.1-5.2.2]
,
62
62

62

There is a typo in Sec.5.2.1 of the thesis: the diagonal entries
h_{i}^{k}
of
h_{k}
are
h_{0}^{k},\ldots,h_{k-1}^{k}
, so
i
starts with
i=0
.
in particular
[
Ž20
, Prop.5.2.14.]
for the description for
S_{\lambda}.

One additional observation that we prove here is

Lemma 7.14
.

The fixed points of the Kazhdan action on
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\lambda}
are isolated, or equivalently, its corresponding
S^{1}
-moment map is Morse if

\lambda=(m,n),
and
m\neq n\ \mathrm{(mod\ 2)}.
63
63

63

The “only if” direction is also true, however, we do not prove it here for brevity.
Briefly, if for some
i\neq j
the
\lambda_{i}
,
\lambda_{j}
are of the same parity, one can explicitly (in terms of flags)
construct a Kazhdan-fixed
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\subset\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}.

Proof.

From the description of
h_{\lambda}
given in

[
Ž20
, p.112]
,
it is clear that if
\lambda=(m,n),

m\neq n\ \mathrm{(mod\ 2)},
then
h_{\lambda}
has distinct entries
(explicitly:
h_{\lambda}=(m-1,m-3,\dots,-(m-1),n-1,n-3,\dots,-(n-1))
).
Thus there is no Kazhdan-invariant subspace on
\mathbb{C}^{n},
and all fixed flags on
\mathcal{B}
are isolated.
Now recall that fixed points in
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\lambda}

are contained in its core, which is
\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}\subset\mathcal{B}.

∎

The basic algorithm we use is that we first find the torsion points
\{\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{Z}/{m}}\}_{m\geq 2}
in the Slodowy slice
S_{\lambda},
then we restrict to the nilpotent cone
\mathcal{N}
in order to get the torsion points

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}/{m}}:=\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{Z}/{m}}\cap\mathcal{N}

in the Slodowy variety
\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}.
Then, using the Springer resolution
\nu
, we find the corresponding torsion points in
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\lambda}=\nu^{-1}(\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}),
denoted by
\{\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Z}/{m}}\}_{m\geq 2}.
As the fibres of the resolution
\nu
depend on the nilpotent orbit
\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}
where they are taken, we will keep track of
\gamma
in the intersections

\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}/{m,\gamma}}:=\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{Z}/{m}}\cap\mathcal{O}_{\gamma},\ \ \mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}/{m}}=\sqcup_{\gamma}\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}/{m,\gamma}}.

In addition, when the Kazhdan action is even, we will consider a square-root of it, in order to avoid all points trivially being
\mathbb{Z}/2
-torsion.
We will use the notation
B_{T_{p}}
instead of
B_{p}
for the Morse–Bott submanifolds, so that it agrees with the spectral sequence labelling.

Example 7.15
.

Slodowy variety
\mathcal{S}_{22}.
For
\lambda=(2,2)
,
n=4,
using
[
Ž20
, Prop.5.2.14.]

one gets the description of the Slodowy slice
S_{22}
in
\mathfrak{sl}_{4}
at the nilpotent element
e_{22}=\text{diag}(J_{2},J_{2}).
In particular,
h_{22}=\text{diag}(1,-1,1,-1).

\small S_{22}=\left\{\begin{bmatrix}a&1&c&0\\
m&a&p&c\\
e&0&-a&1\\
x&e&w&-a\end{bmatrix}\middle|\ a,c,e,m,p,x,w\in\mathbb{C}\right\}.

As
t^{h_{22}}=diag(t,t^{-1},t,t^{-1})
, by formula (
76
) the Kazhdan action on
S_{22}
is

(78)

\small t\cdot\begin{bmatrix}a&1&c&0\\
m&a&p&c\\
e&0&-a&1\\
x&e&w&-a\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}t^{2}a&1&t^{2}c&0\\
t^{4}m&t^{2}a&t^{4}p&t^{2}c\\
t^{2}e&0&-t^{2}a&1\\
t^{4}x&t^{2}e&t^{4}w&-t^{2}a\end{bmatrix}.

Thus, we see that the action is even, so we take a square root of it. Then, we only have powers
t^{1}
and
t^{2}
of
t
in equation (
78
), hence we get only
\mathbb{Z}/2
-torsion points in
S_{22},

\small\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}=\left\{\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0&0\\
m&0&p&0\\
0&0&0&1\\
x&0&w&0\end{bmatrix}\middle|m,p,x\in\mathbb{C}\right\}.

Intersecting it with the nilpotent cone
\mathcal{N}=\{A\mid A^{4}=0\},
after some amount of computations one gets the set of
\mathbb{Z}/2
-torsion points in Slodowy variety
\mathcal{S}_{22}:

(79)

\small\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}=\left\{\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0&0\\
m&0&p&0\\
0&0&0&1\\
x&0&w&0\end{bmatrix}\middle|\ m^{2}+px=0,\ m,p,x\in\mathbb{C}\right\}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}/(\mathbb{Z}/2).

One can check that these matrices, except for the zero matrix, all lie in the regular orbit
\mathcal{O}_{4},
hence are bijectively lifted to
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
under the Springer resolution. Denoting by
A_{mpx}
the matrix in equation (
79
), for
(m,p,x)\neq(0,0,0),
its lift is
the Springer fibre
(A_{mpx},F_{mpx}).
Thus the flag

F_{mpx}=(0\subset F_{1}\subset F_{2}\subset F_{3}\subset\mathbb{C}^{4})

is defined by
A_{mpx}F_{i}\subset F_{i-1}.
Let
(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4})
be the basis in which these matrices are given. Then

\displaystyle F_{mpx}

\displaystyle=(0\subset\langle pv_{1}-mv_{3}\rangle\subset\langle pv_{1}-mv_{3},pv_{2}-mv_{4}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{3},pv_{2}-mv_{4}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{4}),\text{when }(p,m)\neq(0,0),

\displaystyle F_{mpx}

\displaystyle=(0\subset\langle mv_{1}+xv_{3}\rangle\subset\langle mv_{1}+xv_{3},mv_{2}+xv_{4}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{3},mv_{2}+xv_{4}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{4}),\text{when }(m,x)\neq(0,0).

Using (
77
), we see that the Kazhdan action acts on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
by

t\cdot(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4})=(t^{-1}v_{1},tv_{2},t^{-1}v_{3},tv_{4}),

thus the induced action makes the flag
F_{mpx}
fixed. Hence, the fixed locus to which
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
converges (when
t\rightarrow 0
) is a
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
in the core:

\mathfrak{F}_{mpx}:=(0\subset\langle\alpha v_{1}+\beta v_{3}\rangle\subset\langle\alpha v_{1}+\beta v_{3},\alpha v_{2}+\beta v_{4}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{3},\alpha v_{2}+\beta v_{4}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{4}\mid[\alpha:\beta]\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}).

Thus,
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}
has a single connected component, which is a torsion bundle
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}=\mathcal{H}_{2}\rightarrow\mathfrak{F}_{mpx}.
Being a resolution of the cone
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}/{2}}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}/(\mathbb{Z}/2),
it is a
A_{1}
singularity resolution, hence
\mathcal{H}_{2}\cong T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1}.
So, the cohomology of its hypersurface

B_{1/2}=\mathcal{S}_{1/2}\cap\mathcal{H}_{2}
is
isomorphic to the cohomology of the unit bundle
S(T^{*}\mathbb{C}P^{1})\cong\mathbb{R}P^{3},
by
6.12
. As we work over the field
\mathbb{K}
of characteristic zero,

H^{*}(B_{1/2})\cong H^{*}(\mathbb{R}P^{3})=\mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{3}.

The cohomology of the space
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
is isomorphic to the cohomology of its core, the Springer fibre
\mathcal{B}^{22}.
Vector space generators of
H^{*}(\mathcal{B}^{\lambda})
are labelled by row-standard tableaux of shape
\lambda,
and the degrees of the generators can be easily computed by some tableaux combinatorics

[
Fre09

. Using the prescribed method therein, one obtains

H^{*}(\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{22})\cong H^{*}(\mathcal{B}^{22})\cong\mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{2}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{4}^{2}.

Thus, from
6.14
and
6.15
one obtains the cohomology of the slice.
We now find the degree shifts. As the weight decomposition of
\mathfrak{F}_{mpx}
has
H_{0}\oplus H_{2}
as a summand, by
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
-duality it also has
H_{1-2}=H_{-1}
and
H_{1-0}=H_{1}
(recall we square-rooted the action so
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
has weight 1). Thus we have

T_{\mathfrak{F}_{mpx}}\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{22}=H_{-1}\oplus H_{0}\oplus H_{1}\oplus H_{2},
and then by (
64
) we find

\mu(B_{1/2})=0.

By
6.11
(
1
),
the unit is killed by the top class of the time-2 column.
We obtain
Figure

12

at the mid-dimensional cohomology of the time-
2
slice.

since the rest is known by Poincaré duality.

(80)

Example 7.16
.

Slodowy variety
\mathcal{S}_{32}.
For
\lambda=(3,2)
,

as before one computes the
slice
S_{32}:

\small S_{32}=\left\{\begin{bmatrix}2a&1&0&0&0\\
f&2a&1&j&0\\
l&f&2a&p&2j\\
2y&0&0&-3a&1\\
w&y&0&x&-3a\end{bmatrix}\middle|\ a,f,j,l,p,x,y,w\in\mathbb{C}\right\}.

and
h_{32}=\text{diag}(2,0,-2,1,-1).

t^{h_{32}}=\text{diag}(t^{2},t^{0},t^{-2},t^{1},t^{-1}),

76

\small t\cdot\begin{bmatrix}2a&1&0&0&0\\

l&f&2a&p&2j\\
2y&0&0&-3a&1\\
w&y&0&x&-3a\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}2t^{2}a&1&0&0&0\\
t^{4}f&2t^{2}a&1&t^{3}j&0\\
t^{6}l&t^{4}f&2t^{2}a&t^{5}p&2t^{3}j\\
2t^{3}y&0&0&-3t^{2}a&1\\
t^{5}w&t^{3}y&0&t^{4}x&-3t^{2}a\end{bmatrix}.

Thus, we see that
S_{32}
has
\mathbb{Z}/k
-torsion points for all
k=2,3,4,5,6.
Restricting to

\mathcal{S}_{32}=\{A\in S_{32}\mid A^{5}=0\},

some matrix calculations
by computer show that only
\mathbb{Z}/3
and
\mathbb{Z}/5
-torsion points remain on it. Explicitly (where in lower indices we label the coordinates used on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
\mathbb{C}
):

(82)

\small\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}/{3}}=\left\{A_{jy}:=\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0&0&0\\
0&0&1&j&0\\
-5jy&0&0&0&2j\\
2y&0&0&0&1\\
0&y&0&0&0\end{bmatrix}\middle|\ j,y\in\mathbb{C}\right\}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}_{j,y},

(83)

\small\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}/{5}}=\left\{A_{pw}:=\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0&0&0\\

\mathbb{Z}/5
torsion points:

Apart from the zero matrix,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}/{5}}
lies in the regular orbit
\mathcal{O}_{5}.
Therefore it is bijectively lifted to

\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{32}
under the Springer resolution, to pairs

(A_{p0},F_{p})
and
(A_{0w},F_{w})
where

F_{p}:=(0\subset\langle v_{1}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{5})

F_{w}:=(0\subset\langle v_{4}\rangle\subset\langle v_{4},v_{5}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{4},v_{5}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{4},v_{5}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{5})

as one can easily compute from the Springer fibre conditions
A_{0w}F_{w}\subset F_{w},\ A_{p0}F_{p}\subset F_{p}.

Here,
(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4})
is the basis in which the matrices are given.
In the limit
t\rightarrow 0
of the Kazhdan action,

\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}t\cdot(A_{p0},F_{p})=(e_{32},F_{p}),\ \ \ \lim_{t\rightarrow 0}t\cdot(A_{0w},F_{w})=(e_{32},F_{w})

hence, these two
\mathbb{Z}/5
-torsion lines converge to two points
F_{p}
and
F_{w}
in the Springer fibre
\mathcal{B}^{32}.
They yield
two Morse–Bott submanifolds

B_{1/5}=B_{1/5,1}\sqcup B_{1/5,2}\cong S^{1}\sqcup S^{1},

but we cannot compute the shifts
\mu(B_{1/5,i})
yet, as we lack information on the weight decomposition at

\mathscr{F}_{p}
and
\mathscr{F}_{w}
in the core
\mathcal{B}^{32}.
We will be able to do it after considering the
\mathbb{Z}/3
-torsion points.

\mathbb{Z}/3
torsion points:

By checking for which
j,y\in\mathbb{C}
we have
A_{jy}^{4}=0,
we find that in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Z}/{3}}
the two lines
j=0
and
y=0
lie on the subregular orbit
\mathcal{O}_{41},
whereas the rest lie in
\mathcal{O}_{5}.

Thus, for
j,y\neq 0
the fibre above
A_{jy}
is
(A_{jy},F_{jy})
where, after some computation,

F_{jy}=(0\subset\langle jv_{3}-v_{4}\rangle\subset\langle jv_{3}-v_{4},v_{1}+yv_{5}\rangle\subset\langle jv_{3}-v_{4},v_{1}+yv_{5},v_{2}\rangle\subset\langle v_{2},v_{3},v_{4},v_{1}+yv_{5}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{5}).

This flag also makes sense for
j=0
or
y=0,
and due to continuity, we get a slice

S_{jy}=\{(A_{jy},F_{jy})\mid j,y\in\mathbb{C}\}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}.

By (
77
), the Kazhdan action acts on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
by

t\cdot(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4},v_{5})=(t^{-2}v_{1},v_{2},t^{2}v_{3},t^{-1}v_{4},tv_{5}),

thus one immediately gets

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}t\cdot(A_{jy},F_{jy})=(e_{32},F_{big}),
where

F_{big}:=(0\subset\langle v_{4}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{4}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{4}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{5}).

Thus the whole slice
S_{jy}
converges to the flag
F_{big},
yielding a trivial rank 2 torsion bundle
\mathcal{H}_{3}\rightarrow F_{big}.
The corresponding Morse–Bott submanifold
B_{1/3,big}
is cohomologically isomorphic to the sphere bundle

(84)

H^{*}(B_{1/3,big})\cong H^{*}(S(\mathcal{H}_{3}))\cong H^{*}(S^{3})=\mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{3},

and knowing the weight decomposition
H_{3}\oplus H_{3}\oplus H_{-1}\oplus H_{-1}
of
F_{big}
we also compute
\mu(B_{1/3,big})=0.

Now let us consider the
\mathbb{Z}/3
-torsion points in the fibre over
A_{j0},
where
j\neq 0.
The Springer fibre over
A_{j0}
is a Dynkin
A_{4}
-tree of spheres
{\mathbb{P}}_{1}^{1}\cup{\mathbb{P}}_{2}^{1}\cup{\mathbb{P}}_{3}^{1}\cup{\mathbb{P}}_{4}^{1}
that intersect transversally. Standard computations from Springer theory yield their explicit flag description:
66
66

66

In short: One modifies the base
v_{i}
such that it becomes a Jordan base for
A_{j0}
and then one uses the description of the Springer fibre of the standard matrix
e_{41}.

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{1}^{1}[\alpha:\beta]=

\displaystyle(0\subset\langle\alpha v_{1}+\beta(\tfrac{1}{j}v_{4}-v_{3})\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},\tfrac{1}{j}v_{4}-v_{3}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},\tfrac{1}{j}v_{4}-v_{3}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{5})

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{2}^{1}[\alpha:\beta]=

\displaystyle(0\subset\langle v_{1}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},\alpha v_{2}+\beta(\tfrac{1}{j}v_{4}-v_{3})\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},\tfrac{1}{j}v_{4}-v_{3}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{5})

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{3}^{1}[\alpha:\beta]=

\displaystyle(0\subset\langle v_{1}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},\alpha v_{3}+\beta\tfrac{1}{j}v_{4}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{5})

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{4}^{1}[\alpha:\beta]=

\displaystyle(0\subset\langle v_{1}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},2v_{3}+\tfrac{1}{j}v_{4}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},2v_{3}+\tfrac{1}{j}v_{4},\alpha v_{3}+\beta\tfrac{1}{j}v_{5}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{5})

Recall that the Kazhdan action is
t\cdot(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4},v_{5})=(t^{-2}v_{1},v_{2},t^{2}v_{3},t^{-1}v_{4},tv_{5}),
which for the primitive third root of unity
\varepsilon
gives

\varepsilon\cdot(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4},v_{5})=(\varepsilon v_{1},v_{2},\varepsilon^{-1}v_{3},\varepsilon^{-1}v_{4},\varepsilon v_{5}).

Notice that the
\mathbb{Z}/3
-torsion points in the fibre
are exactly the flags fixed by the
\varepsilon
action. Thus, we get that the
\mathbb{Z}/3
-torsion points in the Springer fibre above
A_{j0}
are

{\mathbb{P}}_{1}^{1}[0:1],\ \ {\mathbb{P}}_{1}^{1}[1:0],\ \ {\mathbb{P}}_{3}^{1}[\alpha:\beta],\ \ {\mathbb{P}}_{4}^{1}[0:1],

so three points and a sphere.
Note the point

{\mathbb{P}}_{1}^{1}[0:1]=F_{j0}

is already contained in the slice
S_{jy}.
One computes directly that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}t\cdot(A_{j0},{\mathbb{P}}_{1}^{1}[1:0])=(e_{32},F_{j}^{3}),
and
\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}t\cdot(A_{j0},{\mathbb{P}}_{4}^{1}[0:1])=(e_{32},F_{j}^{1}),
where

F_{j}^{3}:=(0\subset\langle v_{1}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{4}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{4}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{5})

F_{j}^{1}:=(0\subset\langle v_{1}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{4}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{4},v_{5}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{5}).

Thus, these two yield
\mathbb{Z}/3
-torsion line bundles over the points
F_{j}^{3}
and
F_{j}^{1}.
Hence, their Morse–Bott submanifolds are circles,

B_{1/3,j^{3}}\cong B_{1/3,j^{1}}\cong S^{1}.

The convergence (under Kazhdan action and
t\rightarrow 0
) of the spheres

(85)

\small{\mathbb{P}}_{3}^{1}[\alpha:\beta]_{j}=(0\subset\langle v_{1}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},\alpha v_{3}+\beta\tfrac{1}{j}v_{4}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{5}\mid[\alpha:\beta]\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1})

is a bit more involved. Namely, (
85
) converges to

\displaystyle F_{p}=

\displaystyle(0\subset\langle v_{1}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{5}),\text{when }\beta=0

\displaystyle F_{j}^{\prime}:=

\displaystyle(0\subset\langle v_{1}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{4}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{5}),\text{otherwise.}

Notice also that there is a
\mathbb{Z}/3
-torsion sphere in the core between these two points:

C_{jp}=\{(0\subset\langle v_{1}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},\alpha v_{3}+\beta v_{4}\rangle\subset\langle v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{5})\mid[\alpha:\beta]\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\}.

Thus, altogether with
{\mathbb{P}}_{3}^{1}[\alpha:\beta]
we get a
\mathbb{Z}/3
-torsion line bundle over the (non-fixed) sphere
C_{jp}:

\{(A_{j0},F)\mid j\in\mathbb{C}\}\mapsto F\in C_{jp}.

This bundle is trivial, and by Lemma
6.12
the corresponding Morse–Bott submanifold
B_{1/3,jp}
is cohomologically isomorphic to its
sphere bundle,

(86)

H^{*}(B_{1/3,jp})\cong H^{*}(S^{2}\times S^{1})\cong\mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{1}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{3}.

The weight-decomposition of
F_{j}^{\prime}
is
H_{3}\oplus H_{3}\oplus H_{-1}\oplus H_{-1},
thus by formula (
64
) one computes the grading
\mu(B_{1/3,jp})=0.

Now we compute the gradings of the Morse–Bott submanifolds
B_{1/3,j^{3}}
and
B_{1/3,j^{1}}.

We claim that the weight-decompositions of their corresponding fixed loci
F_{j}^{3}
and
F_{j}^{1}
are both

(87)

H_{3}\oplus H_{-1}\oplus H_{1}\oplus H_{1},

due to the following
accumulative
argument. Assuming the contrary and that there is a weight-space
H_{2},
it then yields a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-flowline in the core (as there are no
\mathbb{Z}/2
-torsion points outside of the core)
which converges to another fixed set to weight-space
H_{-2}

(
2.7
).
Thus, by
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
-duality, that fixed set has an
H_{4}
weight-space, which again yields a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-orbit etc. As the number of components of the fixed locus is finite, this process must stop and we get a contradiction. The same would happen if we had assumed that there was a weight-space
H_{k\geq 4}
in
F_{j}^{3}
and
F_{j}^{1}.
Otherwise, assume that
H_{3}
is 2-dimensional. That would mean that there are more outer
67
67

67

I.e., points that are outside of the core.

\mathbb{Z}/3
-torsion points converging to
F_{j}^{3}
and
F_{j}^{1},
so again, a contradiction. Thus the weight-decompositions (
87
) hold, and by (
64
) we get

\mu(B_{1/3,j^{3}})=\mu(B_{1/3,j^{1}})=0.

Completely analogously, considering

.

Similarly to the last example, by counting the row-standard Young tableaux of shape

we get the ordinary cohomology

Example 7.17
.

Slodowy variety
\mathcal{S}_{32},

.

Figure

14
shows the

-equivariant spectral sequence for

. The
0
-th column
H^{*}(Y;\mathbb{K})\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\mathbb{F}
consists of copies of the
\mathbb{K}[u]
-module
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{K}(\!(u)\!)/u\mathbb{K}[\![u]\!]\cong H_{-*}(\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{P}}^{\infty})
mentioned in
Section

1.8
: the larger dots correspond to the
u^{0}
-part, and the smaller dots correspond to the
u^{-j}
-copies of that generator for
j\geq 1
.
This example illustrates
1.31
(and one can see explicitly how
1.33
arises in practice).

The edge-differentials preserve the
u
-action, but unfortunately the
u
-action has been mostly forgotten once we pass to the
E_{1}
-page.
The first three dots in degree
1
for the
B_{1/3}
-column have a non-trivial vertical
u
-action (see
1.8
), so they must hit
u^{<0}
-classes in the
0
-th column. However, the other four dots in degree
1
for the
B_{1/3}
-column may have non-trivial
u
-action hitting the
B_{1/5}
-column, so in principle we only know that at least two of the first four dots in the
0
-th column in degree 2 (which are
u^{0}
-terms) get killed. This ambiguity is precisely the unknown value

in (

).

Remark 7.18
.

Equation

9
and
5.16
can also be used to deduce information about the cohomology of fixed components
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
from the spectral sequence. The
0
-th column in
Figure

13
has a total rank of
10
, and this rank must persist as we add new columns provided we take into account all the green arrows. So if we consider the block given by all columns
p
whose periods
T_{p}
are below a certain slope, we must get total rank
10
. By Corollary
9
the degrees in which these ranks live will shift in complicated ways, as we increase the slope. Observing how the gradings shift can reveal information about the cohomology of individual fixed components
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
.
In Figure
13
an extreme example occurs: if we consider all columns up to
B_{2/5}
, we see that all of the rank
10
is supported in degree
0
. By
Equation

9
, that means each
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
has cohomology supported in just one degree, so each
\mathfrak{F}_{\alpha}
is a point. So
\mathfrak{F}={\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{32}}^{\mathbb{C}^{*}}
consists of 10 points.
Alternatively, this follows from
7.14
.

.

\mathcal{S}_{211},\mathcal{S}_{311},\mathcal{S}_{33},\mathcal{S}_{42}.

7.4.
Moduli of Higgs bundles

The last class of examples that we consider are moduli spaces of Higgs bundles.
They come
with a hyperkähler structure
(\mathcal{M},I,J,K,g)

and a canonical
I
-holomorphic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action
\varphi

that acts with weight-1 on the holomorphic symplectic form

(89)

t\cdot\omega_{\mathbb{C}}=t\omega_{\mathbb{C}},\ \omega_{\mathbb{C}}:=\omega_{I}+i\omega_{J}

and the
S^{1}
-part preserves the Kähler form
\omega_{I}.

There is a proper holomorphic map, the Hitchin fibration,

\Psi:\mathcal{M}\to B\cong\mathbb{C}^{\frac{1}{2}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{M}},

equivariant w.r.t. a certain linear action on the base.

(\mathcal{M},\omega_{I})
is a symplectic
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-manifold globally defined over the convex base
B
via
\Psi.

Given
G\in\{GL(n,\mathbb{C}),\ SL(n,\mathbb{C})\},

d\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
coprime to
n,

consider
\mathcal{M}_{G}(d,g),
the moduli of

G
-Higgs bundles of degree
d
over a Riemann surface of genus
g\geq 2.

Proposition 7.20
.

[
RŽ23
]

Q_{\varphi}=0
for
\mathcal{M}_{G}(d,g),
so
\mathscr{F}_{1}^{\varphi}=H^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{G}(d,g)).

Example 7.21
.

G=GL(1,\mathbb{C}),

\mathcal{M}_{G}(d,g)\cong T^{*}T^{2g}.

For
G=GL(1,\mathbb{C}),

the moduli space is the cotangent bundle of the torus
T^{2g}
(for
SL(1,\mathbb{C})
it is a point), with the standard weight-1
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action on fibres.
The Maslov index is
\mu=\frac{1}{2}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{M}=g,

and the columns in the spectral sequence (after the 0-column) are cohomologies of the sphere bundle

H^{*}(B_{k})=H^{*}(S^{*}T^{2g})
shifted by

2g,4g,6g,\dots.

Due to the Gysin sequence and
\chi(T^{2g})=0,
we get

H^{*}(B_{k})\cong H^{*}(T^{2g})\otimes H^{*}(S^{2g-1}).

By
7.20
, the filtration is trivial,

\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{0}=0\subset H^{*}(\mathcal{M}_{GL(1,\mathbb{C})}(d,g))=\mathscr{F}^{\varphi}_{1}.

Interestingly, one
cannot
conclude this just by looking at the spectral sequence itself
(see
Figure

18
, left).
The unit
1\in H^{0}(\mathcal{M})
could in principle be killed by
H^{top}(B_{2}),
but the triviality of the intersection form
(used in the proof of
7.20
)
prevents this.
Thus the unit gets killed by a
mid-degree class from the time-1 column (lower yellow box in
Figure

18
).
This is very different from CSRs,
whose intersection form is non-degenerate, and mid-degrees in the time-1 column vanish, so differentials from
H^{top}(B_{2})
can hit the unit

1\in H^{0}(\mathcal{M})
(e.g.
Figure

3
).

Figure 18.
Spectral sequence for
GL(1,\mathbb{C})
and
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
Higgs moduli over
g=2

Example 7.22
.

G=SL(2,\mathbb{C}),

g=2.

The topology of Higgs moduli for
G=SL(2,\mathbb{C})
are
well-understood. The
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-fixed locus is completely described
[
Hit87
, Prop.7.1]
. For
g=2

we have two fixed components,

\mathfrak{F}_{0}=\mathfrak{F}_{\min}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{1}
,

H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{0})\cong\mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{3}^{4}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{4}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{6},\ \ \ \ H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{1})\cong\mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{1}^{34}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{2},

with Morse–Bott indices
\mu_{0}=0
,
\mu_{1}=4.
The attraction graph is quite simple: by

\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
-duality, there are only
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
weight spaces at
\mathfrak{F}_{0},
so
there is a weight-1 flow from
\mathfrak{F}_{0}
to
\mathfrak{F}_{1},
creating a
H_{-1}
weight space
at
\mathfrak{F}_{1}.

Due to the Morse–Bott
index,
H_{-1}
is 2-dimensional.
By
\omega_{\mathbb{C}}
-duality, there is 2-dimensional
H_{2},

creating a torsion bundle
\mathcal{H}_{2}\rightarrow\mathfrak{F}_{1}
of rank 2. Thus, the associated Morse–Bott manifold has

H^{*}(B_{1/2})\cong H^{*}(S\mathcal{H}_{2})\cong H^{*}(S^{3})\otimes H^{*}(\mathfrak{F}_{1})\cong\mathbb{K}_{0}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{1}^{34}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{3}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{4}^{34}\oplus\mathbb{K}_{5}.

The time-1 column is computed via
6.14
and the fact that the intersection form is trivial, by Hausel
[
Hau98
]
.
The spectral sequence (see
Figure

18
, right) yields the filtration
